.

Example 3.2.15
.

If
X\sim Unif([-2,2]\cap\mathbb{Z})
and
Y=X^{2}
, then derived distribution
p_{Y}(y)
can be computed as follows:

1.

As
X\sim Unif([-2,2]\cap\mathbb{Z})
and
Y=X^{2}
, so
\Omega_{X}=\{-2,-1,0,1,2\}
transformed to
\Omega_{Y}=\{0,1,4\}
.

2.

Now, we have

\displaystyle p_{Y}(0)

\displaystyle=P(Y=0)=P(X^{2}=0)=P(X=0)=1/5,

\displaystyle p_{Y}(1)

\displaystyle=P(Y=1)=P(X^{2}=1)=P(X\in\{-1,1\})=2/5,

\displaystyle p_{Y}(4)

\displaystyle=P(Y=4)=P(X^{2}=4)=P(X\in\{-2,2\})=2/5.

Example 3.2.16
.

If
X\sim Pois(\lambda))
and
Y=g(X)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\ \mbox{if}\ x\mbox{is even}\\
-1&\ \mbox{if}\ x\mbox{is odd}\end{array}\right.
, then derived distribution
p_{Y}(y)
can be computed as follows:

1.

As
X\sim Pois(\lambda))
and
Y=g(X)
, so
\Omega_{X}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
transformed to
\Omega_{Y}=\{-1,1\}
.

2.

Now, we have

\displaystyle p_{Y}(1)

\displaystyle=P(Y=1)=P(X\ \mbox{is even})=\sum_{\{x\ \mbox{is even}\}}\frac{\lambda^{x}}{x!}e^{-\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+e^{2\lambda}\right),

\displaystyle p_{Y}(-1)

\displaystyle=P(Y=-1)=P(X\ \mbox{is odd})=\sum_{\{x\ \mbox{is odd}\}}\frac{\lambda^{x}}{x!}e^{-\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-2\lambda}\right).

3.2.3
Jointly discrete distribution

In science and in real life, we are often interested in two (or more) random variables at the same time in a random experiment. In such situations the random variables have a joint distribution that allows us to compute probabilities of events involving both variables and understand the relationship between the variables. This is simplest when the variables are independent. When they are not, we use
covariance and correlation as measures of the nature of the dependence between them.

Definition 3.2.17

(Joint probability mass function)
.

Suppose X and Y are two discrete random variables and that X takes values
\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{m}\}
and Y takes values
\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n}\}
. Then, the ordered pair
(X,Y)
take values in the product
\{(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{1},y_{2}),\ldots(x_{m},y_{n})\}
. So, the joint probability mass function (joint pmf) of X and Y can be defined as the function
p_{(X,Y)}(x_{i},y_{j})=P(X=x_{i},Y=y_{j})
as the joint probability of the joint outcome
(X=x_{i},Y=y_{j})
and satisfies the following:

1.

p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)\in[0,1]
for any
(x,y)\in\Omega_{X}\times\Omega_{Y}
,

2.

\sum_{\{(x,y)\in\Omega_{X}\times\Omega_{Y}\}}p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)=1
,

3.

P((X,Y)\in B)=\sum_{\{(x,y)\in B\}}p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)
.

More, explicitly, we can visualize the joint probability mass function for each joint point
\{(x_{i},y_{j})|1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n\}
in the table (
3.1
):

Table 3.1
:
Joint probability table for joint probability mass function
p_{(X,Y)}
of
X\in\Omega_{X}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
and
Y\in\Omega_{Y}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{m}\}
.

X\backslash Y

y_{1}

y_{2}

\ldots

y_{j}

\ldots

y_{n}

x_{1}

P_{X,Y}(x_{1},y_{1})

P_{X,Y}(x_{1},y_{2})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{1},y_{j})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{1},y_{n})

x_{2}

P_{X,Y}(x_{2},y_{1})

P_{X,Y}(x_{2},y_{2})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{2},y_{j})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{2},y_{n})

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\ldots

\vdots

\ldots

\vdots

x_{i}

P_{X,Y}(x_{i},y_{1})

P_{X,Y}(x_{i},y_{2})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{i},y_{j})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{i},y_{n})

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

\ldots

\vdots

\ldots

\vdots

x_{m}

P_{X,Y}(x_{m},y_{1})

P_{X,Y}(x_{m},y_{2})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{m},y_{j})

\ldots

P_{X,Y}(x_{m},y_{n})

Definition 3.2.20

(Marginal PMFs)
.

Marginalization of joint probability mass function: when X and Y are jointly-distributed random variables, we may want to consider only one of them, say X. In that case we need to find the pmf of X without Y. This is called a marginal pmf of X. For each observation/event
X=x
, the
p_{X}
is computed by summing probability of points on
x--
strip i.e.

\displaystyle p_{X}(x)=\sum_{y\in\Omega_{Y}}p_{X,Y}(x,y).\ \mbox{Similarly}\ p_{Y}(y)=\sum_{x\in\Omega_{X}}p_{X,Y}(x,y).

Figure 3.11
:
Visualizing marginal PMFs
p_{X}(x)
and
p_{Y}(y)
respectively.

(X,T)

As motivated by this example, marginal pmf’s are obtained from the joint pmf by summing:

\displaystyle p_{X}(x)=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)\ p_{Y}(y)=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}p_{(X,Y)}(x,y).

The term marginal refers to the fact that the values are written in the margins of the table.

Definition 3.2.22

(Conditional probability mass function of a DRV conditioned on an event)
.

In a probabilistic model if a certain event A has already occurred, then we define conditional PMF of the possible observations of a random variable X conditioned on the event A as follows:

\displaystyle p_{X|A}(x|A)=P(X=x,A|A)=\frac{P(X=x,A}{P(A)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{p_{X}(x)}{P(A)}&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in A,\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ x\notin A.\end{array}\right.

The conditional PMF is a legitimate discrete distribution and satisfies the following:

1.

p_{X|A}(x|A)\geq 0
as
p_{X}(x)\geq 0
,

2.

We have the following normalization property:

\displaystyle\sum_{\{x\in A\}}p_{X|A}(x|A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P(A)}\sum_{\{x\in A\}}p_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{P(A)}\sum_{\{x\in A\}}P(X=x,A)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{P(A)}P(A)=1.

Since for any discrete random variable
X
, we have

\displaystyle a.\ A_{i}\cap A_{j}=X^{-1}(x_{i})\cap X^{-1}(x_{j})=\{\omega|X(\omega)=x_{i}\}\cap\{\omega|X(\omega)=x_{j}\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\varphi\ \mbox{for}\ i\neq j,

\displaystyle b.\ \cup_{\{x_{k}\in\Omega_{X}\}}A_{k}=\cup_{\{x_{k}\in\Omega_{X}\}}X^{-1}(x_{k})=\cup_{\{x_{k}\in\Omega_{X}\}}\{\omega|X(\omega)=x_{k}\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\Omega,

\displaystyle c.\ A_{k}=X^{-1}(x_{k})=\{\omega|X(\omega)=x_{k}\}\neq\varphi\ \mbox{for any}\ x_{k}\in\Omega_{X}.

Figure 3.12
:
Conditional probability mass function of
X
conditioned on an event
A
.

Example 3.2.23
.

Consider an experiment of rolling a dice, A be the event that roll is an even number. Let
X
be the roll of a die, then conditional PMF of X conditioned A is given by

\displaystyle p_{X|A}(x|A)

\displaystyle=P(X=x|A)=\frac{P(X=x,A}{P(A)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{p_{X}(x)}{P(A)}&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in A\\
0&|\mbox{if}\ x\notin A\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1/3&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in A=\{2,4,6\}\\
0&|\mbox{if}\ x\notin A.\end{array}\right.

3.2.4
Conditional PMF

Definition 3.2.24

(Conditional probability mass function)
.

When an experiment is a compound one, in which the second part of the experiment (described by a discrete random variable
Y
) depends upon the outcome of the first part (described another discrete random variable
X
), conditional probability mass function, conditioned on the event
Y=y
, is coming into picture as follows:

\displaystyle p_{X|Y}(x|y)

\displaystyle=P(X=x,Y=y|Y=y)=\frac{P(X=x,Y=y}{P(Y=y)}=\frac{p_{(X,Y)(x,y)}}{p_{Y}(y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{X,Y}(x,y)}{\sum_{x}p_{X,Y}(x,y)}

\displaystyle\ \mbox{for a fixed observation}\ Y=y.

Definition 3.2.25

(Conditional PMFs)
.

Conditioning of joint probability mass function: when X and Y are jointly-distributed random variables, we may want to consider only one of them conditioned on one observation the other, say
X|Y=y
. In that case we need to find the pmf of X conditioned on
Y=y
. This is called a conditional pmf of X given
Y=y
. For each observation/event
X=x
, the
p_{X|Y}
is computed by a normalized probability of a point on
x\to
strip, where the normalization is provided by the value of PMF
p_{Y}
at
Y=y
i.e.
p_{Y}(y)=P(Y=y)=\sum_{x}p_{X,Y}(x,y)
as a total probability of the event
Y=y
on
y-
axis. So, we can have a concrete definition of the conditional PMF as follows:

\displaystyle p_{X|Y}(x|y)

\displaystyle=P(X=x|Y=y)=\frac{P(X=x,Y=y}{P(Y=y)}=\frac{p_{(X,Y)(x,y)}}{p_{Y}(y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{X,Y}(x,y)}{\sum_{x}p_{X,Y}(x,y)}

\displaystyle\ \mbox{for a fixed observation}\ Y=y.

Similarly,

\displaystyle p_{Y|X}(y|x)

\displaystyle=P(Y=y|X=x)=\frac{P(X=x,Y=y}{P(X=x)}=\frac{p_{(X,Y)(x,y)}}{p_{X}(x)}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{X,Y}(x,y)}{\sum_{y}p_{X,Y}(x,y)}

\displaystyle\ \mbox{for a fixed observation}\ X=x.

Figure 3.13
:
Visualizing conditional PMFs
p_{Y|X}(y|x)
and
p_{X|Y}(x|y)
respectively.

So, by using the definition of conditional probability for events we have

\displaystyle p_{(X,Y)}((x,y)

\displaystyle=P(X=x,Y=y)=P(Y=y)P(X=x|Y=y)

\displaystyle=p_{Y}(y)p_{X|Y}(x|y).

It provides a computational framework to determine joint PMF of the joint discrete random variable
(X,Y)
.

Example 3.2.26
.

Two dice are tossed with all outcomes assumed to be equally likely. The number of dots observed on each die are added together. Then compute the conditional PMF of the sum if it is known that the sum is even.

Let Y be the sum and define
X=1
if the sum is even and
X=0
if the sum is odd. Thus, we wish to determine
p_{Y|X}(y|0)
and
p_{Y|X}(y|1)
for all
y\in\Omega_{Y}=\{2,3,\ldots,12\}
. Then

\displaystyle p_{Y|X}(y|1)=\frac{p_{(Y,X)}(y,1)}{p_{X}(1)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}2\cdot\frac{1}{36}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=2,12\\
2\cdot\frac{3}{36}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=4,10\\
2\cdot\frac{5}{36}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=6,8\end{array}\right.=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{1}{18}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=2,12\\
\frac{3}{18}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=4,10\\
\frac{5}{18}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=6,8.\end{array}\right.

And

\displaystyle p_{Y|X}(y|0)=\frac{p_{(Y,X)}(y,0)}{p_{X}(0)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}2\cdot\frac{2}{36}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=3,11\\
2\cdot\frac{4}{36}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=5,9\\
2\cdot\frac{6}{36}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=7\end{array}\right.=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{2}{18}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=3,11\\
\frac{4}{18}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=5,9\\
\frac{6}{18}&\ \mbox{if}\ y=7.\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.14
:
Conditional PMFs
p_{Y|X}(y|1)
and
p_{Y|X}(y|0)
.

Theorem 3.2.27

(Bayes rule for probability mass function)
.

Definition of conditional probability mass function leads to computation of posterior distribution as follows:

\displaystyle p_{X|Y}(x|y)=\frac{p_{X,Y}(x,y)}{p_{Y}(y)}=\frac{p_{X}(x)p_{Y|X}(y|x)}{\sum_{x}p_{X}(x)p_{Y|X}(y|x)}.

where
p_{X}(x)\to
prior distribution of
X
,
p_{Y|X}(y|x)=f(x)\to
is the data generating process as a likelihood of
Y=y
conditioned on
X=x
and
p_{Y}(y)=\sum_{x}p_{X}(x)p_{Y|X}(y|x)\to
probability of observing
Y=y
or evidence.

Figure 3.15
:
Bayes theorem for posterior PMF of Y conditioned on
X=x
and of X conditioned on
Y=y
respectively.

Example 3.2.28

(Computation of joint PMF)
.

(
3.2.28
) Professor May often has her facts wrong, and answers each of her students’ questions incorrectly with probability
1/4
, independently of other questions. In each lecture May is asked 0, 1, or 2 questions with equal probability
1/3
. Find the desired joint probability mass function.

Solution: Let X and Y be the number of questions May is asked and the number of questions she answers wrong in a given lecture, respectively. Now need to compute the joint PMF of X and Y as follows:

\displaystyle p_{(X,Y)}((x,y)=p_{X}(x)p_{Y|X}(y|x).

We have
(a.)\ Y|X=0\in\{0\}
with PMF
p_{Y|X}(y|0)=1,\ (b.)\ Y|X=1\in\{0,1\}
with PMF
p_{Y|X}(y|0)=\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{y}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{1-y}
, and
(c.)\ Y|X=2\in\{0,1,2\}
with PMF
p_{Y|X}(y|0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2\\
y\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{y}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{2-y}
.
Then

\displaystyle p_{(X,Y)}(0,0)

\displaystyle=p_{X}(0)p_{Y|X}(0|0)=1/3\cdot 1=1/3=16/48,

\displaystyle p_{(X,Y)}(0,1)

\displaystyle=p_{X}(0)p_{Y|X}(1|0)=1/3\times 0=0,

\displaystyle=\vdots

\displaystyle p_{(X,Y)}(2,2)

\displaystyle=p_{X}(2)p_{Y|X}(2|2)=1/3\times 1/16=1/48.

Figure 3.16
:
Computation of joint PMF of dependent discrete random variables
X
and
Y
, where
p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)=p_{X}(x)p_{Y|X}(y|x)
.

Example 3.2.29

(Computation of posterior PMF)
.

Question: suppose
X
is the number of
1
s that appear in a binary string of length
L
, each bit in the string is equal to zero or one with probability
1/2
, and the bits are independent. Suppose that the length of the string is also random and uniformly distributed between 1 and 10. We learn that the binary string contains 4 ones. By using the given information, compute the updated PMF for the length of the string L.

Answer: Given
L=\ell
, we know that X has the binomial distribution when
x\leq\ell
(the probability is zero otherwise):

\displaystyle p_{X|L}(x|\ell)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\ell\\
x\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{x}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\ell-x}&\ \mbox{if}\ x\leq\ell,\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

And

\displaystyle p_{L}(\ell)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{10}&\ \mbox{if}\ \ell=1,2,\ldots,10,\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Given
L=\ell
, we have the binary string contains 4 ones (i.e.
X=4
), so likelihood of observing
X=4
is given by

\displaystyle p_{X|L}(4|\ell)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\ell\\
4\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{4}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\ell-4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\ell\\
4\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\ell}=f(\ell).

And by suing the prior probability mass function of L, we get

\displaystyle p_{X}(4)=\sum_{L}p_{(X,L)}(x,\ell)=\sum_{L}p_{L}(\ell)p_{X|L}(4|\ell)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{10}\frac{1}{10}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\ell\\
4\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\ell}

Then, the updated probability mass function as the posterior PMF of L is given by

\displaystyle p_{L|X}(\ell|4)=\frac{p_{L}(\ell)p_{X|L}(4|\ell)}{p_{X}(4)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\frac{1}{10}\times\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\ell\\
4\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\ell}}{p_{X}(4)}&\ \mbox{if}\ \ell=1,2,\ldots,10,\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Definition 3.2.30

(In-dependency or dependency of joint discrete random variables)
.

Jointly-distributed discrete random variables X and Y are independent if observation of one random variable before the other is going to affect their probability mass functions or their joint PMF is the product of the marginal PMF’s:

\displaystyle p_{X|Y}(x|y)=p_{X}(x)\ \mbox{or}\ p_{X,Y}(x,y)=p_{X}(x)p_{Y}(y)\ \mbox{or}F_{X,Y}(x,y)=F_{X}(x)F_{y}(y),

If
E(XY)\neq E(X)E(Y)
or
Cov(X,Y)\neq 0
, then the jointly distributed random variables
X
and
Y
are dependent.

Example 3.2.31
.

Roll two dice. Let X be the value on the first die and let Y be the value on the second die. Independent discrete variables means the probability in a cell must be the product of the marginal probabilities of its row and column. In the probability table below this is true: every marginal probability is
1/6
and every cell contains
1/36
, i.e. the product of the marginals and
X,Y\sim Unif(\{1,2,3,4,5,6\})
. Therefore X and Y are independent.

Example 3.2.33
.

Consider a tossing of a biased coin (with probability of success is 1/4) in the following 2 steps: in the first step we flip the coin until we get a heads and let X denote the trial on which the first heads occurs. In the second step we flip the coin X more times and let Y be the number of heads in the second step. Compute joint PMF of X and Y.

Here, the random variable
X
is geometrically distributed with parameter
p=1/4
, so

\displaystyle p_{X}(x)=p(1-p)^{x-1}=\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{x-1}\frac{1}{4}\ \mbox{for}\ x=1,2,\ldots.

And, the conditional PMF of
Y
conditioned on
X=x
is given by

\displaystyle p_{Y|X}(y|x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x\\
y\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{y}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{x-y}\ \mbox{for}\ y=0,1,2,\ldots,x.

And hence, the joint PMF can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle p_{X,Y}(x,y)=p_{X}(x)p_{Y|X}(y|x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x\\
y\end{array}\right)\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^{y}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{x-y}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^{x-1}\frac{1}{4}

3.2.5
Expectation and variance of discrete random variable

Definition 3.2.34

(Expectation of a discrete random variable)
.

Consider a discrete random variable, if we observe
n
random values of X, then the mean of the
n
values will be approximately equal to expectation of X,
E(X)
, for large
n
. The expectation,
E(X)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle E(X)=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xp_{X}(x).

Definition 3.2.35

(Expected value rule)
.

Consider a discrete random variable and let
Y=g(X)
be a function of X. If we observe
X
many times (
n
times) to give results
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}
and hence
g
provides the observations
g(x_{1}),g(x_{2}),\ldots,g(x_{n})
. The mean of
g(x_{1}),g(x_{2}),\ldots,g(x_{n})
approaches
E(g(X))
, for large
n
. The expectation,
E(g(X))
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle E(g(X))

\displaystyle=E(Y)=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}yp_{Y}(y)=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}y\sum_{g^{-1}(y)=\{x|g(x)=y\}}p_{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}g(x)p_{X}(x).

The expectation tells us important information about the average value of a random variable, but there is a lot of information that it doesn’t provide. If you are investing in the stock market, the expected rate of return of some stock is not the only quantity you will be interested in, you would also like to get some idea of the riskiness of the investment. The typical size of the
fluctuations of a random variable around its expectation is described by another summary statistic, the variance.

Definition 3.2.36

(Variance of a discrete random variable)
.

For a discrete random variable
X
, the variance,
Var(X)
, of
X
is defined by

\displaystyle Var(X)=E\left(\left(X-E(X)\right)^{2}\right)=E(X^{2})-(E(X))^{2}.

provided that this quantity exists. We can also treat variance,
Var(X)
, of X as an expected value
E(g(X))
of the function
g(X)=(X-E(X))^{2}
.

That is variance is a measure of how much the distribution of X is spread out about its mean and only randomness gives rise to variance. Statisticians often prefer to use the standard deviation (in practice for interpretation and visualization) rather than variance (in principle for theoretical analysis and understanding) as a measure of spread.

Definition 3.2.37

(Covariance of two a joint discrete random variable)
.

Consider a joint discrete random variable
(X,Y)
, where
X
and
Y
are associated with the same random experiment, then covariance of
(X,Y)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle Cov(X,Y)=E\left((X-E(X))(Y-E(Y))\right)

\displaystyle=E\left(XY-XE(Y)-YE(X)+E(X)E(Y)\right)=E(XY)-E(X)E(Y).

Theorem 3.2.38
.

If X and Y are independent discrete random variables whose expectations exist, then

\displaystyle(a.)\ E(XY)=E(X)E(Y),\ (b.)\ E(g(X)h(Y))=E(g(X))E(h(Y)).

Example 3.2.39

(A counter-example for converse of (
3.3.51
))
.

Consider a uniformly distributed discrete random variable,
X\in\{-1,0,1\}
i.e.
X\sim Unif([-1,1]\cap\mathbb{Z})
, then

\displaystyle a.\ E(X)=0,\ b.\ E(X^{2})=\frac{2}{3},\ c.\ E(X^{3})=0,

\displaystyle d.\ Cov(X,X^{2})=E(X^{3})-E(X)E(X^{2})=0.

For the observation
X^{2}=t
,

\displaystyle p_{X|X^{2}}(x|t)=\frac{P(X=x,X^{2}=t)}{P(X^{2}=t)}=\frac{P(X=x,X\in\{-\sqrt{t},\sqrt{t}\})}{P(X\in\{-\sqrt{t},\sqrt{t}\})}.

So,

\displaystyle p_{X|X^{2}}(\sqrt{t}|t)=\frac{p_{X}(\sqrt{t})}{p_{X}(-\sqrt{t})+p_{X}(\sqrt{t})}\neq p_{X}(\sqrt{t}).

Also,

\displaystyle p_{X|X^{2}}(-\sqrt{t}|t)=\frac{p_{X}(-\sqrt{t})}{p_{X}(-\sqrt{t})+p_{X}(\sqrt{t})}\neq p_{X}(-\sqrt{t})

Therefore
X
and
X^{2}
are dependent discrete random variable despite of having zero covariance.

Theorem 3.2.40
.

If
X
is a non-negative and non-constant discrete random variable, then
X
and
X^{2}
are positively correlated, and hence dependent.

Proof 3.2.41
.

We have
X\geq 0
and
X^{2}\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle Cov(X,X^{2})

\displaystyle=E\left((X-E(X))(X^{2}-E(X^{2}))\right)

\displaystyle=E\left((X-E(X))(X^{2}-(E(X))^{2}+(E(X))^{2}-E(X^{2}))\right)

\displaystyle=E\left((X-E(X))(X^{2}-(E(X))^{2})\right)

\displaystyle+E\left((X-E(X))((E(X))^{2}-E(X^{2}))\right)

\displaystyle=E\left((X-E(X))(X^{2}-(E(X))^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=E\left((X-E(X))^{2}(X+E(X))\right)>0.

Example 3.2.42
.

There are 5 candidates for 2 job openings, 3 of the candidates are women and 2 are men. Let X be the number of women hired. Find PMF, expected value, and variance of X.

Solution: the count of total possible outcomes of hiring 2 person from 5 candidates as a size of the sample is given by

\displaystyle|\Omega|=\binom{5}{2}=10.

Suppose, X be the number of women hired, then probability mass function of X is computed as:

\displaystyle\{X=0\}\implies|\{X=0\}|=\binom{3}{0}\cdot\binom{2}{2}=1\implies p_{X}(0)=P(X=0)=\frac{1}{10}.

\displaystyle\{X=1\}\implies|\{X=1\}|=\binom{3}{1}\cdot\binom{2}{1}=6\implies p_{X}(1)=P(X=1)=\frac{6}{10}.

\displaystyle\{X=2\}\implies|\{X=2\}|=\binom{3}{2}\cdot\binom{2}{0}=3\implies p_{X}(2)=P(X=2)=\frac{3}{10}.

Then

\displaystyle E(X)=\sum_{x=0}^{2}xp_{X}(x)=\frac{12}{10},\ E(X^{2})=\sum_{x=0}^{2}x^{2}p_{X}(x)=\frac{18}{10}\implies Var(X)=\frac{36}{100}.

Example 3.2.43

(Expectation and variance of various discrete distributions)
.

Determine the expectation and variance of the following discrete random variables:

(a.)

X\sim Ber(p)
,

Solution 3.2.44
.

As
X\sim Ber(p)
, so
p_{X}(x)=p^{x}(1-p)^{1-x}
for
x\in\{0,1\}
. Then

\displaystyle E(X)=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}}xp_{X}(x)=0\cdot(1-p)+1\cdot p=p.

And,

\displaystyle E(X^{2})=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}}x^{2}p_{X}(x)=0\cdot(1-p)+1\cdot p=p.

Then,

\displaystyle Var(X)=E(X^{2})-(E(X))^{2}=p-p^{2}=p(1-p).

(b.)

Y\sim Bin(n,p)
,

Solution 3.2.45
.

As
Y\sim Ber(p)
,
p_{Y}(y)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}n\\
y\end{array}\right)p^{y}(1-p)^{n-y}
for
y\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,n\}
. Then

\displaystyle E(Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}yp_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}y\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}n\\
y\end{array}\right)p^{y}(1-p)^{n-y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}n\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}n-1\\
y-1\end{array}\right)p^{y}(1-p)^{n-y}=np.

And,

\displaystyle E(Y^{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}y^{2}p_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}y^{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}n\\
y\end{array}\right)p^{y}(1-p)^{n-y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}npy\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}n-1\\
y-1\end{array}\right)p^{y-1}(1-p)^{n-y}

\displaystyle=np\left((n-1)p+1\right)=(np)^{2}+np(1-p).

Then,

\displaystyle Var(Y)

\displaystyle=E(Y^{2})-(E(Y))^{2}=(np)^{2}+np(1-p)-(np)^{2}

\displaystyle=np(1-p).

(c.)

Z\sim Geo(p)
,

Solution 3.2.46
.

As
Z\sim Geo(p)
, so
p_{Z}(z)=p(1-p)^{z-1}
for
x\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
. Then

\displaystyle E(Z)=\sum_{z\in\{1,2,\ldots\}}zp_{Z}(z)=\frac{1}{p}.

And,

\displaystyle E(Z(Z-1))=\sum_{z\in\{1,2,\ldots\}}z(z-1)p_{Z}(z)=\frac{2(1-p)}{p^{2}}.

Then,

\displaystyle Var(Z)

\displaystyle=E(Z^{2})-(E(Z))^{2}=E(Z(Z-1))+E(Z)-(E(Z))^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-p}{p^{2}}.

(d.)

W\sim Poiss(\lambda)
.

Solution 3.2.47
.

As
W
, we have
p_{W}(w)=\frac{\lambda^{w}e^{-\lambda}}{w!}\ \mbox{for}\ w=0,1,2,\ldots
with
E(W)=\lambda=Var(W)
.

Definition 3.2.48

(Conditional expectation)
.

In a probabilistic model if a certain event A has already occurred, then we define conditional expectation of the possible observations of a random variable X conditioned on the event A as follows:

\displaystyle E(X|A)=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xp_{X|A}(x|A).

Moreover, if the event A is characterized by another discrete random variable
Y
in the same experiment as
Y=y
and the above summation is absolutely convergent, then the conditional expectation of X conditioned on
Y=y
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle E(X|Y=y)=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xp_{X|Y}(x|y)=g(y).

That is for each possible observation
Y=y,E(X|Y=y)=g(y)
, and hence conditional expectation defines a random variable (as a function of random variable) as follows:

\displaystyle g(Y)=E(X|Y).

Theorem 3.2.49

(Law of iterated expectation)
.

If
\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots\}
be a partition of
\Omega
such that
P(B_{i})>0\ \forall\ i
, and
X
is a discrete random variable in the same experiment, then

\displaystyle E(X)=\sum_{i\geq 1}E(X|B_{i})P(B_{i}).

Moreover, if
Y
is a discrete random variable such that
Y^{-1}(y_{i})=B_{i}=\{\omega|Y(\omega)=y_{i}\}
, then

\displaystyle E(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}E(X|Y=y)P(Y=y)=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{y}\}}E(X|Y=y)p_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=E(E(X|Y)).

Proof 3.2.50
.

We have

\displaystyle E(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xp_{X}(x)=\sum_{x}xP(X=x)=\sum_{x}x\sum_{i}P(X=x,B_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}x\sum_{i}P(B_{i})P(X=x|B_{i})=\sum_{i}P(B_{i})\sum_{x}xP(X=x|B_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}E(X|B_{i})P(B_{i}).

Further, We have

\displaystyle LHS

\displaystyle=E(E(X|Y))=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}E(X|Y=y)p_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xp_{X|Y}(x|y)p_{Y}(y)=\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xp_{(X,Y)}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}x\sum_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xp_{X}(x)=E(X)=RHS.

Theorem 3.2.51
.

Suppose X and Y are discrete random variables and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
are constants. Then

\displaystyle E(aX+bY)=aE(X)+bE(Y)

provided that both
E(X)
and
E(Y)
exist.

Proof 3.2.52
.

\displaystyle E(aX+bY)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\sum_{y}(ax+by)p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)=a\sum_{x}x\sum_{y}p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)

\displaystyle+b\sum_{y}y\sum_{x}p_{(X,Y)}(x,y)=a\sum_{x}xp_{X}(x)+b\sum_{y}yp_{Y}(x)

\displaystyle=aE(X)+bE(Y).

Example 3.2.53
.

Let
X
be the number of rolls of a fair die required to get the first 6. (So X is geometrically distributed with parameter
1/6
.) Now, computation of expectation,
E(X)
, and variance,
Var(X)
, of
X
as below.

Let
B_{1}
be the event that the first roll of the die gives a 6 (i.e.
X|B_{1}\in\{1\}
or
\Omega_{X|B_{1}}=\{1\}
, so that
B_{1}^{c}
is the event that it does not (so
X|B_{1}^{c}\in\{2,3,\ldots\}
or
\Omega_{X|B_{1}^{c}}=\{2,3,\ldots\}
or number of rolls till first 6 given the first roll is nor 6 or
1+X
as
\underline{1}\ \underrightarrow{\ \ X\ \ }
). Then

\displaystyle E(X)

\displaystyle=E(X|B_{1})P(B_{1})+E(X|B_{1}^{c})P(B_{1}^{c})=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{5}{6}E(X|B_{1}^{c})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{5}{6}E(X+1)=1+\frac{5}{6}E(X)\implies E(X)=6.

Above computation is much simpler as compared a direct calculation using the probability mass function:

\displaystyle E(X)=\sum_{x=1}^{\infty}x\left(\frac{1}{6}\right)\left(\frac{5}{6}\right)^{x-1}=\frac{1}{1/6}=6.

\displaystyle E(X^{2})

\displaystyle=E(X^{2}|B_{1})+E(X^{2}|B_{1}^{c})P(B_{1}^{c})=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{5}{6}E(X^{2}|B_{1}^{c})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{5}{6}E((1+X)^{2})=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{5}{6}(1+2E(X)+E(X^{2}))

\displaystyle=11+\frac{5}{6}E(X^{2})\implies E(X^{2})=66.

And hence variance
Var(X)=E(X^{2})-(E(X))^{2}=66-36=30
. Alternatively, we compute the variance as follows:

\displaystyle E(X^{2})=\sum_{x=1}^{\infty}x^{2}\left(\frac{1}{6}\right)\left(\frac{5}{6}\right)^{x-1}=66\implies Var(X)=30.

Example 3.2.54
.

Your spaghetti bowl contains
n
strands of spaghetti. You repeatedly choose
2
ends at random, and join them together. Compute the average number of loops in the bowl, once no ends remain.

Answer:

a.

On the first try you have n noodles and
2n
ends. You pick an end and the probability that you pick up the other end of the same noodle and make a loop is 1 out of
2n-1
i.e
\frac{1}{2n-1}
.

b.

On the next try you have n-1 noodles and
2n-2
ends. You pick an end and the probability that you pick up the other end of the same noodle and make a loop is 1 out of
2n-3
i.e
\frac{1}{2n-3}
.

c.

Since, formation of each loop decreases the number of ends by 2. So, on the ith try you
(n-i)
noodles and
2(n-i)+1)
ends. You pick an end and the probability that you pick up the other end of the same noodle and make a loop is 1 out of
2(n-i)+1
.

Let
X_{i}
be the indicator function of the event that we form a loop at the ith step. Then
E(X_{i})=1/(2(n-i)+1)
. Suppose T be the total number of loops formed, then
T=E(X_{1}+X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n})
. So, using linearity of expectation, we have

\displaystyle E(T)

\displaystyle=E(X_{1}+X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n})=\frac{1}{2n-1}+\frac{1}{2n-3}+\ldots+1/3+1

\displaystyle\approx log(n)\ \mbox{for large}\ n.

Alternatively, let the expected number of loops from n noodles be
E(n)
. Then, obviously,
E(1)=1
. Now, for
n>1
, if you pick up two ends, the possibility for those two ends belongs to the same noodle will be
\frac{1}{2n-1}
. Then you have one loop now and there are
n-1
noodles to keep on going. Otherwise, you get no loop and still have
n-1
noodles to go (the two you pick before are now connected as one). Pick up two ends. They either belong to the same noodle (S) or they don’t (S’ i.e. no loop addition from the two end). So,

\displaystyle E(n)

\displaystyle=E(n|S)P(S)+E(n|S^{\prime})P(S^{\prime})=\left(1+E(n-1)\right)\cdot\frac{1}{2n-1}

\displaystyle+E(n-1)\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{2n-1}\right)=E(n-1)+\frac{1}{2n-1}.

Then, inductively you may expect that

\displaystyle E(n)=1+\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{5}+\ldots+\frac{1}{2n-1}\to\ln(n)\ \mbox{for large n}.

3.2.6
Function of jointly discrete random variables

The purpose of assessing the student’s selection in a campus placement, we might wish to know his/her cgp, numbers of desired courses, number of projects, etc. All these measurements and others are used by an interview panel/recruiter agency for an offer assessment of a candidate.

Theorem 3.2.55

(An another form of total probability)
.

In general to compute probabilities of events it is advantageous to use conditioning arguments whether or not X and Y are independent.

\displaystyle P(Y\in A)=\sum_{x}P\left(Y\in A|X=x\right)p_{X}(x)dx=\sum_{x}\sum_{A}p_{Y|X}(y|x)p_{X}(x).

Corollary 3.2.56

(PDF of a function of jointly discrete random variables
(X,Y)
)
.

Consider
X
and
Y
are two jointly discrete and independent random variables and a function
Z=\frac{Y}{X}
, then PMF of
Z
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle p_{Z}(z)=\sum_{x}p_{X}(x)p_{Y}(xz).

Proof 3.2.57
.

Derivation as follows:

\displaystyle p_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z=z)=P(Y=zX)=\sum_{x}P\left(Y=zx|X=x\right)p_{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}p_{Y|X}(zx|x)p_{x}(x)=\sum_{x}p_{x}(x)p_{Y}(zx).

Corollary 3.2.58

(PDF of a function of jointly discrete random variables
(X,Y)
)
.

Consider
X
and
Y
are two jointly discrete and independent random variables and a function
Z=XY
, then PMF of
Z
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle p_{Z}(z)=\sum_{x}p_{X}(x)p_{Y}(z/x).

Proof 3.2.59
.

Derivation as follows:

\displaystyle p_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z=z)=P\left(Y=\frac{z}{X}\right)=\sum_{x}P\left(Y=\frac{z}{x}|X=x\right)p_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}p_{X}(x)p_{Y|X}(z/x|x)=\sum_{x}p_{X}(x)p_{Y}(z/x).

Conditional PMFs can be used to simplify probability calculations. For example, consider the determination of the PMF for
Z=X+Y
, where X and Y are jointly discrete and independent random variables as
p_{Z}(z)=p_{X}\*p_{Y}(z)
. To solve this problem using conditional PMFs, we ask ourselves the question: Could I find the PMF of Z if X were known? If so, then we should be able to use conditioning arguments to first find the conditional PMF of Z given X, and then uncondition the result to yield the PMF of Z.

Corollary 3.2.60

(PDF of a function of jointly discrete random variables
(X,Y)
)
.

Consider
X
and
Y
are two jointly discrete and independent random variables and a function
Z=X+Y
, then PMF of
Z
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle p_{Z}(z)=\sum_{x}p_{X}(x)p_{Y}(z-x).

Proof 3.2.61
.

Derivation as follows:

\displaystyle p_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z=z)=P(X+Y=z)=\sum_{x}P\left(Y=z-x|X=x\right)p_{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}p_{Y|X}(z-x|x)p_{x}(x)=\sum_{x}p_{x}(x)p_{Y}(z-x).

3.3
Continuous distribution

In the previous section, we discussed discrete random variables and the relevant methods employed to describe them probabilistically. The principal assumption necessary in order to do so is that the range of sample space
\Omega_{X}
, which is the set of all possible outcomes, is finite or at most countably infinite. It followed then that a probability mass function (PMF) could be defined as the probability of each sample point and used to calculate the probability of all possible events (which are subsets of the sample space). Most physical measurements, however, do not produce a discrete set of values but rather a continuum of values such as the rainfall measurement data, maximum temperature measured during the day. The number of possible rainfalls or temperatures take values, in an interval (e.g.
[20,60]
, is in continuum (i.e. infinite and uncountable). Of course, we could always choose to round off the measurement to the nearest unit so that the possible outcomes would then become
\{20,21,\ldots,60\}
. Then, many valid PMFs could be
assigned. But this approach compromises the measurement precision and so is to be avoided if possible. What we are ultimately interested in is the probability of any interval, to do so we must extend our previous approaches to be able to handle this new case. And if we later decide that less precision is warranted, such that the rounding of 20.6 to 21 is acceptable, we will still be able to determine the probability of observing 21. To do so we can regard the rounded temperature of 21 as having arisen from all temperatures in the interval
A=[20.5,21.5)
. Then,
P(\mbox{rounded temperature}=21)=P(A)
, so that we have lost nothing by considering a continuum of outcomes.

Definition 3.3.1

(Continuous random variable)
.

A measurable function
X:\Omega\to\Omega_{X}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
X^{-1}(B)\in\Sigma_{\Omega}\ \forall\ B\in\mathcal{B}
such that

(a.)
X^{-1}(B)=\{\omega|X(\omega)\in B\}\in\Sigma_{\Omega}\ \mbox{for every}\ B\in\mathcal{B}
, (b.)
\Omega_{X}\subset\mathbb{R}
is at least continuum in nature.

(c.)
\Omega_{X}\neq\{x_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\implies B
is an interval or union of interval and we bring at most countable scenario using discretization or
\varepsilon-
covering.

Then, the measurable function X is known as a continuous random variable and its probability distribution is characterized by a probability density function. An event of a continuous random variable
X
is described as below:

\displaystyle X\in B

\displaystyle=\{X=x\in[a,b]\}\equiv\{X\in[a,b]\}\equiv\{\omega|X(\omega)\in[a,b]\}

\displaystyle\ \mbox{for some real numbers}\ a,b\in\mathbb{R}.

Here,
\{X\in B\}\to
the event that the random variable
X
takes the given value x from an interval
B=[a,b]
in continuum manner.

Figure 3.17:
Continuous random variable as a mapping of an at least continuum sample space into an at least continuum set in
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 3.3.2

(Probability density function)
.

Distribution pattern of a continuous random variable is characterized by a probability density function (pdf) (see in (
3.18
)),
f:\Omega_{X}\to\mathbb{R_{+}}
as a probability per unit length and defined as follows:

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{P(x\leq X\leq x+\delta)}{\delta}\implies P(x\leq X\leq x+\delta)\approx f_{X}(x)\delta.

Figure 3.18
:
Characterization probability density function as a probability per unit length.

The PDF
p_{X}
satisfies the following properties:

(a.)
f_{X}(x)\geq 0
, (b.)
\int_{\Omega_{X}}f_{X}(x)dx=1
, (c.) for any event
X\in B
, we have
P(X\in B)=\int_{x\in B}f_{X}(x)dx
.

Nature of an event in term of continuous random variable: if
X
is a continuous random variable, then
\{\omega|X(\omega)\leq\}=\{X\leq x\}=(-\infty,x]
is a basic event for every x. If we have an event
\{a<X\leq b\}
, then it is characterized as
\{a<X\leq b\}=\{X>a\}\cap\{X\leq b\}
, where
\{X>a\}=\{X\leq a\}^{c}
. Furthermore,
\{a-\frac{1}{n}<X\leq a+\frac{1}{n}\}
is an event for every
n
and consequently implies
\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\{a-\frac{1}{n}<X\leq a+\frac{1}{n}\}=\{X=a\}
is also an event. And, probability of the above events are computed as follows

\displaystyle P(X\in(a,b])

\displaystyle=P(\{a<X\leq b\})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{X}(x_{i})\delta x_{i}=\int_{a}^{b}f_{X}(x)dx,

\displaystyle P(\{X=a\})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{X}(a)\frac{1}{n}=0\ \mbox{as}\ P(x_{i-1}<X\leq x_{i})\approx f_{X}(a)\delta x_{i}.

3.3.1
Various continuous distribution

Example 3.3.3

(Uniform distribution)
.

If a continuous random variable
X
takes values in an interval
[a,b]
, and all subintervals of the same length (with respect to uniform partition of the interval) are equally likely. That is,
X
has a uniform continuous distribution (i.e. a uniform PDF) if

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{b-a}&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in[a,b]\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ x\notin[a,b].\end{array}\right.

So,
X
is a uniformly continuous random variable and we write
X\sim Unif([a,b])
. e.g. in a wheel of fortune game, a gambler spins a wheel of fortune, continuously calibrated between 0 and 1, and observes the resulting number. Then, all subintervals of
[0,1]
of the same length (under a uniform discretization scheme) are equally likely, and hence

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in[0,1]\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ x\notin[0,1].\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.19
:
Probability density function as a uniformly distributed random variable on
[a,b]
.

Remark 3.3.4

(Characterization of PDF of continuous random variable)
.

The uniform distribution is the simplest continuous random variable and that obeys uniform law (or equally likely principle) as follows (for a small incremental length
\delta
):

for any two partition points
x_{i},x_{j}
of
[a,b]
,

\displaystyle P(x_{i}<X\leq x_{i}+\delta)=P(x_{j}<X\leq x_{j}+\delta)\implies f_{X}(x_{i})\delta=f_{X}(x_{j})\delta

\displaystyle\implies f_{X}(x_{i})=f_{X}(x_{j}).

So
f_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{b-a}\to
is a constant and hence defines a uniform continuous distribution.

For other than uniform continuous random variable, the corresponding PDF is non uniform is characterized as below:

For small values of
\delta
and for any two partition points
x_{i},x_{j}
of
[a,b]
, we have

\displaystyle P(x_{i}<X\leq x_{i}+\delta)\neq P(x_{j}<X\leq x_{j}+\delta)\implies f_{X}(x_{i})\delta\neq f_{X}(x_{j})\delta

\displaystyle\implies f_{X}(x_{i})\neq f_{X}(x_{j}).

So
f_{X}(x)
is a non-constant and hence defines a non-uniform continuous distribution.

Example 3.3.5

(A piecewise continuous PDF)
.

Alvin’s driving time to work is between
15
and
20
minutes if the day is sunny, and between
20
and
25
minutes if the day is rainy, with all times being equally likely in each case. Assume that a day is sunny with probability
2/3
and rainy with probability
1/3
. Compute the PDF of the driving time.

Here, all times are equally likely in each case means the PDF of
X
is constant in each of the two subintervals
[15,20]
and
[20,25]
. And hence

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}c_{1}&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in[15,20],\\
c_{2}&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in[20,25],\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Further, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}=P(sunny\ day)=\int_{15}^{20}f_{X}(x)dx=5c_{1}\implies c_{1}=\frac{2}{15},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}=P(rainy\ day)=\int_{20}^{25}f_{X}(x)dx=5c_{2}\implies c_{2}=\frac{1}{15}.

Example 3.3.6

(Exponential distribution)
.

A continuous random variable
X
takes values in an interval
[0,\infty)
, and has an exponential continuous distribution (i.e. an exponential PDF) with a parameter
\lambda
if

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}&\ \mbox{if}\ x\geq 0,\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ x<0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.20
:
Probability density function as a exponentially distributed random variable on
[0,\infty)
.

So,
X
is an exponentially continuous random variable with a discontinuous at
x=0
and we write
X\sim Exp(\lambda)
. One important property of exponential distribution is that it provides a formula for right tail probability in order to model life-time or decay-time of a product or object as follows

\displaystyle P(X>a)

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{\infty}f_{X}(x)dx=\int_{a}^{\infty}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}dx=\lim_{b\to\infty}\int_{a}^{b}\lambda e^{-\lambda x}dx

\displaystyle=\lim_{b\to\infty}\left[-e^{-\lambda x}\right]_{a}^{b}=e^{-\lambda a}-\lim_{b\to\infty}e^{-\lambda b}=e^{-\lambda a}.

For example, if
X
is the failure time in days of a light-bulb, then
P(X>100)
is the probability that the light-bulb will fail after 100 days or it will last for at least 100 days. This can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle P(X>100)=e^{-100\lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0.367&\ \mbox{if}\ \lambda=0.01,\\
0.904&\ \mbox{if}\ \lambda=0.001.\end{array}\right.

Example 3.3.7

(Normal distribution)
.

A continuous random variable
X
takes values in an interval
(-\infty,\infty)
, and has a normal distribution (i.e. a bell-shaped Gaussian PDF) with a pair of parameters
(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\mu\in(-\infty,\infty),\sigma\in[0,\infty)
if

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\ \mbox{for}\ x\in(-\infty,\infty).

Figure 3.21
:
Probability density function as a normally distributed random variable on
(-\infty,\infty)
.

So,
X
is an normally continuous random variable characterized by the pair of parameters
(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\mu\in(-\infty,\infty),\sigma\in[0,\infty)
and we write
X\sim Exp(\lambda)
.

The parameter
\mu
indicates the center of the PDF with a depiction of the average value of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a mean or expectation of
X
.

The parameter
\sigma^{2}
indicates the width of the PDF with a depiction of variability of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a variance of
X
.

Example 3.3.8

(Laplace distribution)
.

A continuous random variable
X
takes values in an interval
(-\infty,\infty)
, and has a Laplace distribution (similar to normal distribution, but it does not decrease as rapidly from its maximum value) with a pair of parameters
(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\mu\in(-\infty,\infty),\sigma\in[0,\infty)
if

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}|x|\right)\ \mbox{for}\ x\in(-\infty,\infty).

Figure 3.22
:
Probability density function as a Lapalcianally distributed random variable on
(-\infty,\infty)
.

So,
X
is an Laplacianally continuous random variable characterized by the pair of parameters
(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\mu\in(-\infty,\infty),\sigma\in[0,\infty)
and we write
X\sim Exp(\lambda)
.

The parameter
\mu=0
indicates the center of the PDF with a depiction of the average value of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a mean or expectation of
X
.

The parameter
\sigma^{2}
indicates the width of the PDF with a depiction of variability of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a variance of
X
. Here, the outcomes are larger due to the larger probability in the tails of the PDF (i.e.. the tail region of the PDF is that for which
|x|
is large). This PDF is used as a model for speech amplitudes.

Example 3.3.9

(Cauchy distribution)
.

A continuous random variable
X
takes values in an interval
(-\infty,\infty)
, and has a Cauchy distribution (as a ratio of two standard normal distributions) if

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi(1+x^{2})}\ \mbox{for}\ x\in(-\infty,\infty).

Figure 3.23
:
Probability density function as a Cauchy distributed random variable on
(-\infty,\infty)
.

So,
X
is an Cauchy continuous random variable characterized by the pair of parameters
(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\mu\in(-\infty,\infty),\sigma\in[0,\infty)
and we write
X\sim Exp(\lambda)
.

The parameter
\mu=0
indicates the center of the PDF with a depiction of the average value of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a mean or expectation of
X
.

The parameter
\sigma^{2}
indicates the width of the PDF with a depiction of variability of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a variance of
X
.

Example 3.3.10

(Gamma distribution)
.

A continuous random variable
X
takes values in an interval
[0,\infty)
, and has a gamma distribution (as a generalization of many continuous distributions) if

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\lambda^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}x^{\alpha-1}\exp\left(-\lambda x\right)&\ \mbox{for}\ x\geq 0,\\
0&\ \mbox{for}\ x<0.\end{array}\right.

where
\lambda>0,\alpha>0
, and
\Gamma(\alpha)
is the Gamma function which is defined as below:

\displaystyle\Gamma(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\alpha-1}\exp(-x)dx.

Figure 3.24
:
Probability density function as a gamma distributed random variable on
[0,\infty)
.

Few properties of the gamma distributed random variable are as follows:

a.

\Gamma(\alpha+a)=\alpha\Gamma(\alpha),

b.

Gamma(n)=(n-1)!
for a natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

c.

\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt{\pi}
.

The Gamma PDF reduces to many well known PDFs for appropriate choices of the parameters
\alpha
a and
\lambda
as follows:

a.

If
\alpha=1
, the gamma distribution reduces to an exponential distribution as follows:

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\lambda}{\Gamma(1)}\exp\left(-\lambda x\right)&\ \mbox{for}\ x\geq 0,\\
0&\ \mbox{for}\ x<0.\end{array}\right.

b.

If
\alpha=n/2
and
\lambda=1/2
, the the gamma distribution reduces to a Chi-squared PDF with n degrees of freedom (i.e. the sum of the squares of n independent standard normal random variables all with the same PDF
N(0,1)
) as follows:

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{2^{n}\Gamma(n/2)}x^{n/2-1}\exp\left(-x/2\right)&\ \mbox{for}\ x\geq 0,\\
0&\ \mbox{for}\ x<0.\end{array}\right.

c.

If
\alpha=n
, the the gamma distribution reduces to an Erlang PDF with n degrees of freedom (i.e. the sum of n independent exponential random variables all with the same
\lambda
) as follows:

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\lambda^{n}}{\Gamma(n)}x^{n-1}\exp\left(-\lambda x\right)&\ \mbox{for}\ x\geq 0,\\
0&\ \mbox{for}\ x<0.\end{array}\right.

Example 3.3.11

(Rayleigh distribution)
.

A continuous random variable
X
takes values in an interval
[0,\infty)
, and has a Rayleigh distribution (as a square-root of the sum of two independent standard normal distributions with the same PDF
N(0,1)
) if

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{x}{\sigma^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)&\ \mbox{if}\ x\geq 0,\\
0,&\ \mbox{if}\ x<0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.25
:
Probability density function of a CRV with Rayleigh distribution on
[0,\infty)
.

So,
X
is an Cauchy continuous random variable characterized by the pair of parameters
(\mu,\sigma^{2}),\mu\in(-\infty,\infty),\sigma\in[0,\infty)
and we write
X\sim Exp(\lambda)
.

The parameter
\mu=0
indicates the center of the PDF with a depiction of the average value of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a mean or expectation of
X
.

The parameter
\sigma^{2}
indicates the width of the PDF with a depiction of variability of the observations made by the random variable
X
as a variance of
X
.

Remark 3.3.12
.

Finally, note that many of these PDFs arise as the PDFs of transformed Gaussian random variables. Therefore, realizations of the random variable may be obtained by first generating multiple realizations of independent standard normal or
N(0,1)
random variables, and then performing the appropriate transformation. An alternative and more general approach to generating realizations of a random variable, once the PDF is known, is via the probability integral transformation.

Example 3.3.13

(Multi-valued function of a CRV)
.

In general, if
y=g(x)
has k solutions namely
y_{i}=g_{i}^{-1}(y)
for
i=1,2,\ldots,k
, then we have

\displaystyle p_{Y}(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{X}(g^{-1}_{i}(y))\left|\frac{dg^{-1}_{i}(y)}{dy}\right|.

Theorem 3.3.14
.

Consider a continuous random variable X with a continuous and monotonically increasing cumulative distribution function
F_{X}
, then
Y=F_{X}(X)
is a uniformly distributed random variable on
[0,1]
.

Proof 3.3.15
.

For
y\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(F_{X}(X)\leq y)=P(X\leq F_{X}^{-1}(y))=F_{X}(F_{X}^{-1}(y))

\displaystyle=y\implies f_{Y}(y)=\frac{dF_{Y}(y)}{dy}=1.

So, we have

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\ \mbox{if}\ y\in[0,1]\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ y\notin[0,1].\end{array}\right.

Theorem 3.3.16
.

Consider a continuous random variable X such that
X=F_{X}^{-1}(U)
, where
U\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)
, then X has the probability density function
f_{X}(x)=\frac{dF_{X}(x)}{dx}
.

Proof 3.3.17
.

\displaystyle F_{X}(x)

\displaystyle=P(X\leq x)=P(F_{X}^{-1}(U)\leq x)=P(U\leq F_{X}(x))=F_{U}(F_{X}(x))

\displaystyle=F_{X}(x)\implies f_{X}(x)=\frac{dF_{X}(x)}{dx}.

Remark 3.3.18
.

In simulating the outcome of a discrete random variable
X
, first an outcome of a
U\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)
random variable is generated and then mapped into a value of
X
under the inverse of the CDF. This result is also valid for a continuous random variable so that
X=F_{X}^{-1}(U)
is a random variable with CDF
F_{X}(x)
.

Example 3.3.19

(Laplace distribution)
.

Consider a continuous random variable with the following Laplace distribution:

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}|x|\right)\ \mbox{for}\ x\in(-\infty,\infty).

Then, its cumulative distribution can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle F_{X}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}|x|\right)dx.

For
x<0
, we have

\displaystyle F_{X}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}x\right)dx=\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}x\right)

For
x\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle F_{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}x\right)dx+\int_{0}^{x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}x\right)dx

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}x\right).

Now, we have
y=F_{X}(x)
for each
x
, so

\displaystyle y=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}x\right)&\ \mbox{if}\ x<0\\
1-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}}x\right)&\ \mbox{if}\ x\geq 0.\end{array}\right.

So,
0<y<1/2
for
x<0
and
1/2\leq y<1
for
x\geq 0
, then we have the inverse map

\displaystyle x=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}/2}\ln(2y)&\ \mbox{for}\ y\in(0,1/2)\\
\sqrt{\sigma^{2}/2}\ln(1-2y)&\ \mbox{for}\ y\in[1/2,1).\end{array}\right.

Finally to generate the outcome of a Laplacian random variable we can use

\displaystyle x=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{\sigma^{2}/2}\ln(2u)&\ \mbox{for}\ u\in(0,1/2)\\
\sqrt{\sigma^{2}/2}\ln(1-2u)&\ \mbox{for}\ u\in[1/2,1),\end{array}\right.

where
u
is a realization of a uniformly distributed random variable
\mathcal{U}(0,1)
.

Figure 3.26
:
An example of the outcomes of a Laplacian random variable with
\sigma^{2}=1
, along with the true PDF (the solid curve) and the estimated PDF (the bar plot).

3.3.2
Jointly continuous distribution

In science and in real life, we are often interested in two (or more) random variables at the same time in a random experiment. In such situations the random variables have a joint distribution that allows us to compute probabilities of events involving both variables and understand the relationship between the variables. This is simplest when the variables are independent. When they are not, we use
covariance and correlation as measures of the nature of the dependence between them.

Example 3.3.20

(Health of a student at IIIT-DWD)
.

Consider an experiment of investigate health of a student at III-TDWD, then we need to measure a list of possible attributes (jointly) such as:

a.

health of a student
\to
varies randomly in continuum i.e. it may be characterized as a random variable
H
.

b.

weight of a student
\to
varies randomly in continuum i.e. it may be characterized as a random variable
W
.

c.

average blood pressure of a student
\to
varies randomly in continuum i.e. it may be characterized as a random variable
B
.

d.

body temperature of a student
\to
varies randomly in continuum i.e. it may be characterized as a random variable
T
.

e.

and a few more.

So, using some statistical method, we can come up with a conclusion that

\displaystyle\mbox{helth of a student at IIITDWD}\ =g(H,W,B,T,O).

Definition 3.3.21

(Jointly continuous random variables)
.

Suppose X and Y are two continuous random variables and that X takes values
\Omega_{X}
and Y takes values
\Omega_{Y}
. The ordered pair
(X,Y)
take values in the product
\Omega_{X}\times\Omega_{Y}
. The joint probability density function (joint pdf) of X and Y,
f_{(X,Y)}(x,y)
is defined as the probability per unit as follows:

\displaystyle f_{X,Y}(x,y)=\lim_{(\delta x,\delta y)\to(0^{+},0^{+})}\frac{P(x\leq X\leq x+\delta x,y\leq Y\leq y+\delta y)}{\delta x\delta y}.

And satisfies the following properties:

1.

f_{(X,Y)}(x,y)\geq 0
for any
(x,y)\in\Omega_{X}\times\Omega_{Y}
,

2.

\int_{\{(x,y)\in\Omega_{X}\times\Omega_{Y}\}}f_{(X,Y)}(x,y)dxdy=1
,

3.

P((X,Y)\in B)=\int_{\{(x,y)\in B\}}f_{(X,Y)}(x,y)dxdy
.

Computationally visualizing of joint PDF over the red region in figure (
3.27
).

Figure 3.27
:
A visualizing of computation of joint PDF over the red region.

Definition 3.3.22

(Conditional PDF for a CRV X conditioned on
Y=y
)
.

Consider X and Y are jointly-distributed and continuous random variables with a statistically dependent, we may want to consider only one of them say
X
conditioned on one observation the other, say
X|Y=y
. Then, conditional CDF of X, conditioned on
Y=y
is defined as

\displaystyle f_{X|Y}(x|y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{P(x\leq X\leq x+\delta|Y=y)}{\delta}=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)}{f_{Y}(y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)}{\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f_{X,Y}(x,y)dx}.

Where derivation of conditional probability
P(x\leq X\leq x+\delta|Y=y)
performed as:

\displaystyle P(x\leq X\leq x+\delta|Y=x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}P\left(x\leq X\leq x+\delta\leq x|Y\in(y-\varepsilon,y+\varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{P(x\leq X\leq x+\delta,y\leq Y\leq y+\varepsilon)}{P(y\leq Y\leq y+\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle\approx\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)\delta\varepsilon}{f_{Y}(y)\varepsilon}=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)\delta}{f_{Y}(y)}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle f_{Y|X}(y|x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{P(y\leq Y\leq y+\delta|X=x)}{\delta}=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)}{f_{X}(x)}

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)}{\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}f_{X,Y}(x,y)dy}.

More explicitly, computational visualization of conditional PDFs in figure (
) as:

Figure 3.28
:
Computationally visualizing conditional PDFs
f_{Y|X}(y|x)\&f_{X|Y}(x|y)
.

A geometrical visualization of conditional PDFs including joint and marginal PDFs in figure (
3.29
) as:

Figure 3.29
:
A geometrical visualizing conditional PDF
f_{X_{1}|X_{2}}(x_{1}|b)
including joint and marginal PDFs.

Example 3.3.23

(Conditional PDF from standard bivariate PDF)
.

Consider a joint continuously distributed bivariate
(X,Y)
with a standard bivariate Gaussian PDF as follows:

\displaystyle f_{X,Y}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{(1-\rho^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}-2\rho xy+y^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\right).

and the marginal PDF for X is computed as

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}f_{X,Y}(x,y)dy=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right).

Now, the condition PDF of Y conditioned on
X=x
can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle f_{Y|X}(y|x)=\frac{\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{(1-\rho^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}-2\rho xy+y^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\right)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{(1-\rho^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{Q}{2}\right),

where

\displaystyle Q=-\frac{x^{2}-2\rho xy+y^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}+\frac{x^{2}}{2}=\frac{(y-\rho x)^{2}}{(1-\rho^{2})}.

So, we can write
Y|X=x\sim N\left(x\rho,1-\rho^{2}\right)
and visualize in figure (
3.30
) as follows:

Figure 3.30
:
Standard bivariate Gaussian PDF with
\rho=0.9
and its cross-section at
x=-1
. The
normalized cross-section is the conditional PDF.

And, visualizing joint marginal and conditional PDFs together in figure (
3.31
) as follows:

Figure 3.31
:
Visualizing joint marginal and conditional PDFs in together.

Example 3.3.24
.

Let S denote the height (in inches) of a randomly chosen father, and T denote the height (in inches) of his son at maturity. Suppose each of S and T has a
N(\mu,\sigma^{2})
distribution with
\mu=69
and
\sigma=2
. Suppose also that the standardized variables
\frac{S-\mu}{\sigma}
and
\frac{T-\mu}{\sigma}
have a standard bivariate normal distribution with correlation
\rho=0.3
. If Sam has a height of
S=74
inches, the predict the ultimate height T of his young son Tom.

In standardized units, Sam has height
X=\frac{S-\mu}{\sigma}
, which we are given to equal
2.3
. Tom’s ultimate standardized height is
Y=\frac{T-\mu}{\sigma}
. By assumption, before the value of X was known, the pair
(X,Y)
has a standard bivariate normal distribution with correlation
\rho
. The conditional distribution of Y given that
X=2.5
is

\displaystyle Y|X=2.5\sim N(2.5\rho,1-\rho^{2}).

In the original units, the conditional distribution of T given
S=74
is normal with mean
\mu+2.5\rho
and variance
(1-\rho^{2})\sigma^{2}
, that is, Tom’s ultimate height | Sam’s height = 74 inches
\sim N(70.5,3.64)
. If I had to make a guess, I would predict that Tom would ultimately reach a height of
70.5
inches.

Definition 3.3.25

(Conditional CDF for a CRV X conditioned on
Y=y
)
.

Consider X and Y are jointly-distributed and continuous random variables with a statistically dependent, we may want to consider only one of them say
X
conditioned on one observation the other, say
X|Y=y
. Then, conditional CDF of X, conditioned on
Y=y
is defined as

\displaystyle F_{X|Y}(x|y)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}P\left(X\leq x|Y\in(y-\varepsilon,y+\varepsilon)\right)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}f_{X|Y}(x|y)dx.

Example 3.3.26
.

Consider a joint continuously distributed bivariate
(X,Y)
with a standard bivariate Gaussian PDF as follows:

\displaystyle f_{X,Y}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{(1-\rho^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}-2\rho xy+y^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\right).

and the marginal PDF for X is computed as

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}f_{X,Y}(x,y)dy=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right).

Now, the condition PDF of Y conditioned on
X=x
can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle f_{Y|X}(y|x)=\frac{\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{(1-\rho^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}-2\rho xy+y^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\right)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{(1-\rho^{2})}}\exp\left(-\frac{Q}{2}\right),

where

\displaystyle Q=-\frac{x^{2}-2\rho xy+y^{2}}{2(1-\rho^{2})}+\frac{x^{2}}{2}=\frac{(y-\rho x)^{2}}{(1-\rho^{2})}.

So, we can write
Y|X=x\sim N\left(x\rho,1-\rho^{2}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle F_{Y|X}(y|x)=\int_{-\infty}^{y}f_{Y|X}(y|x)dy=1-\Phi\left(\frac{y-\rho x}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}\right).

Example 3.3.27
.

Consider two continuously distributed random variables
X,Y\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)
and distributed jointly with the following joint PDF:

\displaystyle f_{X,Y}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}cxy&\ \mbox{if}0\leq y\leq x\leq 1\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Then compute (a.)
c
, (b.)
P\left(Y\leq\frac{X}{2}\right)
and (c.)
P\left(Y\leq\frac{X}{4}\right)
. And, visualization of domain of joint PDF and surface of the joint PDF in figure (
) as:

Figure 3.32
:
Visualizing domain of joint PDF in left plot and surface of the joint PDF in right plot.

Answer: to compute
c
, we have

\displaystyle 1=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}f_{X,Y}dxdy=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{y}^{1}cxydxdy=\frac{c}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-y^{2})ydy=\frac{c}{8}

\displaystyle\implies c=8.

Now, to compute the probability
P(Y\leq\frac{X}{2})
, we have

\displaystyle P(Y\leq\frac{X}{2})=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\frac{x}{2}}8xydydx=\int_{0}^{1}x^{3}dx=\frac{1}{4}.

Finally, to compute the probability
P(Y\leq\frac{X}{4})
, we have

\displaystyle P(Y\leq\frac{X}{4})=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\frac{x}{4}}8xydydx=\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{1}x^{3}dx=\frac{1}{16}.

Theorem 3.3.28

(Computation of joint PDF)
.

So, by using the definition of conditional probability for events we have

\displaystyle f_{(X,Y)}((x,y)=f_{Y}(y)f_{X|Y}(x|y)=f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x).

It provides a computational framework to determine joint PMF of the joint discrete random variable
(X,Y)
.

Example 3.3.29

(Life-time of a spare bulb)
.

Question: a professor uses the overhead projector for his class. The time to failure of a new bulb
X
has the exponential PDF
f_{X}(x)=\lambda\exp(-\lambda x)u(x)
, where x is in hours. A new spare bulb also has a time to failure
Y
that is modelled as an exponential PDF.
However, the time to failure of the spare bulb depends upon how long the spare bulb sits unused. Assuming the spare bulb is activated as soon as the original bulb fails, the time to activation is given by X. As a result, the expected time to failure of the spare bulb is decreased as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda_{Y}}=\frac{1}{\lambda(1+\alpha x)}\ \mbox{for}\ \alpha\in(0,1),

where
\alpha
is some factor that indicates the degradation of the unused bulb with storage time. Then compute the PDF of
Y
.

Answer: the expected time to failure of the spare bulb decreases as the original bulb is used longer (and hence the spare bulb must sit unused longer). Thus, we model the time to failure of the spare bulb as

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y|x)=\lambda_{Y}\exp(-\lambda_{Y}y)u(y)=\lambda(1+\alpha x)\exp\left(-\lambda(1+\alpha x)y\right)u(y).

Then, we compute the desired PDF of
Y
as follows:

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x)dx=\int_{x}\lambda^{2}(1+\alpha x)e^{\left(-\lambda(x+(1+\alpha x)y)\right)}dx

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\exp(-\lambda y)\int_{x}(1+\alpha x)\exp(ax)dx,\ a=-\lambda(\alpha y+1)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\exp(-\lambda y)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp(ax)dx+\alpha\int_{0}^{\infty}x\exp(ax)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\exp(-\lambda y)\left(-\frac{1}{a}+\frac{\alpha}{a^{2}}\right),\ a=-\lambda(\alpha y+1)\implies

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\exp(-\lambda y)\left(\frac{1}{(\lambda y+1)}+\frac{\alpha}{(\alpha y+1)^{2}}\right)u(y).

Figure 3.33
:
Conditional PDF for lifetime of spare bulb with dependence on time to failure x of original bulb, and PDFs for time to failure of original bulb
X
& spare bulb
Y
.

Theorem 3.3.30

(An another form of total probability)
.

In general to compute probabilities of events it is advantageous to use conditioning arguments whether or not X and Y are independent.

\displaystyle P(Y\in A)=\int_{x}P\left(Y\in A|X=x\right)f_{X}(x)dx=\int_{x}\int_{A}f_{Y|X}(y|x)dyf_{X}(x)dx.

Theorem 3.3.31

(Bayes rule for probability density function)
.

Consider two jointly continuous random variables
X
with PDF
f_{X}(x)
and
Y
with PDF
f_{Y}(y)
having joint PDF
f_{X,Y}(x,y)
, then restatement of the definition of conditional probability density function leads to computation of a posterior distribution as:

\displaystyle f_{(X,Y)}(x,y)

\displaystyle=f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x)=f_{Y}(y)f_{X|Y}(x|y)\implies

\displaystyle f_{X|Y}(x|y)

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x)}{f_{Y}(y)}=\frac{f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x)}{\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x)},

where
f_{X}(x)\to
prior distribution of
X
,
f_{Y|X}(y|x)=f(x)\to
is the data generating process as a likelihood of
Y=y
conditioned on
X=x
and
f_{Y}(y)=\sum_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x)\to
probability of observing
Y=y
or evidence.

Definition 3.3.32

(Independent jointly continuous random variables)
.

Jointly-distributed continuous random variables X and Y are independent if observation of one random variable before the other is not going to affect their probability density functions or their joint PDF is the product of the marginal PDF’s:

\displaystyle f_{X|Y}(x|y)=f_{X}(x)\ \mbox{or}\ f_{Y|X}(y|x)=f_{Y}(y),

or

\displaystyle f_{(X,Y)}(x,y)=f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(y)\ \mbox{for any}\ (x,y)\in\Omega_{X}\times\Omega_{Y}.

3.3.3
Derived distribution of a continuous random variable

Definition 3.3.33

(Derived distribution of a continuous random variable)
.

Consider a continuous random variable
X
with its PDF
f_{X}(x)
and a transformation
Y=g(X)
as a function of
X
such that
\Omega_{Y}
is continuum in nature. Then probability density function of
Y
can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)=P(Y\leq y)=P(g(X)\leq y)=P(X\leq g^{-1}(y))=F_{X}(g^{-1}(y))

\displaystyle\implies f_{Y}(y)=\frac{dF_{Y}(y)}{dy}=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dy}=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dg^{-1}(y)}\left|\frac{dg^{-1}(y)}{dy}\right|.

So, computation of derived distribution
p_{Y}(y)
of a continuous random variable
X
(with
\Omega_{Y}
as continuum in nature) follows two steps:

1.

Determining all possible observed value of the derived random variable
Y
i.e.
\Omega_{Y}
,

2.

Computing the derived distribution as follows:

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y)=\frac{dF_{Y}(y)}{dy}=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dy}=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dg^{-1}(y)}\left|\frac{dg^{-1}(y)}{dy}\right|.

Example 3.3.34

(Derived distribution of a CRV
X\sim Unif(0,1)
)
.

If
X\sim Unif(0,1)
and
Y=\sqrt{X}
, then derived PDF
f_{Y}(y)
can be computed as:

1.

As
X\sim Unif(0,1)
and
Y=\sqrt{X}
, so
\Omega_{X}=[0,1]
transformed to
\Omega_{Y}=[0,1]
and for each
x\in\Omega_{X},y=\sqrt{x}\implies x=g^{-1}(y)=y^{2}
.

2.

Now, we have

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)=P(Y\leq y)=P(\sqrt{X}\leq y)=P(X\leq y^{2})=\int_{0}^{y^{2}}dx=y^{2},

\displaystyle\implies f_{y}(y)=2y\ \mbox{for}\ y\in[0,1]..

Alternatively

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(g(X)\leq y)=P(X\leq g^{-1}(y))

\displaystyle=F_{X}(g^{-1}(y))=P(X\leq y^{2})=F_{X}(y^{2}),\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dg^{-1}(y)}\left|\frac{dy^{2}}{dy}\right|=1\times 2y=2y.

Example 3.3.35
.

Question: if
X\sim Unif(-1,1)
and
Y=|X|
, then compute the derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=|X|
c.

Answer: derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=|X|
can be computed as:

1.

As
X\sim Unif(-1,1)
and
Y=|X|
, so
\Omega_{X}=[-1,1]
transformed to
\Omega_{Y}=[0,1]
and for each
x\in\Omega_{X},y=|x|\implies x=g^{-1}(y)=\{-y,y\}
.

2.

Now, we have

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(|X|\leq y)=P(X\leq y\in\{-y,y\})

\displaystyle=P(-y\leq X\leq y)=P(X\leq y)-P(X\leq-y),

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=1\ \mbox{for}\ y\in[0,1]..

Alternatively

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(|X|\leq y)=P(X\leq y\in\{-y,y\})

\displaystyle=P(-y\leq X\leq y)=F_{X}(y)-F_{Y}(-y),

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dg^{-1}(y)}\left|\frac{dg^{-1}(y)}{dy}\right|=\frac{1}{2}\times 1+\frac{1}{2}\times 1=1.

Example 3.3.36
.

Question: If
X\sim Unif(-1,1)
and
Y=X^{2}
, then compute the derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=X^{2}
.

Answer: derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=X^{2}
can be computed as:

1.

As
X\sim Unif(-1,1)
and
Y=X^{2}
, so
\Omega_{X}=[-1,1]
transformed to
\Omega_{Y}=[0,1]
and for each
x\in\Omega_{X},y=x^{2}\implies x=g^{-1}(y)=\{-\sqrt{y},\sqrt{y}\}
.

2.

Now, we have

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(X^{2}\leq y)=P(X\leq y\in\{-\sqrt{y},\sqrt{y}\})

\displaystyle=P(-\sqrt{y}\leq X\leq\sqrt{y})=F_{X}(\sqrt{y})-F_{X}(-\sqrt{y}),

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{y}}+\frac{1}{4\sqrt{y}}.

Alternatively

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(X^{2}\leq y)=P(X\leq y\in\{-\sqrt{y},\sqrt{y}\})

\displaystyle=P(-\sqrt{y}\leq X\leq\sqrt{y})=F_{X}(\sqrt{y})-F_{X}(-\sqrt{y})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dg^{-1}(y)}\left|\frac{dg^{-1}(y)}{dy}\right|==\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}}\right)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}}.

Example 3.3.37
.

Question: if
X\sim N(0,1)
and
Y=g(X)=X^{2}
, then compute the derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=X^{2}
.

Answer: derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=X^{2}
can be computed as:

1.

As
X\sim N(0,1)
and
Y=g(X)=X^{2}
, so
\Omega_{X}=(-\infty,\infty)
transformed to
\Omega_{Y}=[0,\infty)
.

2.

Now, for
y\in[0,\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(X^{2}\leq y)=P(X\leq y\in\{-\sqrt{y},\sqrt{y}\})

\displaystyle=P(X\leq-\sqrt{y})+P(X\leq\sqrt{y})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=f_{X}(x)\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi y}}\exp(-y/2).

Alternatively

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(g(X)\leq y)=P(X\leq g^{-1}(y))

\displaystyle=F_{X}(g^{-1}(y))=P(X\leq y\in\{-y,y\})=F_{X}(-y)+F_{Y}(y),

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=\frac{dF_{X}(g^{-1}(y))}{dg^{-1}(y)}\left|\frac{dy^{2}}{dy}\right|=f_{X}(x)\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi y}}\exp(-y/2).

And,
Y
does not observe any value in
(-\infty,0)
, so, we have

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi y}}\exp(-y/2)&\ \mbox{if}\ y\geq 0\\
0,&\ \mbox{if}\ y<0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.34
:
Probability density function for
Y=X^{2},X\sim N(0,1)
.

Example 3.3.38
.

If
X\sim N(0,1)
and
Y=g(X)=\exp(X)
, then compute the derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=\exp(X)
.

Answer: derived distribution
f_{Y}(y)
of
Y=g(X)=\exp(X)
can be computed as:

1.

As
X\sim N(0,1)
and
Y=g(X)=\exp(X)
, so
\Omega_{X}=(-\infty,\infty)
transformed to
\Omega_{Y}=(0,\infty)
.

2.

Now, for
y\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(\exp(X)\leq y)=P(X\leq\ln(y))=F_{X}(\ln(y))

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=\frac{dF_{X}(\ln(y))}{d\ln(y)}\left|\frac{d\ln(y)}{dy}\right|=f_{X}(\ln(y))\frac{1}{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}y}\exp(-(\ln(y))^{2}/2).

Alternatively

\displaystyle F_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=P(Y\leq y)=P(g(X)\leq y)=P(X\leq g^{-1}(y))

\displaystyle=F_{X}(g^{-1}(y))=P(X\leq y\in\{-y,y\})=F_{X}(-y)+F_{Y}(y),

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{y}(y)=\frac{dF_{X}(\ln(y))}{d\ln(y)}\left|\frac{d\ln(y)}{dy}\right|=f_{X}(\ln(y))\frac{1}{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}y}\exp\left(-\frac{(\ln(y))^{2}}{2}\right).

And,
Y
does not observe any value in
(-\infty,0)
, so, we have

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}y}\exp\left(-\frac{(\ln(y))^{2}}{2}\right)&\ \mbox{if}\ y>0\\
0,&\ \mbox{if}\ y\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.35
:
Probability density function for
Y=X^{2},X\sim N(0,1)
.

This PDF is called the log-normal PDF. It is frequently used as a model for a quantity that is measured in decibels (dB) and which has a normal PDF in dB quantities.

Corollary 3.3.39

(PDF of a function of jointly continuous random variables
(X,Y)
)
.

Consider
X
and
Y
are two jointly continuous and independent random variables and a function
Z=\frac{Y}{X}
, then PDF is
Z
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(xz)|x|dx.

Proof 3.3.40
.

Derivation as follows:

\displaystyle F_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z\leq z)=P(Y\leq zX)=\int_{x}P\left(Y\leq zx|X=x\right)f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)=\frac{\partial F_{Z}(z)}{\partial z}=\int_{x}\frac{\partial F_{Y|X}(zx|X=x)}{\partial z}f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{x}f_{Y|X}(zx|X=x)|x|f_{X}(x)dx=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(zx)|x|dx.

Corollary 3.3.41

(PDF of a function of jointly continuous random variables
(X,Y)
)
.

Consider
X
and
Y
are two jointly continuous and independent random variables and a function
Z=XY
, then PDF is
Z
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(z/x)\frac{1}{|x|}dx.

Proof 3.3.42
.

Derivation as follows:

\displaystyle F_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z\leq z)=P\left(Y\leq\frac{z}{X}\right)=\int_{x}P\left(Y\leq\frac{z}{x}|X=x\right)f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)=\frac{\partial F_{Z}(z)}{\partial z}=\int_{x}\frac{\partial F_{Y|X}(z/x|X=x)}{\partial z}f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{x}f_{Y|X}(z/x|X=x)\frac{1}{|x|}f_{X}(x)dx=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(z/x)\frac{1}{|x|}dx.

Theorem 3.3.43

(PDF of a sum of jointly continuous random variables
(X,Y)
)
.

Consider
X
and
Y
are two jointly continuous and independent random variables and a function
Z=X+Y
, then PDF of
Z
is computed as follows:

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(z-x)dx.

Proof 3.3.44
.

Derivation as follows:

\displaystyle F_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z\leq z)=P\left(X+Y\leq z\right)=\int_{x}P\left(Y\leq z-x|X=x\right)f_{X}(x)dx\implies

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial F_{Z}(z)}{\partial z}=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)\frac{\partial F_{Y}(z-x)}{\partial z}dx=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(z-x|X=x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{x}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(z-x)dx.

Example 3.3.45
.

Suppose X and Y are uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
and independent random variables. Find the PDF of
Z=X+Y
.

Solution: We have
X,Y\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)
, then

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&x\in[0,1],\\
0&x\notin[0,1].\end{array}\right.

and

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&y\in[0,1],\\
0&y\notin[0,1].\end{array}\right.

Now,
Z=X+Y
, so
f_{Z}(z)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(z-x)dx
can be computed case-wise as follows:

Case-I: If
0\leq z\leq 1
, then
0\leq x\leq z
, so we have

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)=\int_{0}^{z}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(z-x)dx=\int_{0}^{z}1\cdot 1\cdot dx=z

Case-II: If
1<z\leq 2\equiv 0<z-1\leq 1
then
z-1\leq x\leq 1
, so, we have

\displaystyle f_{Z}(z)=\int_{z-1}^{1}f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(z-x)dx=\int_{z-1}^{1}1\cdot 1\cdot dx=2-z

Figure 3.36
:
Convolution of two uniform and independent distributions on
[0,1]
.

Figure 3.37
:
Convolution of
n
uniform and independent distributions on
[0,1]
.

Example 3.3.46
.

Backdrop: consider two jointly continuous and independent random variables X and Y uniformly distributed in
[0,1]
.

Question: Let
Z=XY
, then compute CDF and PDF of
Z
.

Answer: we have
X\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)
and
Y\sim\mathcal{U}(0,1)
and independent to each other. So, we get

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\ \mbox{if}\ x\in[0,1]\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ x\notin[0,1].\end{array}\right.\ \mbox{and}\ f_{Y}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\ \mbox{if}\ y\in[0,1]\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ y\notin[0,1].\end{array}\right.\implies

\displaystyle f_{X,Y}(x,y)=f_{X}(x)f_{Y}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\ \mbox{if}\ (x,y)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\\
0&\ \mbox{if}\ (x,y)\notin[0,1]\times[0,1].\end{array}\right.

Now, cumulative distribution function of
Z
is given by

\displaystyle F_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z\leq z)=P(XY\leq z)=P\left(X\leq\frac{z}{Y}\right)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\min\left(1,\frac{z}{y}\right)}dxdy

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{\min\left(1,\frac{z}{y}\right)}dxdy=\int_{0}^{1}\min\left(1,\frac{z}{y}\right)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}dy+\int_{z}^{1}\frac{z}{y}dy=z-z\ln(z)\implies f_{Z}(z)=-\ln(z)=\ln\left(\frac{1}{z}\right).

Alternatively, using conditioning and law of total probability, we get

\displaystyle F_{Z}(z)

\displaystyle=P(Z\leq z)=P(XY\leq z)=P\left(X\leq\frac{z}{Y}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}P\left(X\leq\frac{z}{y}|Y=y\right)f_{Y}(y)dy=\int_{0}^{1}\min\left(1,\frac{z}{y}\right)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}dy+\int_{z}^{1}\frac{z}{y}dy=z-z\ln(z)\implies f_{Z}(z)=-\ln(z)=\ln\left(\frac{1}{z}\right).

Figure 3.38
:
In left plot there are depiction of limits for inner integrating element
dx
and in the right plot there are depiction of limits for outer integrating element
dy
.

3.3.4
Expectation and variance of continuous random variables

Definition 3.3.47

(Expectation of a continuous random variable)
.

Consider a continuous random variable with a continuous distribution or probability density function
f_{X}(x)
. The expectation,
E(X)
is defined as:

\displaystyle E(X)=\int_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xf_{X}(x)dx.

Definition 3.3.48

(Expected value rule)
.

Consider a continuous random variable X with PDF
f_{X}(x)
and let
Y=g(X)
be a function of X. The expectation,
E(g(X))
is defined as:

\displaystyle E(g(X))

\displaystyle=\int_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}yf_{Y}(y)dy=\int_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}y\left(\int_{g^{-1}(y)=\{x|g(x)=y\}}f_{X}(x)dx\right)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}g(x)f_{X}(x)dx.

The expectation tells us important information about the average value of a random variable, but there is a lot of information that it doesn’t provide. If you are investing in the stock market, the expected rate of return of some stock is not the only quantity you will be interested in, you would also like to get some idea of the riskiness of the investment. The typical size of the
fluctuations of a random variable around its expectation is described by another summary statistic, the variance.

Definition 3.3.49

(Variance of a continuous random variable)
.

For a continuous random variable
X
with a PDF
f_{X}(x)
, the variance,
Var(X)
is defined as:

\displaystyle Var(X)=E\left(\left(X-E(X)\right)^{2}\right)=E(X^{2})-(E(X))^{2}.

provided that this quantity exists. We can also treat variance,
Var(X)
, of X as an expected value
E(g(X))
of the function
g(X)=(X-E(X))^{2}
.

That is variance is a measure of how much the distribution of X is spread out about its mean and only randomness gives rise to variance. Statisticians often prefer to use the standard deviation (in practice for interpretation and visualization) rather than variance (in principle for theoretical analysis and understanding) as a measure of spread.

Definition 3.3.50

(Covariance of two jointly continuous random variables)
.

Consider a jointly continuous random variable
(X,Y)
with joint PDF
f_{X,Y}(x,y)
, where
X
and
Y
are associated with the same random experiment, then covariance of
(X,Y)
is defined as:

\displaystyle Cov(X,Y)

\displaystyle=E\left((X-E(X))(Y-E(Y))\right)=E\left(XY-XE(Y)-YE(X)\right.

\displaystyle+\left.E(X)E(Y)\right)=E(XY)-E(X)E(Y).

Furthermore, the variance-covariance matrix is given by

\displaystyle C_{X,Y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}Var(X)&Cov(X,Y)\\
Cov(X,Y)&Var(Y)\end{array}\right).

Theorem 3.3.51
.

If X and Y are independent and jointly continuous random variables whose expectations exist, then

\displaystyle a.\ E(XY)=E(X)E(Y)\ \mbox{and}\ b.\ Cov(X,Y)=0.

Definition 3.3.52

(Conditional expectation)
.

In a probabilistic model if a certain event A has already occurred, then we define conditional expectation of the possible observations of a continuous random variable X conditioned on the event A as follows:

\displaystyle E(X|A)=\int_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xf_{X|A}(x|A)dx.

Moreover, if the event A is characterized by another continuous random variable
Y
in the same experiment as
Y=y
, then the conditional expectation of X conditioned on
Y=y
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle E(X|Y=y)=\int_{\{x\in\Omega_{X}\}}xf_{X|Y}(x|y)dx=g(y).

That is for each possible observation
Y=y,E(X|Y=y)=g(y)
, and hence conditional expectation defines a random variable (as a function of random variable Y) as:

\displaystyle g(Y)=E(X|Y).

Example 3.3.53
.

Question: consider two jointly continuous random variables
(X,Y)
with a joint PDF
f_{X,Y}=2
over a triangle region
X+Y\leq 1,X\geq 0,Y\geq 0
. Compute the conditional expectation
E(X|Y=y)
conditioned on the observation
Y=y
.

Answer: we have the joint PDF as

\displaystyle f_{X,Y}(x,y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}2&x+y\leq 1,x\geq 0,y\geq 0\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.39
:
In left plot there is a joint region of the joint
f_{X,Y}=2
, in right plot its marginal PDF
f_{Y}(y)
.

Now, we compute marginal PDF
f_{Y}(y)
(for
0\leq y\leq 1
) from the given joint PDF
f_{X,Y}(x,y)
as:

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{X,Y}(x,y)dx=\int_{0}^{1-y}2dx=2(1-y).

And the condition PDF
f_{X|Y}(x|y)
, conditioned on
Y=y
, can be computed as:

\displaystyle f_{X|Y}(x|y)=\frac{f_{X,Y}(x,y)}{f_{Y}(y)}=\frac{2}{2(1-y)}=\frac{1}{1-y},0\leq y\leq 1.

Then, we compute the conditional expectation
E(X|Y=y)
, conditioned on
Y=y
, can be computed as:

\displaystyle E(X|Y=y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}xf_{X|Y}(x|y)dx=\int_{0}^{1-y}x\frac{1}{(1-y)}dx=\frac{1-y}{2}.

As
y
varies, so does the conditional expectation
E(X|Y=y)
, and hence, we have the conditional expectation as a random variable as:

\displaystyle E(X|Y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-Y}{2}\implies E(X)=\frac{1}{2}(1-E(Y)),E(X)=E(Y)\implies

\displaystyle E(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}.

Figure 3.40
:
In left plot there is a conditional PDF
