f_{X|Y}(x|y)
, in right plot its conditional expectation
E(X|Y=y)
.

Theorem 3.3.54

(Law of iterated expectation)
.

If
\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots\}
be a partition of
\Omega
such that
P(B_{i})>0\ \forall\ i
, and
X
is a continuous random variable in the same experiment, then

\displaystyle E(X)=\sum_{i\geq 1}E(X|B_{i})P(B_{i}).

Moreover, if
Y
is a continuous random variable such that
Y^{-1}(y_{i})=B_{i}=\{\omega|Y(\omega)=y_{i}\}
, then

\displaystyle E(X)=\int_{\{y\in\Omega_{Y}\}}E(X|Y=y)f_{Y}(y)dy=E(E(X|Y)).

Theorem 3.3.55
.

Consider two jointly continuous random variables
(X,Y)
. Then, the variance of conditional expectation and expected value of conditional variance are related as:

\displaystyle Var(X)=Var(E(X|Y))+E(Var(X|Y)).

Proof 3.3.56
.

We have

\displaystyle Var(X)

\displaystyle=E((X-E(X))^{2})=E((X-E(X|Y)+E(X|Y)-E(X))^{2})

\displaystyle=E((X-E(X|Y))^{2})+2E((X-E(X|Y))(E(X|Y)-E(X)))

\displaystyle+E((E(X|Y)-E(X))^{2})

Example 3.3.57

(
(
Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis 2008
)
)
.

Question: we start with a stick of length
\ell
. We break it at a point which is chosen randomly and uniformly over its length, and keep the piece that contains the left end of the stick. We then repeat the same process on the stick that we were left with. Compute the expected length of the stick that we are left with, after breaking twice.

Answer: Let Y be the length of the stick after we break for the first time. Let X be the length after the second time. We have
E(X|Y)=\frac{Y}{2}
, since the breakpoint is chosen uniformly over the length Y of the remaining stick. For a similar reason, we also have
E(Y)=\frac{\ell}{2}
. Then, the expected value of
X
can be computed as:

\displaystyle E(X)=E(E(X|Y))=E(Y/2)=\frac{\ell}{4}.

3.4
General random variable

It may be of interest to note that discrete and continuous random variables can be subsumed under the topic of a general random variable. There exists the mathematical machinery to analyse both types of random variables simultaneously. This theory is called measure theory.

Chapter 4
Large sample theory

Till now, we have described and computed the exact probability of events based on known probability mass functions (PMFs)/probability density functions (PDFs) and the implementation of its summation/integration. Also of importance were the methods to determine the moments/statistic/parameters of these random variables. In many practical situations the PMF/PDF may be unknown or the summation/integration may not be easily carried out. It would be of great utility, therefore, to be able to approximate the desired quantities using much simpler methods e.g. random sampling and point estimation. Law of large numbers asserts that the sample mean (the average of IID copies) converges to the expected value, a number, of each random variable in the average. Also, it provides a justification for the relative frequency interpretation of probability. Central limit theorem asserts that a properly normalized sum of I.I.D. random variables converges to a Gaussian random variable. Alternatively, it can be demonstrated that the repeated convolution of PDFs produces a Gaussian PDF.

So, in this section, we discuss large sample theory of sample statistic, when distribution of a measurement (or a random variable) is unknown. It discuss one (i.e. large sample size) of two important concepts to understand, obverse and utilize Big-Data. Another one (i.e. high-dimension) will be discussed in the next chapter (
5
).

4.1
Sample statistic

Consider a random variable (or a measurement)
X
with unknown distribution, then it is very difficult to compute statistic or parameter of the observed values under the unknown distribution. Now, we need to define sample statistic instead with respect to a random sample of size
n
(i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to n
independent and identically distributed (I.I.D.) copies of X).

Definition 4.1.1

(Sample mean)
.

Consider a random variable (or a measurement)
X
with unknown distribution. Assume a random sample of size
n
(i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to n
independent and identically distributed (I.I.D.) copies of X), then sample mean is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{n}=\frac{X_{1}+X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n}}{n}.

The sample mean is an estimator of the mean
\mu
of the measurement X and its bias is defined as follows:

\displaystyle Bias(\bar{X})=E(\bar{X})-\mu.

Consider a realization/observation of the random sample of size n,
(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
, then the corresponding realization of the sample mean as
\bar{x}
returns the sample average

\displaystyle\bar{x}=\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}+\ldots+x_{n}}{n}.

Definition 4.1.2

(Sample variance)
.

Consider a random variable (or a measurement)
X
with unknown distribution. Assume a random sample of size
n
(i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to n
independent and identically distributed (I.I.D.) copies of X), then sample variance is defined as follows:

\displaystyle S_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}-\bar{X}_{n}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}-n\bar{X}^{2}\right).

The sample variance is an estimator of the variance
\sigma^{2}
of the measurement X and its bias is defined as follows:

\displaystyle Bias(S^{2})=E(S^{2})-\sigma^{2}.

Consider a realization/observation of the random sample of size
n,(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
, then the corresponding realization of the sample variance as
S^{2}
returns the sample variance

\displaystyle S^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}-\bar{x}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}-n\bar{x}^{2}\right).

Alternatively, we can have another definition of sample variance as follows:

\displaystyle S^{2}=\hat{Var(X)}

\displaystyle=\bar{X^{2}}-\left(\bar{X}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}-\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}-n\bar{X}^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}^{2}-\bar{X}\right)^{2}.

Definition 4.1.3

(Sample standard deviation)
.

In the above definition (
4.1.2
),
S^{2}
is a sample variance of X, so the sample standard deviation is defined as

\displaystyle S=\sqrt{S^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}-\bar{X}_{n}\right)^{2}}.

Further, we have

\displaystyle 0<Var(S^{2})=E(S^{2})-\left(E(S)\right)^{2}=\sigma^{2}-\left(E(S)\right)^{2}\implies E(S)<\sigma.

So, the sample standard deviation
S
is a biased estimator of the standard deviation
\sigma
with a bias

\displaystyle Bias(S)=E(S)-\sigma<0.

Consider a realization/observation of the random sample of size
n,(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
, then the corresponding realization of the sample standard deviation as
S
returns the sample standard error

\displaystyle S=\sqrt{S^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}-\bar{x}\right)^{2}}.

Example 4.1.4

(Average mark in the course MA201)
.

Consider
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4},X_{5}
are the marks in the different evaluation components (e.g. quiz-1,quiz-2, assignment, mid-term, end-term respectively) i.e. we have a random sample of size 5 (upto normalization). Then, the sample mean
\bar{X}=\frac{X_{1}+X_{2}+\ldots+X_{5}}{5}
with
70
realization in section-A and 71 realization in section-B and
\bar{X}
is a unbiased estimator of the average mark in the course MA201.

Example 4.1.5
.

Backdrop: let
T
be the time that is needed for a specific task in a factory to be completed. In order to estimate the mean and variance of
T
, we observe a random sample
(T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{6})
. Thus,
T_{i}
’s are i.i.d. and have the same distribution as
T
.

Question: we obtain the following values (in minutes) as a realization/observation of the random sample of size
6
:

\displaystyle(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4},t_{5},t_{6})=(18,21,17,16,24,20).

Then compute the values of the sample mean, the sample variance, and the sample standard deviation for the observed sample.

Answer: as we have

\displaystyle\bar{T}=\frac{T_{1}+T_{2}+\ldots+T_{6}}{6}\ S^{2}=\frac{1}{5}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{i}^{2}-6\bar{T}^{2}\right)

So, with respect to the realization/observation
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4},t_{5},t_{6})=(18,21,17,16,24,20)
of the random sample of size 6, we have

\displaystyle\bar{t}=\frac{t_{1}+t_{2}+\ldots+t_{6}}{6}\ S^{2}=\frac{1}{5}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}^{2}-6\bar{t}^{2}\right)

So, we have the following computations:

\displaystyle\bar{T}=\bar{t}=19.33,\ S^{2}=8.67,\ S=\sqrt{S^{2}}=2.94.

4.1.1
Methods of estimation for sample statistic

Various methods of estimation for sample statistic such as:

a.

maximum likelihood estimation,

b.

method of moments,

c.

max a-posteriori estimation,

d.

least squared estimation

will be discussed in detail in next chapter (
5
).

4.1.2
Method of sampling

In practice (e.g. in everyday activity or scientific research), our understanding and applicability are based on observation of some relevant samples. For example, a person (with several years of experience with an institute) can often determine an opinion, of an institute that conducts thousands of transactions everyday, by one or two encounters. In other scenario, a traveller who spends 10 days in a country and then proceeds to write a book telling the inhabitants how to revive their industries, reform their political system, balance their budget, and improve the food in their hotels is a familiar figure of fun. But in reality he differs from the political scientist who devotes 20 years to living and studying in the country only in that he bases his conclusions on a much smaller sample of experience and is less likely to be aware of the extent of his ignorance. In every branch of science we lack the resources to "study more than a fragment of the phenomena that might advance our knowledge. A list of the principal advantages of sampling as compared with complete enumeration.

a.

Reduced cost: if data are secured from only a small fraction of the aggregate, expenditures may be expected to be smaller than if a complete census is attempted.

b.

Greater speed: for the same reason, the data can be collected and summarized more quickly with a sample than with a complete
count. This may be a vital consideration when the information is urgently needed.

c.

Greater scope: in certain types of inquiry, highly trained personnel or specialized equipment, limited in availability, must be used to obtain the data. A complete census may then be impracticable: the choice lies between obtaining the information by sampling or not at all. Thus surveys which rely on sampling have more scope and flexibility as to the types of information that can be obtained. On the other hand, if information is wanted for many subdivisions or segments of the population, it may be found that a complete enumeration offers the best solution.

d.

Greater accuracy: because personnel of higher quality can he employed and can be given intensive training, a sample may actually produce more accurate results than the kind of complete enumeration that it is feasible to take.

There are various methods of sampling:

a.

Random sampling

b.

Other sampling

c.

Issues with sampling such as sampling bias.

4.2
Probabilistic inequalities

There are three fundamental probabilistic inequalities:

a.

Markov inequality

b.

Chebushev inequality

c.

Chernoff inequality

4.2.1
Probabilistic inequalities and non-asymptotic sample theory

The mean and variance of a random variable indicate the average value and variability of the outcomes of a repeated experiment. As such, they summarize important information about the distribution (i.e. PMF/PDF). However, they are not sufficient to determine probabilities
of events. For example, consider the PDFs:

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right),\ f_{Y}(y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{2}|y|\right).

both have
E(X)=0=E(Y)
(due to symmetry about origin) and
var(X)=1=Var(Y)
. Yet, the probability of a given interval can be very different. Although the relationship between the mean and variance, and the probability of an event is not a direct one, we can still obtain some information about the probabilities based on the mean and variance. In particular, it is possible to bound the probability or to be able to assert that

\displaystyle P(|X-E(X)|\geq\varepsilon)\leq\delta<1.

For example, if the probability of a speech signal of mean 0 and variance 1 exceeding a given magnitude
\varepsilon
is to be no more than

1\%
, then we would be satisfied if we could determine a
\varepsilon
so that

\displaystyle P(|X-E(X)|\geq\varepsilon)\leq\delta<0.01.

We now show that the probability for the event
|X-E(X)|\geq\varepsilon
can be bounded if we know the mean and variance. Computation of the probability is not required and therefore the distribution does not need to be known. Estimating the mean and variance is much easier than the entire distribution.

Theorem 4.2.1

(Chebyshev inequality)
.

Consider a random variable X, then we have

\displaystyle P(|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon)\leq\frac{Var(X)}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Hence, the probability that a random variable deviates from its mean by more than
\varepsilon
(in either direction) is less than or equal to
\frac{Var(X)}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. This agrees with the intuition that the probability of an outcome departing from the mean must become smaller as the width of the distribution decreases or equivalently as the variance decreases.

Proof 4.2.2
.

We have

\displaystyle Var(X)

\displaystyle=E((X-E(X))^{2})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(x-E(X)\right)^{2}f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon\cup|X-E(X)|\leq\varepsilon}\left(x-E(X)\right)^{2}f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle\geq\int_{|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon}\left(x-E(X)\right)^{2}f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon^{2}\int_{|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon}f_{X}(x)dx=\varepsilon^{2}P(|X-E(X)|\geq\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle P\left(|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{Var(X)}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Theorem 4.2.3

(Chebyshev inequality and confidence interval)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to n
I.I.D. copies of a random variable
X
with unknown distribution and
\mu=E(X),\sigma^{2}=Var(X)<\infty
. Then, for any small positive real number
\varepsilon
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\varepsilon^{2}}\leq\delta\equiv P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|\leq\varepsilon\right)\geq 1-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\varepsilon^{2}}\geq 1-\delta

\displaystyle\implies\bar{X}\in\left[\mu-\sigma\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\delta}},\mu+\sigma\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\delta}}\right]\ \mbox{with high probability}\ 1-\delta.

Example 4.2.4
.

Consider a random variable X with
E(X)=0
and
Var(X)=1
, then for an
\varepsilon
, we have

\displaystyle P(|X|>\varepsilon)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

If
\varepsilon=3
, then

\displaystyle P(|X|>3)\leq\frac{1}{9}\approx 0.11.

This is a rather loose bound as follows:

a.

if
X\sim N(0,1)
, then the actual value of this probability is

\displaystyle P(|X|>3)

\displaystyle=1-P(|X|\leq 3)=1-\left(P(3)-P(-3)\right)

\displaystyle=1-Q(3)+(1+Q(3))=2(1-Q(3))\approx 0.0026.

b.

if
X\sim L(0,1)
, then the actual value of this probability is

\displaystyle P(|X|>3)

\displaystyle=\int_{x<-3\cup x>3}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{2}|x|\right)dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{3}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\exp\left(-\sqrt{2}|x|\right)

\displaystyle=exp\left(-3\sqrt{2}\right)\approx 0.0144.

Hence, the actual probability is indeed less than or equal to the bound of
0.11
, but quite a bit less i.e. the bound provides a gross overestimation of the probability. A graph of the Chebyshev bound as well as the actual probabilities of
P(|X|>\varepsilon)
versus
\varepsilon
is shown in the below figure.

Figure 4.1
:
Probabilities
P(|X|>\varepsilon)
for Gaussian and Laplacian random variables with zero mean and unity variance compared to Ghebyshev inequality.

Theorem 4.2.5

(Markov inequality)
.

Consider a non-negative random variable X, then we have

\displaystyle P(X>\varepsilon)\leq\frac{E(X)}{\varepsilon}.

Furthermore, if X is any random variable and
g
is a non-negative function, then

\displaystyle P(g(X)>\varepsilon)\leq\frac{E(g(X))}{\varepsilon}.

Proof 4.2.6
.

We have

\displaystyle E(g(X))

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(x)f_{X}(x)dx=\int_{\{x:g(x)\geq\varepsilon\cup g(x)<\varepsilon\}}g(x)f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\{x:g(x)\geq\varepsilon\}}g(x)f_{X}(x)dx\geq\varepsilon\int_{\{x:g(x)\geq\varepsilon\}}f_{X}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\varepsilon P(g(X)\geq\varepsilon)\implies P(g(X)>\varepsilon)\leq\frac{E(g(X))}{\varepsilon}.

Example 4.2.7

(Bound for QuickSort run
(
Tsun 2020
)
)
.

Question: Suppose the expected runtime of QuickSort is
2n\ln(n)
operations/comparisons to sort an array of size n (we can show this using linearity of expectation with dependent indicator variables). Use Markov’s inequality to bound the probability that QuickSort runs for longer than
20n\ln(n)
time.

Answer: Let X be the runtime of QuickSort, with
E(X)=2n\ln(n)
. Then, since X is non-negative, we can use Markov’s inequality as:

\displaystyle E(X>20n\ln(n))\leq\frac{E(X)}{20n\ln(n)}=\frac{2n\ln(n)}{20n\ln(n)}=\frac{1}{10}.

So we know there’s at most
10\%
probability that QuickSort takes this long to run. Again, we can get this bound despite not knowing anything except its expectation.

Example 4.2.8

(coin toss)
.

Question: A coin is weighted so that its probability of landing on heads is
20\%
, independently of other flips. Suppose the coin is flipped 20 times. Use Markov’s inequality to bound the probability it lands on heads at least 16 times.

Answer: We actually do know this distribution; the number of heads is
X\sim Bin(n=20,p=0.2)
. Thus,
E(X)=np=20\cdot 0.2=4
. By Markov’s inequality: we have

\displaystyle P(X\geq 16)\leq\frac{E(X)}{16}=\frac{4}{16}=0.25,\ \mbox{but}

\displaystyle P(X\geq 16)=\sum_{k=16}^{20}\binom{n}{k}(0.2)^{k}(0.8)^{20-k}\approx 1.38\times 10^{-8}.

Corollary 4.2.9

(Chebyshev inequality as a corollary of the Markov inequality)
.

Consider a function of
X
as
g(X)=\left(X-E(X)\right)
, then we have

\displaystyle P(|X-E(X(X)|

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon)=P\left((X-E(X))^{2}\geq\varepsilon^{2}\right)\leq\frac{E\left(X-E(X))^{2}\right)}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{Var(X)}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Example 4.2.10

(coin toss)
.

Question: Consider toss of a weighted coin independently with probability of landing heads
p=0.2
. Upper bound the probability it lands on heads at least
16
times out of
20
flips using Chebyshev’s inequality.

Answer: We actually do know this distribution; the number of heads is
X\sim Bin(n=20,p=0.2)
. Thus,
E(X)=np=20\cdot 0.2=4
. and variance
Var(X)=np(1-p)=20\cdot 0.2\cdot 0.8=3.2
. By Chebyshev’s inequality: we have

\displaystyle P(X\geq 16)

\displaystyle=P(X-4\geq 12)\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot P(|X-4|\geq 12)\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{Var(X)}{144}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{3.2}{144}\approx 0.011,\ \mbox{but}\ P(X\geq 16)=\sum_{k=16}^{20}\binom{n}{k}(0.2)^{k}(0.8)^{20-k}

\displaystyle\approx 1.38\times 10^{-8}.

Remark 4.2.11
.

As we are not able to improve Markov’s Inequality and Chebyshev’s Inequality in general, it is worth to consider whether we can say something stronger for a more restricted, yet interesting, class of random variables. This idea brings us to consider the case of a random variable that is the sum of a number of independent random variables.

This scenario is particularly important and ubiquitous in statistical applications. Examples of such random variables are the number of heads in a sequence of coin tosses, or the average support obtained by a political candidate in a poll.

Can Markov’s and Chebyshev’s Inequality be improved for this particular kind of random variable? Before confronting this question, let us check what Chebyshev’s Inequality (the stronger of the two) gives us for a sum of independent random variables.

Consider a sequence of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n},\ldots
(i.e.
\{X_{n}\}
) with
E(X_{i})=\mu_{i}
and
Var(X_{i})=\sigma^{2}_{i}
. Then for any real positive number
\varepsilon
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{Var(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i})}{\varepsilon^{2}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}_{i}}{\varepsilon^{2}}

In particular, for identically distributed random variables with expectation
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle P\left(|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\mu|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\varepsilon^{2}}.

Theorem 4.2.12

(Chernoff inequality)
.

Consider a random variable X, then we have

\displaystyle P(|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Hence, the probability that a random variable deviates from its mean by more than
\varepsilon
(in either direction) is less than or equal to
2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)
. This agrees with the intuition that the probability of an outcome departing from the mean must become smaller as the width of the distribution decreases or equivalently as the variance decreases and the sample size increases.

Remark 4.2.13
.

To improve a point estimation to a high accuracy (i.e. a small confidence interval), we need to improve either assumptions for the tight bound or sample size, because, confidence intervals produced by typical concentration inequalities have size
O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
.

Example 4.2.14

(Chernoff inequality for a sample mean)
.

Consider a random variable X, then we have the Chernoff inequality as:

\displaystyle P(|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Now, consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size n from
X\sim Ber(p)
. Then, the Chernoff inequality for the sample mean is given by

\displaystyle P(|\bar{X}-E(X)|>\varepsilon)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{n\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right).

Theorem 4.2.15

(Chernoff inequality and confidence interval)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a sub-Gaussian random variable
X
(i.e.
M_{X-E(X)}(s)\leq\exp\left(\frac{s^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}\right)
) with unknown distribution and
\mu=E(X),\sigma^{2}=Var(X)
. Then, for any small positive real number
\varepsilon
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|>\varepsilon\right)\leq 2e^{-\frac{n\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\leq\delta\equiv P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|\leq\varepsilon\right)\geq 1-2e^{-\frac{n\varepsilon^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\implies

\displaystyle\bar{X}\in\left[\mu-\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}\log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)},\mu+\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}\log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)}\right]\ \mbox{with high prob.}\ 1-\delta.

Theorem 4.2.16

(Chernoff-Hoeffding inequality)
.

Consider a random variable X that observes values only in
[a,b]
, then we have

\displaystyle P(|X-E(X)|>\varepsilon)\leq 2\exp\left(-2\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{(b-a)^{2}}\right).

Hence, the probability that a random variable deviates from its mean by more than
\varepsilon
(in either direction) is less than or equal to
\frac{Var(X)}{\varepsilon^{2}}
. This agrees with the intuition that the probability of an outcome departing from the mean must become smaller as the width of the distribution decreases or equivalently as the variance decreases.

Theorem 4.2.17

(Chernoff-Hoeffding inequality for sample mean)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable
X
having a unknown distribution supported on
[a,b]
. Then, for any small positive real number
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|>\varepsilon\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2n\varepsilon^{2}}{(b-a)^{2}}\right)\leq\delta.

In particular, when X is supported on
[a,b]
we have

\displaystyle P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|>\varepsilon\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2n\varepsilon^{2}}{(b-a)^{2}}\right)\leq\delta.

Theorem 4.2.18

(Chernoff-Hoeffding inequality and confidence interval)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable
X
having a unknown distribution supported on
[a,b]
. Then, for any small positive real number
\varepsilon
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|>\varepsilon\right)\leq 2e^{-\frac{2n\varepsilon^{2}}{(b-a)^{2}}}\leq\delta\equiv P\left(|\bar{X}-\mu|\leq\varepsilon\right)\geq 1-2e^{-\frac{2n\varepsilon^{2}}{(b-a)^{2}}}\implies

\displaystyle\bar{X}\in\left[\mu-(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{1}{2n}\log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)},\mu+(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{1}{2n}\log\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)}\right]\ \mbox{w. h. p.}\ 1-\delta.

4.2.2
Stochastic convergence and asymptotic large sample theory

Definition 4.2.19

(Convergence in probability)
.

Consider a sequence of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n},\ldots
(i.e.
\{X_{n}\}
). Then,
\{X_{n}\}
converges in probability to a random variable
X
for a small positive real number
\varepsilon
, if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P(|X_{n}-X|>\varepsilon)=0\equiv\lim_{n\to\infty}P(|X_{n}-X|\leq\varepsilon)=1.

This is abbreviated as

\displaystyle X_{n}\to X\equiv plim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}=X.

Example 4.2.20

(Sample variance)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e. n copies of independent and identically distributed random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
of a random X with unknown distribution having mean
\mu=E(X)
and variance
\sigma^{2}=Var(X)
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P(|\bar{X}-E(X)|>\varepsilon)=0\equiv\lim_{n\to\infty}P(|\bar{X}-E(X)|\leq\varepsilon)=1

We have

\displaystyle P(|\bar{X}-E(X)|>\varepsilon)\leq\frac{Var(\bar{X})}{\varepsilon^{2}}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\varepsilon^{2}}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Example 4.2.21
.

Let X be a random variable such that
X_{n}=X+Y_{n}
where
E(Y_{n})=\frac{1}{n}
and
Var(Y_{n})=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}
. Then prove that
X_{n}
converges to X in probability as
n\to\infty
.

Solution: for a random variable X, it is given that

\displaystyle X_{n}=X+Y_{n},\ \mbox{where}\ E(Y_{n})=\frac{1}{n},\ Var(Y_{n})=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}.

Now, we have

\displaystyle|Y_{n}|=|Y_{n}-E(Y_{n})+E(Y_{n})|\leq|Y_{n}-E(Y_{n})|+\frac{1}{n}.

(4.1)

Then, using the above inequality (
4.2.21
) and Chebyshev inequality, we have

\displaystyle P(|X_{n}-X|>\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=P(|Y_{n}|>\varepsilon)\leq P(|Y_{n}-E(Y_{n})|>\varepsilon)\leq\frac{Var(Y_{n})}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\varepsilon^{2}}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Definition 4.2.22

(Convergence in mean square)
.

Consider a sequence of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n},\ldots
(i.e.
\{X_{n}\}
). Then,
\{X_{n}\}
converges in mean square error to a random variable
X
for a small positive real number
\varepsilon
, if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}E((X_{n}-X)^{2})=0.

This is abbreviated as

\displaystyle X_{n}\to X\equiv mselim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}=X.

Remark 4.2.23

(Consistent an estimator in mean square error)
.

The
2-
mean convergence plays a remarkable role in defining a consistent estimator. Consider a sample statistic
\hat{\Theta}=T(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
of a parameter
\theta
. We say that the estimator is consistent if its Mean Squared Error (MSE)

\displaystyle MSE(\hat{\Theta})

\displaystyle=E\left((\hat{\Theta}-\theta)^{2}\right)=E\left(\left(\hat{\Theta}-E(\hat{\Theta})+E(\hat{\Theta})-\theta\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left((\hat{\Theta}-E(\hat{\Theta}))^{2}\right)+\left(E(\hat{\Theta})-\theta\right)^{2}

\displaystyle-2E\left(\hat{\Theta}-E(\hat{\Theta})\right)\left(E(\hat{\Theta})-\theta\right)

\displaystyle=Var(\hat{\Theta})+Bias^{2}(\hat{\Theta})-0\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Definition 4.2.24

(Convergence in distribution)
.

Consider a sequence of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n},\ldots
(i.e.
\{X_{n}\}
). Then,
\{X_{n}\}
converges in distribution to a random variable
X
for a small positive real number
\varepsilon
, if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P(X_{n}\leq x)=P(X\leq x)\equiv lim_{n\to\infty}F_{X_{n}}(x)=F(x)..

This is abbreviated as

\displaystyle X_{n}\to X\equiv dlim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}=X.

Example 4.2.25

(Sample mean)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e. n copies of independent and identically distributed random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
of a random X with unknown distribution having mean
\mu=E(X)
and variance
\sigma^{2}=Var(X)
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P\left(\frac{\bar{X}-\mu}{\sigma}\leq z\right)=\varphi(z).

Definition 4.2.26

(Convergence almost surely)
.

Consider a sequence of random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n},\ldots
(i.e.
\{X_{n}\}
). Then,
\{X_{n}\}
converges almost surely to a random variable
X
if

\displaystyle P\left(\{\omega:\lim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}(\omega)=X(\omega)\}\right).

A probability of outcomes with point-wise convergence.

Remark 4.2.27
.

Let
X_{n}
be a sequence of random variables and X a random variable. Then the following implication diagram is satisfied:

Figure 4.2
:
Relation and implication of various stochastic convergences of a sequence of random variables.

4.3
Limit theorems

Often, our goal is to understand how collections of a large number of independent random variables behave. This is what the laws of large numbers reveal. In general, the idea is that the average of a large number of I.I.D. random variables will approach the expectation. Sometimes that is enough, but usually, we also need to understand how fast does the average converge to the expectation.

4.3.1
Law of large numbers

Theorem 4.3.1

(Strong law of large numbers)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e. n copies of independent and identically distributed random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
of a random X with unknown distribution having mean
\mu=E(X)
and variance
\sigma^{2}=Var(X)
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P(|\bar{X}-E(X)|>\varepsilon)=0\equiv\lim_{n\to\infty}P(|\bar{X}-E(X)|\leq\varepsilon)=1.

Proof 4.3.2
.

Consider the probability of the sample mean random variable deviating from the expected value by more than
\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon
is a small positive number. This probability is given by

\displaystyle P\left(|\bar{X}-E(\bar{X})|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(|\bar{X}-E(X)|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\frac{Var(\bar{X})}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\varepsilon^{2}}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

It says that for large enough n, the probability of the error in the approximation of
\bar{X}
by
E(X)
exceeding
\varepsilon
(which can be chosen as small as desired) will be exceedingly small.

Example 4.3.3

(Bernoulli law of large numbers)
.

Prove that if a fair coin is tossed n times in succession, then the relative frequency of heads, i.e., the number of heads observed divided by the number of coin tosses, should be close to
1/2
. This was why we intuitively accepted the assignment of a probability of 1/2 to the event that the outcome of a fair coin toss would be a head.

Proof 4.3.4
.

Consider coin toss experiment as a sequence of n Bernoulli sub-experiments i.e. a random sample of size n for the experiment i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}),X_{i}\sim Ber(1/2)
.

, Now, all possible relative frequency is given by the sample mean of the random sample of size n

\displaystyle\bar{X}=\frac{X_{1}+X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n}}{n}.

In order to see asymptotic behaviour of the sample mean
\bar{X}
, we compute the following:

\displaystyle E(\bar{X})=E\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}}{n}\right)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(X_{i})}{n}=1/2.

\displaystyle Var(\bar{X})=Var\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}}{n}\right)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}Var(X_{i})}{n^{2}}=\frac{1}{4n}.

Now, as per definition of variance, we have

\displaystyle Var(\bar{X})=E\left((\bar{X}-E(\bar{X}))^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{4n}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty

\displaystyle\implies\bar{X}\to E(\bar{X})=1/2\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Figure 4.3
:
A realization of sample mean random variable of n I.I.D. Bernoulli random variables with
p=1/2
as n increases.

More generally for a Bernoulli sub-experiment with probability p, we have that

\displaystyle Var(\bar{X})=E\left((\bar{X}-E(\bar{X}))^{2}\right)=\frac{p(1-p)}{n}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty

\displaystyle\implies\bar{X}\to E(\bar{X})=p\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Example 4.3.5

(Sample mean for I.I.D. Gaussian random variables)
.

Consider a sequence of n Gaussian sub-experiments i.e. a random sample of size n for the experiment i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}),X_{i}\sim N(\mu,\sigma^{2})
. Then
\bar{X}\to\mu
in probability.

4.3.2
Limit theorems

At an intuitive level, it says that the appropriately scaled sum of a bunch of independent random variables behaves like a Gaussian (AKA Normal) random variable.

Theorem 4.3.6

(Central limit theorem)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e. n copies of independent and identically distributed random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}
of a random X with unknown distribution having mean
\mu=E(X)
and variance
\sigma^{2}=Var(X)
. And, define a standardized sample mean is given by

\displaystyle\bar{Z}_{n}=\frac{\bar{X}_{n}-E(\bar{X}_{n})}{\sqrt{Var(\bar{X})}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-n\mu}{\sqrt{n\sigma^{2}}}.

Then, for any
z
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P(\bar{Z}_{n}\leq z)=P(Z\leq z)=\varphi(z)=\int_{-\infty}^{z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)dx.

In summary,
\bar{Z}_{n}\to Z\sim N(0,1)
in distribution. Furthermore, the Berry-Esséen inequality gives a more precise quantification of the speed of this convergence

\displaystyle\left\|P(\bar{Z}_{n}\leq z)-\int_{-\infty}^{z}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)dx\right\|\leq\frac{0.77E\left(|X_{1}-E(X_{1})|^{3}\right)}{(Var(X_{1}))^{3/2}\sqrt{n}}.

Proof 4.3.7
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e. n I.I.D. copies of X as
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
and the corresponding standardized sample mean is given by

\displaystyle\bar{Z}_{n}=\frac{\bar{X}_{n}-E(X)}{\sqrt{Var(\bar{X}_{n})}}=\frac{n\bar{X}_{n}-nE(X)}{\sqrt{nVar(X)}}=\frac{S_{n}-nE(X)}{\sqrt{nVar(X)}}.

Now, we have

\displaystyle M_{\bar{Z}_{n}}(s)

\displaystyle=E_{\bar{Z}_{n}}(\exp(s\bar{Z}_{n}))=E\left(\exp\left(\frac{s}{\sigma\sqrt{n}}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-n\mu)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=E\left(\exp\left(\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\left(\frac{X_{1}-\mu}{\sigma}\right)+\ldots+\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\left(\frac{X_{n}-\mu}{\sigma}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=E\left(\exp\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{X_{1}-\mu}{\sigma}\right)\right)\cdots E\left(\exp\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{X_{1}-\mu}{\sigma}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(E\left(\exp\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}\right)\right)\right)^{n}=\left(M_{\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}}\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\right)^{n}

\displaystyle\approx\left(M_{\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}}(0)+M_{\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}}^{{}^{\prime}}(0)\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}+M_{\frac{X-\mu}{\sigma}}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(s_{1}/\sqrt{n})\frac{s^{2}}{2n}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle\approx\left(1+\frac{s^{2}}{2n}\right)^{n}\to\exp\left(\frac{s^{2}}{2}\right)\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty\implies\bar{Z}_{n}\xrightarrow{D}Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Example 4.3.8

(
(
Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis 2008
)
)
.

We have 100 bags, with weight distributed uniformly in
[5,50]
, so that the mean weight is
27.5
lbs and the variance is
168.75
lbs. Can we upper bound the probability that the total weight is larger than
3000
lbs?

For comparison, let’s see what happens if we use Chebyshev’s inequality

\displaystyle P(S_{100}>3000)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\times P\left(\left|\frac{1}{100}\sum_{i=1}^{100}X_{i}-\frac{2750}{100}\right|>2.5\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{Var(X)}{100\cdot(2.5)^{2}}\approx 0.13=13\%.

That is a strict inequality, but intuitively it is overestimating the probability by a factor of two since it is also including the case of the average being significantly smaller than the mean as well.

If we use the CLT instead,

\displaystyle P\left(\sum_{i=1}^{100}X_{i}>3000\right)

\displaystyle=1-P\left(\sum_{i=1}^{100}X_{i}\leq 3000\right)

\displaystyle=1-P\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{100}X_{i}-3000}{\sqrt{100\cdot 168.75}}\leq\frac{275}{\sqrt{100\cdot 168.75}}\right)

\displaystyle\approx 1-P\left(Z\leq\frac{275}{\sqrt{100\cdot 168.75}}\right)=0.027.

where Z is a random variable distributed as
N(0,1)
. So the CLT predicts that the probability of having a total weight of over 3000lbs is about
3\%
, which is much lower than the
27\%
bound (or
13\%
if we divide by two) that we got from Chebyshev’s inequality! It turns out that the Normal approximation here is very good even though the CLT is not a bound and
3\%
is not an appropriately low number according to the Berry-Esséen inequality. This is because the CLT is very good for the sums of bounded random variables like the uniform.

Remark 4.3.9

(An inequality for computing tail bounds for Gaussian distribution)
.

In order to use the CLT to get easily calculated bounds, the following approximations will often prove useful: for any
z>0
,

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)}{z\sqrt{2\pi}}\leq\int_{z}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right)dx\leq\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)}{z\sqrt{2\pi}}.

Example 4.3.10
.

Backdrop: Assume a realization/people
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
(of an I.I.D random sample of size n from
X\sim Ber(p)
)vote party A with probability p. We poll n people. Let
M_{n}
be the fraction/average of the polled persons for Party A and defined as

\displaystyle M_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}.

Find n such that estimator
M_{n}
to be within
\pm 1\%
of the true value p with probability at least
95\%
, statistically

\displaystyle P(|M_{n}-p|>0.01)\leq 0.05.

And geometrically, can be visualized as in the figure (
4.4
):

Figure 4.4
:
The distribution
M_{n}-p
is symmetric, and we are looking for tail bounds.

Answer: Since the distribution of
M_{n}
is symmetric around its mean, we can use this symmetry to write
P(|M_{n}-p|>0.01)\approx 2P(M_{n}-p>0.01)
. Because the
X_{i}\sim Ber(p)
, we can bound their variance by
1/4
, so
Var(M_{n})\leq 1/4n
. Dividing both sides by the square root of the variance to put the probability into the CLT form, we get

\displaystyle P(|M_{n}-p|>0.01)\approx 2P(M_{n}-p>0.01)\approx 2P\left(\frac{M_{n}-p}{\sqrt{1/4n}}>\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{1/4n}}\right)\to

\displaystyle 2P\left(Z>\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{1/4n}}\right)\implies P\left(Z>\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{1/4n}}\right)=1-\varphi\left(\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{1/4n}}\right)\leq.025

\displaystyle\implies\varphi\left(\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{1/4n}}\right)\geq 0.9750\implies\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{1/4n}}\geq 1.96\implies n\geq 9604.

In this calculation we used the approximation given by the CLT, but how good is it? If we work out the exact computation, using the fact that
M_{n}
is a scaled binomial and the worse-case parameter
p=1/2
, then we get that
n=9604
will give us a
95.1\%
probability of being within
0.01
of the true value p: in this case, the CLT’s approximation is very good. So if the CLT is so much better, why use Chebyshev’s inequality at all? The reason is that Chebyshev is a bound that works for all
\varepsilon
and probabilities, is always a valid upper bound but is very conservative. The CLT is often much sharper, however it only works for
\varepsilon
of appropriate scale and does not give a rock-solid bound as it is an approximation and not a bound. Is it possible to strengthen Chebyshev’s inequality to get a bound nearly as sharp as the CLT’s, under appropriate assumptions? Yes, the Chernoff inequality provides the optimal approximation.

Remark 4.3.11

(Chernoff inequality and CLT)
.

To get some insight into the CLT by exploring how the value of the KL divergence
D(a\|p)
of a scales when a is in the vicinity of p.

\displaystyle D(a\|p)=E_{a}\left(\ln\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\right)=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sum_{x\in\Omega_{X}}\ln\left(\frac{a(x)}{p(x)}\right)a(x)=\sum_{x\in\Omega_{X}}a(x)\ln(a(x))-\sum_{x\in\Omega_{X}}a(x)\ln(p(x))\\
\int_{x\in\Omega_{X}}\ln\left(\frac{a(x)}{p(x)}\right)a(x)dx=\int_{x\in\Omega_{X}}a(x)\ln(a(x))dx-\int_{x\in\Omega_{X}}a(x)\ln(p(x))dx.\end{array}\right.

KL divergence
D(a\|p))
of a in the vicinity of p is the difference of entropy of a and expected value of
\ln(p)
with respect to a:

\displaystyle D(a\|p)=a\ln(a)+(1-a)\ln(1-a)-a\ln(p)-(1-a)\ln(1-p).

Chapter 5
Statistical methods

In the previous chapter we discuss large sample theory that plays very important role in the statistical inference of statistical methods to be discussed here in details.

5.1
Point estimation

There are various approaches of point estimations such as

(i)

maximum likelihood estimation i.e. MLE

(ii)

method of moments

(iii)

Max a posterior estimation i.e. MAP.

5.1.1
Sample statistic

Consider a random variable (or a measurement)
X
with unknown distribution, then it is very difficult to compute statistic or parameter of the observed values under the unknown distribution. Now, we need to define sample statistic instead with respect to a random sample of size n (i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) copies of X). Then, recalling concepts of sample statistic from subsection (
) of chapter (
4
):

a.

Sample mean:

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{n}=\frac{X_{1}+X_{2}+\ldots+X_{n}}{n}\ \mbox{with a realization}\ \bar{x}=\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}+\ldots+x_{n}}{n}.

b.

Sample variance:

\displaystyle S_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}-\bar{X}_{n}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{2}-n\bar{X}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\ \mbox{with a realization}\ S^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}-\bar{x}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{n-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}-n\bar{x}^{2}\right).

c.

Sample standard deviation:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\sqrt{S^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(X_{i}-\bar{X}_{n}\right)^{2}},\ \mbox{with a realization}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\sqrt{s^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}-\bar{x}\right)^{2}}.

5.1.2
Maximum likelihood estimation

Likelihood function

Definition 5.1.1
.

Consider a realization/data
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
an i.i.d. random sample of size n from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
, where
\theta
is a parameter (or vector of parameters). Now, we define a likelihood of
\mathbb{x}
given
\theta
to be the probability of observing the data
\mathbb{x}
as

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)&\ \mbox{if X is discrete}\\
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}f_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)&\ \mbox{if X is continuous}.\end{array}\right.

Figure 5.1
:
A likelihood function
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})
wrt a probabilistic model
p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
with a unknown parameter
\theta
.

The likelihood function
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})
has the following silent features:

a.

The likelihood function is not a probability density function.

b.

It is an important component of both frequentist and Bayesian analyses.

c.

It measures the support provided by the data for each possible value of the parameter.

If we compare the likelihood function at two parameter points and find that
L(\theta_{1}|\mathbb{x})>L(\theta_{2}|\mathbb{x})
then the realized sample data
\mathbb{x}
, we actually observed, is more likely to have occurred if
\theta=\theta_{1}
than if
\theta=\theta_{2}
. This can be interpreted as
\theta_{1}
is a more plausible value for
\theta
than
\theta_{2}
.

Example 5.1.2

(A Bernoulli model)
.

Question: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=1,x_{2}=0,x_{3}=1)
of an i.i.d. sample of size 3 from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, where
\theta
is the unknown probability of a success. Compute the likelihood function
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})
.

Answer: it is given a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=1,x_{2}=0,x_{3}=1)
of an i.i.d. samples from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, then we have

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{3}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{3}\theta^{x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{1-x_{i}}=\theta^{2}(1-\theta).

Figure 5.2
:
A likelihood function
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=\theta^{2}(1-\theta)
wrt
X\sim Ber(\theta)
with a unknown parameter
\theta
.

Example 5.1.3
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=3,x_{2}=0,x_{3}=2,x_{4}=7)
of an I.I.D. sample of size 4 from
X\sim Pois(\theta)
, where
\theta
is the historical average number of events in a unit of time).

Answer: it is given a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=1,x_{2}=0,x_{3}=1)
of an I.I.D. samples from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, then we have

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{4}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{4}e^{\theta}\frac{\theta^{x_{i}}}{(x_{i})!}=e^{-4\theta}\frac{\theta^{12}}{2!3!7!}.

Maximum likelihood estimation

Definition 5.1.4

(Maximum likelihood estimate)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size n from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
, where
\theta
is a parameter (or vector of parameters). Then, the maximum likelihood estimate
\hat{\theta}_{MLE}
of
\theta
to be the parameter which maximizes the likelihood (or equivalently, the log-likelihood) of the data as:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\arg\max_{\theta}L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=\arg\max_{\theta)}ln\left(L(\theta|\mathbb{x})\right).

Example 5.1.5

(Bernoulli model)
.

Question: consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=1,x_{2}=1,x_{3}=1,x_{4}=1,x_{5}=0)
of an I.I.D. sample of size 4 from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
distribution with unknown parameter
\theta
. Find the maximum likelihood estimate
\hat{\theta}
of
\theta
.

We have the likelihood function with respect to the given realization of the sample as

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i=1}^{5}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{5}\theta^{x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{1-x_{i}}=\theta^{4}(1-\theta).

The plot of the likelihood with
\theta
on the x-axis and
L(HHHHT|\theta)
on the y-axis in (
5.3
) as:

Figure 5.3
:
A likelihood function with the Bernoulli model for unknown parameter
\theta
(the probability of heads).

and we can actually see the
\theta
which maximizes the likelihood is
\hat{\theta}=4/5
. But sometimes it is not possible to plot the likelihood, so we solve it analytically now.

To find the
\theta
which maximizes this likelihood, so we take the derivative with respect to
\theta
and set it to 0:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}(L(\theta|\mathbb{x}))=\frac{d}{d\theta}(\theta^{4}(1-\theta))=\theta^{3}(4-5\theta)=0\implies\theta_{opt}=4/5.

So, in MLE, we’re trying to find the
\theta
that maximizes the likelihood, and we don’t care what the maximum value of the likelihood is. We didn’t even compute it. We just care that the
\arg\max_{\theta}L(\theta|x)
is
4/5
.

Remark 5.1.6

(Likelihood vs log-likelihood)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size
n
from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
, where
\theta
is a parameter (or vector of parameters). The log-likelihood of x given
\theta
are defined as:

\displaystyle ln\left(L(\theta|x\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sum_{i=1}^{n}ln\left(p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta\right)&\ \mbox{X is discrete}\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}ln\left(f_{X}(x_{i}|\theta\right)&\ \mbox{X is continuous}.\end{array}\right..

Since,
\ln(\cdot)
is a monotone increasing function, so it preserves order of the argument, so whatever was the maximizer (
\arg\max
) in the original function, will also be maximizer in the log function. For example, we can see in the following picture

Figure 5.4
:
The values are different in y-axis, but if you look at the x-axis, it happens that both functions are maximized at
1
i.e. the
\arg\max
’s are the same.

Example 5.1.7
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=1,x_{2}=0,x_{3}=1)
of an I.I.D. sample of size
n=3
from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
.Compute the MLE estimate of
\theta
.

Answer: Since
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, so we have
p_{X}(x|\lambda)=\lambda^{x}(1-\lambda)^{1-x}
). Now, likelihood function is defined as

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{1-x_{i}}.

Taking log both sides, we get

\displaystyle\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}\ln(\theta)+(1-x_{i})\ln(1-\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\ln(\theta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}+\ln(1-\theta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i}).

Take the derivative(s) with respect to
\theta
and set to 0, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}\left(\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\frac{1}{1-\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta(1-\theta)}\left((1-\theta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\theta\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta(1-\theta)}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-n\theta\right)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}\left(\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\theta^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}-\frac{1}{(1-\theta)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-x_{i})

Equating the derivative of
\ln(L(\theta|x))
to 0, we get

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}\ \mbox{with}\ \frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}\left(\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)\right)\leq 0.\ \mbox{So,}\ \hat{\Theta}=\bar{X}.

Example 5.1.8

(Poisson model)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. sample of size n from
X\sim Pois(\theta)
. Compute the MLE estimate of
\theta
.

Answer: Since
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, so we have
p_{X}(x|\theta)=e^{-\theta}\frac{\theta^{x}}{x!}
). Now, likelihood function is defined as:

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\theta}\frac{\theta^{x_{i}}}{(x_{i})!}.

Now, taking log sides, we get

\displaystyle\ln\left(L(x|\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln\left(p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}-\theta+x_{i}\ln(\theta)-\ln((x_{i})!).

Take the derivative(s) with respect to
\theta
and set to 0, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-1+\frac{x_{i}}{\theta}\right)=0\implies-n+\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}=0\implies

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}\ \mbox{with}\ \frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\frac{x_{i}}{\theta^{2}}\right)\leq 0.\ \mbox{So,}\ \hat{\Theta}=\bar{X}.

Example 5.1.9
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. sample of size
n
from
X\sim Exp(\theta)
. Compute the MLE estimate of
\theta
.

Answer: Since
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, so we have
p_{X}(x|\theta)=\theta e^{-x\theta}
). Now, likelihood function is defined as:

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\theta e^{-x_{i}\theta}.

Now, taking log sides, we get

\displaystyle\ln\left(L(x|\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln\left(p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}-x_{i}\theta+\ln(\theta).

Take the derivative(s) with respect to
\theta
and set to 0, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-x_{i}+\frac{1}{\theta}\right)=0\implies\frac{n}{\theta}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=0\implies

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}\ \mbox{with}\ \frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}\ln\left(L(\theta|x)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}\right)\leq 0.\ \mbox{So,}\ \hat{\Theta}=\bar{X}.

Example 5.1.10
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. sample of size
n
from
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
. Compute the MLE estimate of
\theta
.

Answer: Since
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, so we have
p_{X}(x|\theta)=\frac{1}{\theta}I_{0\leq x\leq\theta}
). Now, likelihood function is defined as:

\displaystyle L(x|\theta)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=p_{X}(x_{1}|\theta)\cdot p_{X}(x_{2}|\theta)\cdots p_{X}(x_{n}|\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta}I_{\{0\leq x_{1}\leq\theta\}}\cdot\frac{1}{\theta}I_{\{0\leq x_{2}\leq\theta\}}\cdots\frac{1}{\theta}I_{\{0\leq x_{n}\leq\theta\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}I_{\{0\leq x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\leq\theta\}}.

Figure 5.5
:
The first (left) plot is graph of
\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}
, and the second (right) plot of the actual likelihood function
\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}I_{\{0\leq x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\leq\theta\}}\equiv\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}I_{\{x_{max}\leq\theta\}}
.

Take the derivative(s) with respect to
\theta
and set to 0, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}L(\theta|x)=\frac{-n}{\theta^{n+1}}I_{\{0\leq x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\leq\theta\}}=0\implies\hat{\theta}=\max_{\{i=1,2,\ldots,n\}}x_{i}

\displaystyle=x_{max}\ \mbox{with}\ \frac{d^{2}}{d\theta^{2}}L(\theta|x)=\frac{n(n+1)}{\theta^{n+2}}I_{\{0\leq x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\leq\theta\}}\geq 0.

\displaystyle\ \mbox{So,}\ \hat{\Theta}=max_{\{i=1,2,\ldots,n\}}X_{i}=X_{max}.

5.1.3
Method of moments

In the last sub-section, we have seen maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) had a nice intuition but mathematically is a bit tedious to solve. So, we’ll learn an another technique for estimating parameters called the Method of Moments (MoM) with several examples which hopefully makes things clearer.

Definition 5.1.11

(kth moment)
.

Let X be a random variable and
c\in\mathbb{R}
be a scalar. Then: (a.) the kth moment of X and (b.) the kth moment of X (about c) are defined as:

\displaystyle(a.)\ E(X^{k})\ \mbox{and}\ (b.)\ E((X-c)^{k})\ \mbox{respectively}.

Usually, we are interested in the first moment of X:
\mu=E(X)
, and the second moment of X about
\mu
:
Var(X)=E((X-\mu)^{2})
.

Definition 5.1.12

(kth sample moment)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size
n
from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
with unknown parameter
\theta
and
c\in\mathbb{R}
be a scalar. Then: (a.) the kth sample moment of X and (b.) the kth sample moment of X (about c) are defined as:

\displaystyle(a.)\ \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k}\ \mbox{and}\ (b.)\ \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X-c)^{k}\ \mbox{respectively}.

Usually, we are interested in the first sample moment of X:
\bar{x}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}
, and the second sample moment of X about
\mu
:
S^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}
.

Example 5.1.13
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample size
n
from
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
, where
\theta
is a unknown parameter. Compute MoM estimator of
\theta
.

Answer: since
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, so we have
p_{X}(x|\theta)=\frac{1}{\theta}I_{0\leq x\leq\theta}
), so
E(X)=\frac{\theta}{2}
. Then, from first moment approximation, we have

\displaystyle E(X)=\frac{\theta}{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\implies\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}.

It can be observed that

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\neq\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=x_{max}.

And the MoM estimator of
\theta
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}=2\bar{X}.

Example 5.1.14
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. sample of size
n
from
X\sim Exp(\theta)
. Compute the MoM estimator of
\theta
.

Answer: since
X\sim Exp(\theta)
, so we have
f_{X}(x|\theta)=\theta e^{-x\theta}
), so
E(X)=\frac{1}{\theta}
. Then, from first moment approximation, we have
We set the first true moment to the first sample moment as follows (recall that
X\sim Exp(\lambda),E(X)=\frac{1}{\lambda}
):

\displaystyle E(X)=\frac{1}{\theta}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\implies\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}=\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{\bar{x}}.

So, the MoM estimator of the
\theta
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}}=\frac{1}{\bar{X}}.

Example 5.1.15
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample size
n
from
X\sim Pois(\theta)
. Compute MoM estimator of
\theta
.

Answer: since
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, so we have
p_{X}(x|\theta)=\frac{1}{\theta}I_{0\leq x\leq\theta}
), so
E(X)=\theta
. Then, from first moment approximation, we have

\displaystyle E(X)=\theta=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\implies\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}.

Notice that in this case, the MoM estimate disagrees with the MLE as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\bar{x}.

So, the MoM estimator of
\theta
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}=\bar{X}.

5.1.4
Max a posterior estimation

In order to begin with Bayesian estimation, it requires us to learn at least few special distributions (e.g. Beta distribution) especially meant for prior distribution assumption.

The Beta Random Variable

In order to proceed in Bayesian framework, it is very much essential to define few useful distributions to be utilized for prior distribution of the unknown parameter
\theta
.

Definition 5.1.16

(Beta distribution
(
Tsun 2020
)
)
.

A random variable X is having beta distribution i.e.
X\sim Beta(\alpha,\beta)
, if and only if X has the following density function (and range
\Omega_{X}=[0,1]
):

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{B(\alpha,\beta)}x^{\alpha-1}(1-x)^{\beta-1}&\ x\in[0,1],\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

X is typically the belief distribution about some unknown probability of success, where we pretend we’ve seen
\alpha-1
successes and
\beta-1
failures. Hence the mode (most likely value of the probability/point with highest density)
\arg\max_{x\in[0,1]}f_{X}(x)
, is

\displaystyle mode(X)=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha-1+\beta-1}.

Also note the following:

a.

The first term in the pdf,
\frac{1}{B(\alpha,\beta)}
is just a normalizing constant (ensures the pdf to integrates to
1). It is called the Beta function, and so our random variable is named as a Beta random variable.

b.

There is an annoying off-by-1 issue: (
\alpha-1
heads and
\beta-1
tails), so when choosing these parameters, be careful.

c.

x is the probability of success, and
(1-x)
is the probability of failure.

Example 5.1.17

(
(
Tsun 2020
)
)
.

If you flip a coin with unknown probability of heads X, identify the parameters of the most appropriate Beta distribution to model your belief:

a.

You didn’t observe anything (i.e.
\alpha=\#heads+1=0+1=1,\beta=\#tails+1=0+1=1
)?

It’s
B(0+1,0+1)\equiv B(1,1)\equiv Unif(0,1)\to
NO mode (because it follows the Uniform distribution, every point has same density).

b.

You observed 8 heads and 2 tails?

It’s
B(8+1,2+1)\equiv B(9,3)\to
mode(X) =
\frac{9-1}{9-1+3-1}=\frac{8}{10}=0.8
.

c.

You observed 80 heads and 20 tails?

It’s
B(80+1,20+1)\equiv B(81,21)\to
mode(X) =
\frac{81-1}{81-1+21-1}=\frac{80}{100}=0.8
.

d.

You observed 2 heads and 3 tails?

It’s
B(2+1,3+1)\equiv B(3,4)\to
mode(X) =
\frac{3-1}{3-1+4-1}=\frac{2}{5}=0.4
.

The Dirichlet random vector generalizes the Beta random variable to having a belief distribution over
(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{r})
(like in the multinomial distribution so
\sum_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}=1
), and has r parameters
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
. It has the similar interpretation of pretending you’ve seen
\alpha_{i}-1
outcomes of type i.

Definition 5.1.18

(Dirichlet distribution)
.

A random variable X is having Dirichlet distribution i.e.
X\sim Dir(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
, if and only if X has the following density function (and range
\Omega_{X}=[0,1]
):

\displaystyle f_{X}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{B(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{r})}\Pi_{i=1}^{r}x^{\alpha_{i}-1}&\ x_{i}\in[0,1],\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}=1\\
0&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

This is a generalization of the Beta random variable from 2 outcomes to r. X is typically the belief distribution about some unknown probability of different outcomes, where we pretend we’ve seen
\alpha_{1}-1
outcomes of type 1,
\alpha_{2}-1
outcomes of type 2
\ldots
,
\alpha_{r}-1
outcomes of type r. Hence the mode (most likely value of the probability/point with highest density)
\arg\max_{x_{i}\in[0,1],\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}=1}f_{X}(x)
, is

\displaystyle mode(X)=\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\alpha_{i}-1)},\frac{\alpha_{2}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\alpha_{i}-1)},\ldots,\frac{\alpha_{r}-1}{\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\alpha_{i}-1)}\right).

Also note the following:

a.

Similar to the Beta RV, the first term in the pdf,
\frac{1}{B(\alpha)}
is just a normalizing constant (ensures the pdf to integrates to 1), where
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{r})
.

b.

Notice that this is the probability distribution over the random vector
x_{i}
’s, which is the vector of probabilities, so they must sum to 1 i.e.
\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}=1
.

MAP

Definition 5.1.19

(MAP)
.

Consider a realization/data
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. sample of size
n
from probability mass function
p_{X}(x:\Theta=\theta)
(if X discrete), or from density
f_{X}(x;\Theta=\theta)
(if X continuous), where
\Theta
is the random variable representing the
parameter (or vector of parameters). We define the Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) estimate
\hat{\theta}_{MAP}
of
\theta
as

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MAP}=\arg\max_{\theta}\pi_{\Theta}(\theta|x)=\arg\max_{\theta}\pi_{\Theta}(\theta)L_{\Theta}(\theta|x).

That is, it’s exactly the same as maximum likelihood, except instead of just maximizing the likelihood, we are maximizing the likelihood multiplied by the prior.

Example 5.1.20

(uniform prior distribution and
\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\hat{\theta}_{MAP}
)
.

Question: consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(0,0,1,1,0)
of an I.I.D. random sample of size 5 from
X\sim Ber(\Theta)
, where
\Theta\in\{0.2,0.5,0.7\}
. Compute the (a.)
\hat{\theta}_{MLE}
and
\hat{\theta}_{MAP}
with respect to prior distribution
\pi_{\Theta}(\theta)=1/3
for
\theta\in\{0.2,0.5,0.7\}
.

Solution: it is given a realization
\mathbb{x}=(0,0,1,1,0)
of an i.i.d. sample of size 5 from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, then we have

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{5}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{5}\theta^{x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{1-x_{i}}=\theta^{2}(1-\theta)^{3}.

Then,

\displaystyle L(0.2|x)

\displaystyle=P(x|0.2)=(0.2)^{2}\cdot(0.8)^{3}=0.02048,

\displaystyle L(0.5|x)

\displaystyle=P(x|0.5)=(0.5)^{2}\cdot(0.5)^{3}=0.03125,

\displaystyle L(0.7|x)

\displaystyle=P(x|0.7)=(0.7)^{2}\cdot(0.3)^{3}=0.01323.

So,
\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\arg\max_{\theta\in\{0.2,0.5,0.7\}}L(\theta|x)=0.5
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\pi_{\Theta}(0.2)L(0.2|x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\cdot(0.2)^{2}\cdot(0.8)^{3}\approx 0.00683,

\displaystyle\pi_{\Theta}(0.5)L(0.5|x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\cdot(0.5)^{2}\cdot(0.5)^{3}\approx 0.01042,

\displaystyle\pi_{\Theta}(0.7)L(0.7|x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\cdot(0.7)\cdot(0.3)^{3}=0.00441.

So,
\hat{\theta}_{MAP}=\arg\max_{\theta\in\{0.2,0.5,0.7\}}\pi_{\Theta}(\theta)L(\theta|x)=0.5
. i.e. with a uniform prior distribution of
\Theta
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=0.5=\hat{\theta}_{MAP}.

Example 5.1.21

(Non-uniform prior distribution and
\hat{\theta}_{MLE}\neq\hat{\theta}_{MAP}
)
.

Question: consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=0,x_{2}=0,x_{3}=1,x_{4}=0)
of an I.I.D. random sample of size 4 from
X\sim Ber(\Theta)
, where
\Theta\in\{0.2,0.5,0.7\}
with a prior distribution
p_{\Theta}(0.2)=0.10,p_{\Theta}(0.5)=0.01
and
p_{\Theta}(0.7)=0.89
. Compute the (a.)
\hat{\theta}_{MLE}
and
\hat{\theta}_{MAP}
.

Solution: it is given a realization
\mathbb{x}=(0,0,1,0)
of an i.i.d. sample of size 4 from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, then we have

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{4}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{4}\theta^{x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{1-x_{i}}=\theta(1-\theta)^{3}.

Then,

\displaystyle L(0.2|x)

\displaystyle=P(x|0.2)=(0.2)\cdot(0.8)^{3}=0.01024,

\displaystyle L(0.5|x)

\displaystyle=P(x|0.5)=(0.5)\cdot(0.5)^{3}=0.06250,

\displaystyle L(0.7|x)

\displaystyle=P(x|0.7)=(0.7)\cdot(0.3)^{3}=0.01890.

So,
\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\arg\max_{\theta\in\{0.2,0.5,0.7\}}L(\theta|x)=0.5
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\pi_{\Theta}(0.2)L(0.2|x)

\displaystyle=0.10\cdot(0.2)\cdot(0.8)^{3}=0.001024,

\displaystyle\pi_{\Theta}(0.5)L(0.5|x)

\displaystyle=0.01\cdot(0.5)\cdot(0.5)^{3}\approx 0.000625,

\displaystyle\pi_{\Theta}(0.7)L(0.7|x)

\displaystyle=0.89\cdot(0.7)\cdot(0.3)^{3}=0.16821.

So,
\hat{\theta}_{MAP}=\arg\max_{\theta\in\{0.2,0.5,0.7\}}\pi_{\Theta}(\theta)L(\theta|x)=0.7
. i.e. with a non-uniform prior distribution of
\Theta
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=0.5\neq 0.7=\hat{\theta}_{MAP}.

5.1.5
Goodness of fit

In this section, we have discussed various estimation techniques to find an estimate of a unknown parameter that leads to an estimator or sample statistic of the corresponding parameter and runs over all possible realizations/observations of an I.I.D. random sample of size n. Now, we need to find out which is relatively best i.e. how can we determine which estimator is better (rather than the technique)? There are even more different ways to estimate besides MLE/MoM/MAP, and in different scenarios, different techniques may work better. Here, we will consider some properties of estimators that allow us to compare their goodness.

Bias

Once, we come up with an estimator of a parameter using some estimation technique, we need to measure the estimator whether or not in expectation equals to the true parameter and the measure is known as bias of the estimator.

Definition 5.1.22

(Bias)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X with unknown but parametrized distribution
p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
. Suppose
\hat{\Theta}
be an estimator of the unknown (but fixed) parameter
\theta
under a suitable estimation technique. Then, bias of the estimator
\hat{\Theta}
for
\theta
is defined as

\displaystyle Bias(\hat{\Theta})=E\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)-\theta.

Now, we have

a.

If
Bias(\hat{\Theta})=0\equiv E\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)=\theta
, then we say
\hat{\Theta}
is an unbiased estimator of
\theta
.

b.

If
Bias(\hat{\Theta})>0
, then
\hat{\Theta}
typically overestimates
\theta
.

c.

If
Bias(\hat{\Theta})<0
, then
\hat{\Theta}
typically underestimates
\theta
.

Example 5.1.23

(Sample mean)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
Pois(\theta)
, then the MLE and MoM both estimate the same given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}=\hat{\theta}_{MoM}\implies\hat{\Theta}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}=\bar{X}.

Question: Prove that the estimator (or the sample mean),
\hat{\Theta}=\bar{X}
, is a unbiased estimator of the
\theta
(or the unknown mean).

Solution: We have

\displaystyle E\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)=E\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(X_{i})=\theta.

This makes sense: the average of your samples should be on-target for the true average.

Example 5.1.24

(Sample statistic for
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
, then the MLE and MoM both estimate the same given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=x_{max},\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=2\bar{x}\implies\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=X_{max},\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}=2\bar{X}.

Question: Sure,
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}
maximizes the likelihood, so in a way
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}
is better than
\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}
. But, what are the biases of these estimators? Before doing any computation: do you think
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}
and
\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}
are overestimates, underestimates, or unbiased?

Solution: Actually, it can be considered that
\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}
is spot-on since the average of the samples should be close to
\frac{\theta}{2}
and multiplying by 2 would seem to give the true
\theta
. On the other hand,
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}
might be a bit of an underestimate, since we probably wouldn’t have
\theta
be exactly the largest (maybe a little larger).

a.

Computation of
Bias(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE})
wrt
\theta
: we have

\displaystyle f_{\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=f_{X_{max}}(x)=\frac{d}{dx}\left(F_{X_{max}}(x)\right)=\frac{d}{dx}\left(P(X_{max}\leq x)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dx}\left(P(X_{1}\leq x,X_{2}\leq x,\ldots X_{n}\leq x)\right)=\frac{d}{dx}\left((F_{X}(x))^{n}\right)

\displaystyle=n(F_{X}(x))^{n-1}f_{X}(x)=n\left(\frac{x}{\theta}\right)^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}\right).

Then

\displaystyle E\left(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}\right)

\displaystyle=E(X_{max})=\int_{0}^{\theta}xn\left(\frac{x}{\theta}\right)^{n-1}\left(\frac{1}{\theta}\right)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\theta^{n}}\int_{0}^{\theta}x^{n}dx=\frac{n}{n+1}\theta.

So, we have

\displaystyle Bias\left(E\left(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}\right)\right)=E\left(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}\right)-\theta=\frac{n}{n+1}\theta-\theta=\frac{-\theta}{n+1}<0.

This makes sense because if we had 3 sample points from
Unif(0,1)
for example, we would expect them at
1/4,2/4,3/4
, and so it would be
n/(n+1)
as our expected max. Similarly, if we had 4 sample points, then we would expect them at
1/5,2/5,3/5,4/5
, and so it would again be
n/(n+1)
as our expected max.

b.

Computation of
Bias\left(\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}\right)
wrt
\theta
: we have

\displaystyle E\left(\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left(\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right)=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(X_{i})=\frac{2}{n}\cdot\frac{\theta}{2}=\theta.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1}=1,x_{2}=9,x_{3}=2)
of an I.I.D. sample of size 3 from
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
, then we have the following estimates

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=x_{max}=9,\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{2}{3}\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}=\frac{2}{3}\cdot(1+9+2)=8.

However, based on our realization of the sample, the MoM estimator is impossible. If the actual parameter were 8, then that means that the distribution we pulled the sample from is
Unif(0,8)
, in which case the likelihood that we get a
9
is 0. But we did see a 9 in our sample. So, even though
\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}
is unbiased, it still yields an impossible estimate. This just goes to show that finding the right estimator is actually quite tricky.

A good solution would be to de-bias the MLE by scaling it appropriately. If we decided to have a new estimator based on the MLE:

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}=\frac{n+1}{n}\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=\frac{n+1}{n}X_{max}.

Now, we have an unbiased estimator, but now it does not maximize the likelihood any more. Actually, the MLE is what we say to be asymptotically unbiased as follows:

\displaystyle Bias\left(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}\right)=E\left(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}\right)-\theta=\frac{n}{n+1}\theta-\theta=\frac{-\theta}{n+1}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Example 5.1.25

(A reciprocal of sample mean)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
Exp(\theta)
, then the MLE and MoM both estimate the same given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}=\frac{1}{\bar{x}}=\hat{\theta}_{MoM}\implies\hat{\Theta}=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}}=\frac{1}{\bar{X}}.

Question: Prove that the estimator,
\hat{\Theta}=\frac{1}{\bar{X}}
, is a biased estimator of the
\theta
(or the unknown parameter).

Solution: We have

\displaystyle E\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)=E\left(\frac{1}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{E\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right)}=\frac{1}{\theta}.

The function
g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\frac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}
is convex (at least in the positive octant when all
x_{i}\geq 0
) since
\frac{1}{x}
is a convex function. Then, Jensen’s inequality says that
E(g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))\geq g(E(x_{1}),E(x_{2}),\ldots,E(x_{n}))
because of convexity. So
E\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)\geq\theta
systematically, and we typically have an overestimate.

Variance and mean squared error

We are often also interested in accuracy of an estimator i.e. how much an estimator varies (we would like it to be unbiased and have small variance to that it is more accurate). There is a metric namely mean squared error that captures this property of estimators and defined as below.

Definition 5.1.26

(Mean squared error of an estimator)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X with unknown but parametrized distribution
p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
. Suppose
\hat{\Theta}
be an estimator of the unknown (but fixed) parameter
\theta
under a suitable estimation technique. Then, mean squared error of the estimator
\hat{\Theta}
for
\theta
is defined as

\displaystyle MSE(\hat{\Theta})=E\left(\left(\hat{\Theta}-\theta\right)^{2}\right)=E\left(\left(\hat{\Theta}-E(\hat{\Theta})+E(\hat{\Theta})-\theta\right)^{2}\right)

We have

\displaystyle MSE(\hat{\Theta})=E\left(\left(\hat{\Theta}-\theta\right)^{2}\right)=E\left(\left(\hat{\Theta}-E(\hat{\Theta})+E(\hat{\Theta})-\theta\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left(\left(\hat{\Theta}-E(\hat{\Theta})\right)^{2}\right)+\left(E(\hat{\Theta})-\theta\right)^{2}+2E\left(\hat{\Theta}-E(\hat{\Theta})\right)\left(E(\hat{\Theta})-\theta\right)

\displaystyle=Var\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)+Bias^{2}\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)+0=Var\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)+Bias^{2}\left(\hat{\Theta}\right).

Variance-Bias trade-off: Usually, we want to minimize MSE, and these two quantities are often inversely related. That is, decreasing one leads to an increase in the other, and finding the balance will minimize the MSE i.e.a small MSE often involves a trade-off between
variance and bias. For unbiased estimators,
MSE(\hat{\Theta})=Var(\hat{\Theta})
, so no trade-off can be made..

Example 5.1.27

(MSE of sample mean)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
Pois(\theta)
, then the MLE and MoM both estimate the same given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}=\hat{\theta}_{MoM}\implies\hat{\Theta}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}=\bar{X}.

Question: Compute the mean squared error of the estimator (or the sample mean),
\hat{\Theta}=\bar{X}
, with respect to the unknown parameter
\theta
(or the unknown mean).

Solution: We have

\displaystyle E\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)=E\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(X_{i})=\theta\implies Bias(\hat{\Theta})=0.

And

\displaystyle Var(\hat{\Theta})=Var(\bar{X})=\frac{Var(X)}{n}=\frac{\theta}{n}.

So, mean squared error of
\hat{\Theta}
is given by

\displaystyle MSE(\hat{\Theta})=E\left(\left(\right)^{2}\right)=Var(\hat{\Theta})+Bias^{2}(\hat{\Theta})=\frac{\theta}{n}.

Consistency of an estimator

Consistency of an estimator means that as the sample size gets large the estimate gets closer and closer to the true value of the parameter.

Definition 5.1.28

(Consistency of an estimator)
.

Consider a random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X with unknown but parametrized distribution
p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
. Suppose
\hat{\Theta}
be an estimator of the unknown (but fixed) parameter
\theta
under a suitable estimation technique. Then, the estimator
\hat{\Theta}
is consistent if
\hat{\Theta}
converges in probability to
\theta
. That is, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P\left(|\hat{\Theta}-\theta|>\varepsilon\right)=0\equiv\lim_{n\to\infty}P\left(|\hat{\Theta}-\theta|\leq\varepsilon\right)=1.

Also, the estimator
\hat{\Theta}
is consistent in mean squared error i.e.
\hat{\Theta}
converges in mean squared error to
\theta
if

\displaystyle MSE(\hat{\Theta})=Var(\hat{\Theta})+Bias^{2}(\hat{\Theta}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Example 5.1.29

(Sample statistic for
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
, then the MLE and MoM both estimate the same given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=x_{max},\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=2\bar{x}\implies\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=X_{max},\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}=2\bar{X}.

Question: Prove that
\hat{\Theta}
is a consistent estimator of
\theta
.

Answer:

a.

For unbiased estimator
\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}
and
\varepsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle P\left(|\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}-\theta|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(|\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}-E(\hat{\Theta}_{MoM})|>\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Var(\hat{\Theta}_{MoM})}{\varepsilon^{2}}=\frac{Var(2\bar{X})}{\varepsilon^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4Var(X)}{n\varepsilon^{2}}\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

b.

For biased estimator
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}
, we cannot use Chebyshev’s inequality unfortunately. For computation of
Bias(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE})
wrt
\theta
: we have

\displaystyle F_{\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=F_{X_{max}}(x)=P(X_{max}\leq x)=P(X_{1}\leq x,X_{2}\leq x,\ldots X_{n}\leq x)

\displaystyle=(F_{X}(x))^{n}=\left(\frac{x}{\theta}\right)^{n}.

Now, we have

\displaystyle P(|\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}-\theta|>\varepsilon)=P(\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}<\theta-\varepsilon)+P(\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}>\theta+\varepsilon)=

\displaystyle P(\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}<\theta-\varepsilon)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(\frac{\theta-\varepsilon}{\theta}\right)^{n}&\varepsilon<\theta\ \mbox{i.e.}\ \theta-\varepsilon>0\\
0&\varepsilon\geq\theta\ \mbox{i.e.}\ \theta-\varepsilon\leq 0.\end{array}\right.\to 0\ \mbox{as}\ n\to\infty.

Now we’ve seen that, even though the MLE and MoM estimators of
\theta
for an i.i.d. sample of size n from
Unif(0,\theta)
are different, they are both consistent. Furthermore for
Unif(0,\theta)

a.

\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}
is an unbiased and consistent estimator of
\theta
for an I.I.D. sample of size n from
Unif(0,\theta)
meaning it is correct in expectation, also converges to the true parameter (consistent) since the variance goes to 0.

b.

However, if we ignore all the sample points and just take the first one and multiply it by 2,
\hat{\Theta}=2X_{1}
, it is
unbiased (as
E(\hat{\Theta})=E(2X_{1})=2E(X_{1})=2\cdot\frac{\theta}{2}=\theta
), but it’s not consistent i.e. the estimator doesn’t get better and better with more n because we’re not using all n sample points. Consistency requires that as we get more samples, we approach
the true parameter.

c.

Biased but consistent, on the other hand, we established that the MLE estimator
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}
(with expectation
E(\hat{\Theta}_{MLE})=\frac{n}{n+1}\theta\to\theta
as
n\to\infty
) is biased but consistent.

d.

\hat{\Theta}=\frac{1}{\bar{X}^{2}}
is neither unbiased nor consistent would just be some random expression.

Efficiency

Efficiency says that the estimator has as low variance as possible. This property combined with consistency and unbiasedness make our estimator is on target (unbiased).

Definition 5.1.30
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X with unknown but parametrized distribution
p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
. Then, define the following

a.

Score function:

\displaystyle s(\mathbb{x})=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(\ln(f_{X}(x|\theta))\right)=\frac{f_{X}^{{}^{\prime}}(x|\theta)}{f_{X}(x|\theta)}.

Then, expectation of score statistic is given by

\displaystyle E(S(\theta))

\displaystyle=E\left(\frac{f_{X}^{{}^{\prime}}(x|\theta)}{f_{X}(x|\theta)}\right)=\int\frac{f_{X}^{{}^{\prime}}(x|\theta)}{f_{X}(x|\theta)}f_{X}(x|\theta^{*})dx
