\displaystyle\approx\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\int f_{X}(x|\theta^{*})dx=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}(1)=0.

b.

Fisher information matrix:

\displaystyle I(\theta)=E\left((S(\theta)-E(S(\theta)))(S(\theta)-E(S(\theta))^{\top}\right)=E\left(S(\theta)S^{\top}(\theta)\right)=

\displaystyle nE\left(\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial}\left(\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x})\right)\right)^{2}\right)=-E\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\left(\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x}))\right)\right)=-E\left(H_{\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x})}\right).

Definition 5.1.31

(CRLB)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
, where
\theta
is a parameter (or vector of parameters). If
\hat{\Theta}
is an unbiased estimator for
\theta
, then

\displaystyle MSE(\hat{\Theta})=Var(\hat{\Theta})\geq\frac{1}{I(\theta)}.

where
I(\theta)
is the Fisher information. What this is saying is, for any unbiased estimator
\hat{\Theta}
for
\theta
, the variance
Var(\hat{\Theta})=MSE(\hat{\Theta})
is at least
\frac{1}{I(\theta)}
. If we achieve this lower bound, meaning our variance
is exactly equal to
\frac{1}{I(\theta)}
, then we have the best variance possible for our estimate. That is, we have the minimum variance unbiased estimator (MVUE) for
\theta
.

If If
\hat{\Theta}
is an unbiased estimator for
\theta
, then

\displaystyle MSE(\hat{\Theta})=Var(\hat{\Theta})+Bias^{2}(\Theta)\geq\frac{1}{I(\theta)}.

If
\hat{\Theta}
is any unbiased estimator for
\theta
, there is a minimum possible variance (variance = MSE for unbiased estimators) and the estimator achieves this lowest possible variance, it is said to be efficient.

Definition 5.1.32

(Efficiency)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
, where
\theta
is a parameter (or vector of parameters). If
\hat{\Theta}
is an unbiased estimator for
\theta
, then its efficiency is defined as

\displaystyle e(\hat{\Theta})=\frac{I^{-1}(\theta)}{Var(\hat{\Theta})}\leq 1.

This will always be between
0
and
1
because if our variance is equal to the CRLB, then it equals
1
, and anything greater will result in a smaller value. A larger variance will result in a smaller efficiency, and we want our efficiency to be as high as possible.
An unbiased estimator is said to be efficient if it achieves the CRLB - meaning
e(\hat{\Theta})=1
. That is, it could not possibly have a lower variance. Again, the CRLB is not guaranteed for biased estimators.

Let
\hat{\Theta}_{1}
and
\hat{\Theta}_{2}
be two unbiased estimators of a unknown parameter
\theta
with equal sample sizes. Then,
\hat{\Theta}_{1}
is a more efficient estimator than
\hat{\Theta}_{2}
if
Var(\hat{\Theta}_{1})<Var(\hat{\Theta}_{2})
i.e.
\varepsilon(\hat{\Theta}_{1})>\varepsilon(\hat{\Theta}_{2})
.

Example 5.1.33

(Efficiency of sample mean)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
Pois(\theta)
, then the MLE and MoM both estimate the same given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}=\hat{\theta}_{MoM}\implies\hat{\Theta}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}=\bar{X}.

Question: Prove that the estimator (or the sample mean),
\hat{\Theta}=\bar{X}
, with respect to the unknown parameter
\theta
(or the unknown mean) is efficient or not.

Solution: First, we need to check that it’s unbiased, as the CRLB only holds for unbiased estimators.

\displaystyle E\left(\hat{\Theta}\right)=E\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(X_{i})=\theta\implies Bias(\hat{\Theta})=0.

Now, we compute the variance of
\hat{\Theta}
:

\displaystyle Var(\hat{\Theta})=Var(\bar{X})=\frac{Var(X)}{n}=\frac{\theta}{n}.

Then, we need to define likelihood function
L(\theta|x)
to compute Fisher Information that gives us the CRLB, and see if our
variance matches.

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=P(\mathbb{x}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\theta}\frac{\theta^{x_{i}}}{(x_{i})!}\implies

\displaystyle\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\theta+x_{i}\ln(\theta)-\ln((x_{i})!)\right)\implies\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left(\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x}))\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-1+\frac{x_{i}}{\theta}\right)\implies\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\left(\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x}))\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\frac{x_{i}}{\theta^{2}}\right)=\frac{-n\bar{x}}{\theta^{2}}.

Then, in order to compute information matrix
I(\theta)
, we compute the expectation of second derivative of log-likelihood function as

\displaystyle E\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\left(\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x}))\right)\right)=E\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}-\frac{x_{i}}{\theta^{2}}\right)=\frac{-\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(X_{i})}{\theta^{2}}=\frac{-n\theta}{\theta^{2}}=\frac{-n}{\theta}.

So, Fisher Information is the negative expected value of the second derivative of the log-likelihood, so, we have

\displaystyle I(\theta)=-E\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\left(\ln(L(\theta|\mathbb{x}))\right)\right)=\frac{n}{\theta}.

Finally, efficiency of the estimator
\hat{\Theta}
as the inverse of the Fisher Information over the variance computed as

\displaystyle e(\hat{\Theta})=\frac{I^{-1}(\theta)}{Var(\hat{\Theta}}=1.

Thus, we’ve shown that, since the efficiency is 1, the estimator is efficient. That is, it has the best possible variance among all unbiased estimators of
\theta
.

Sufficiency

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. sample of size n from a known distribution model with unknown parameter
\theta
. Imagine we have two people:

a.

Statistician A: Knows the entire sample, gets n quantities:
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

b.

Statistician B: Knows
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=t
, a single number which is a function of the realizations of an I.I.D. sample of size n. For example, the sum or the maximum of the sample points.

Heuristically,
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is a sufficient statistic if Statistician B can do just as good a job as Statistician A, given information. For example, if the samples are from the Bernoulli distribution, knowing
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}
(the number of heads) is just as good as knowing all the individual outcomes, since a good estimate would be the number of heads over the number of total trials. Hence, we don’t actually care the ORDER of the outcomes, just how many heads occurred. The word sufficient in English roughly means enough, and so this terminology is well-chosen.

Definition 5.1.34

(Statistic)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of X having a known distribution model with unknown parameter
\theta
. Then, we define a statistic as a function
T:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
of realizations
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
of the I.I.D. sample of size n. For examples:

a.

T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\to
the sum.

b.

T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\to
the sample mean.

c.

T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=max_{i=1,2,\ldots,n}x_{i}=x_{max}\to
the max/largest value.

d.

T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=x_{1}\to
just take the first sample point.

e.

T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=7\to
ignore all the sample points.

All estimators are statistics because they take in our n data points and produce a single number.

Definition 5.1.35

(Sufficient statistic)
.

A statistic
T=T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
is a sufficient statistic if the conditional distribution of
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
given
T=t
and
\theta
does not depend on
\theta
i.e.

\displaystyle P(X_{1}=x_{1},\ldots,X_{n}=x_{n}|T=t,\theta)=P(X_{1}=x_{1},\ldots,X_{n}=x_{n}|T=t).

Theorem 5.1.36
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n with likelihood
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})
. A statistic
T=T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
is sufficient if and only if there exist non-negative functions g and h such that:

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})=g(\mathbb{x})\cdot h(T(\mathbb{x}),\theta)

That is, the likelihood of the data can be split into a product of two terms: the first term g can depend on the entire data, but not
\theta
, and the second term h can depend on
\theta
, but only on the data through the sufficient statistic T. (In other words, T is the only thing that allows the data
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and
\theta
to interact.) That is, we don’t have access to the n individual quantities
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
just the single number (T, the sufficient statistic).

But basically, we want to split the likelihood into a product of two terms/functions:

a.

For the first term g, you are allowed to know each individual sample point if you want, but NOT
\theta
.

b.

For the second term h, you can only know the sufficient statistic (single number)
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\theta
. We may not know each individual
x_{i}
.

Example 5.1.37

(Sample statistic for
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
X\sim Unif(0,\theta)
, then the MLE and MoM both estimate the same given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=x_{max},\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=2\bar{x}\implies\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=X_{max},\hat{\Theta}_{MoM}=2\bar{X}.

Question: Prove that
\hat{\Theta}_{LME}=X_{max}
is a sufficient estimator of
\theta
.

Answer: We have the likelihood function
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})
as:

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\theta}I_{\{0\leq x_{i}\leq\theta\}}=\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}I_{\{0\leq x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\leq\theta\}}=\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}I_{\{0\leq x_{max}\leq\theta\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}I_{\{T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\leq\theta\}}.

Now, we choose

\displaystyle g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=1,\ h(T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\theta)=\frac{1}{\theta^{n}}I_{\{T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\leq\theta\}}

By the Neyman-Fisher Factorization Criterion,
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=X_{max}
is a sufficient estimator. For the h term, notice that we just need to know the max of the samples
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
to compute h: we don’t actually need to know each individual
x_{i}
. It is noted that here the only interaction between the data and parameter
\theta
happens through the sufficient statistic (the max of all the values).

Example 5.1.38

(Sample statistic for
X\sim Pois(\theta)
)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
X\sim Pois(\theta)
, then the we have

\displaystyle T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i},\ \hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}\implies

\displaystyle T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=S_{n},\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=\bar{X}.

Question: Prove that
T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=S_{n}
is a sufficient statistic and hence
\hat{\Theta}_{LME}=\bar{X}
is a sufficient estimator. (The reason this is true is because we don’t need to know each individual sample to have a good estimate for
\theta
, we just need to know how many events happened total.)

Answer: We have the likelihood function
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})
as:

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\theta}\frac{\theta^{x_{i}}}{(x_{i})!}=\frac{e^{-n\theta}\theta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}}{\Pi_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i})!}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Pi_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i})!}\cdot e^{-n\theta}\theta^{T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}.

Now, we choose

\displaystyle g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\frac{1}{\Pi_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i})!},\ h(T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\theta)=e^{-n\theta}\theta^{T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}.

By the Neyman-Fisher Factorization Criterion,
T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
is a sufficient statistic. This implies
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=\frac{T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}{n}
is also a sufficient statistic, since knowing the total number of events and the average number of
events is equivalent (since we know n).

It is noted that here we had the g term handle some function of only
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
but not
\theta
. For the h term though, we do have
\theta
but don’t need the individual samples
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
to compute h. Imagine being just given
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, now you have enough information to compute h.

Example 5.1.39

(Sample statistic for
X\sim Ber(\theta)
)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a random variable X from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, then the we have

\displaystyle T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i},\ \hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}\implies

\displaystyle T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=S_{n},\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=\bar{X}.

Question: Prove that
T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=S_{n}
is a sufficient statistic and hence
\hat{\Theta}_{LME}=\bar{X}
is a sufficient estimator. (The reason this is true is because we don’t need to know each individual sample to have a good estimate for
\theta
, we just need to know how many events happened total.)

Answer: We have the likelihood function
L(\theta|\mathbb{x})
as:

\displaystyle L(\theta|\mathbb{x})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}p_{X}(x_{i}|\theta)=\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\theta^{x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{1-x_{i}}=\theta^{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}}(1-\theta)^{n-x_{1}-\ldots-x_{n}}

\displaystyle=\theta^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}(1-\theta)^{n-\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}}.

Now, we choose

\displaystyle g(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=1,\ h(T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\theta)=\theta^{T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}(1-\theta)^{n-T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}.

By the Neyman-Fisher Factorization Criterion,
T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
is a sufficient statistic. This implies
\hat{\Theta}_{MLE}=\frac{T(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})}{n}
is also a sufficient statistic, since knowing the total number of events and the average number of
events is equivalent (since we know n).

It is noted that here we had the g term handle some function of only
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
but not
\theta
. For the h term though, we do have
\theta
but don’t need the individual samples
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
to compute h. Imagine being just given
T(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, now you have enough information to compute h.

We can notice that here the only interaction between the data and parameter
\theta
happens through the sufficient statistic (the sum/mean of all the values). We don’t actually need to know each individual
x_{i}
.

5.2
Interval estimation

Till now, we have discussed several techniques to estimate unknown parameters and the goodness criterion of the estimator. Even if the estimator is having all the good properties, the probability that the estimator for
\theta
is exactly correct is 0 i.e.
P(\hat{\Theta}=\theta)=0
, since
\theta
is continuous (a decimal number). So, we need to construct confidence intervals around the estimator, containing the true value of the parameter with high probability i.e.
\hat{\theta}
is very close to
\theta
with high probability. Confidence intervals are used in the Frequentist setting, which means the population parameters are assumed to be unknown but will always be fixed, not random variables. Credible intervals, on the other hand, are a Bayesian version of a Frequentist’s confidence interval.

5.2.1
Confidence interval

Theorem 5.2.1

(A
100(1-\alpha)\%
confidence interval)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size n from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
. Using CLT, an
100(1-\alpha)\%
confidence interval of an unbiased estimate
\hat{\theta}
for
\theta
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}\in\left[\theta-z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})},\theta+z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}\right].

Where
z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
is z-score given by

\displaystyle z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}=\varphi^{-1}(a-\frac{\alpha}{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1.96&\ \mbox{for}\ \alpha=5\%=0.05\\
2.576&\ \mbox{for}\ \alpha=1\%=0.01.\end{array}\right.

Proof 5.2.2
.

We have the following information:

i.

X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
, so

\displaystyle E(\Theta)=g_{1}(\theta)\ \mbox{and}\ Var(\Theta)=g_{2}(\theta).

ii.

a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
with given n, computes a value of
\hat{\theta}
. Also,
Var(\Theta)=\frac{g_{1}(\theta)}{n}
.

iii.

In
100(1-\alpha)\%
the
\alpha
is given (e.g.
\alpha=5\%
or
1\%
etc).

iv.

A technique-central limit theorem.

To compute a
100(1-\alpha)\%
confidence interval, we have

\displaystyle P(|\hat{\theta}-\theta|>\varepsilon)\leq\alpha\implies P\left(|\frac{\hat{\theta}-\theta}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}\right)\leq\alpha\approx

\displaystyle P\left(|Z|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}\right)\leq\alpha\implies 1-P\left(|Z|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}\right)\geq 1-\alpha

\displaystyle P\left(|Z|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}\right)\geq 1-\alpha\implies 2\varphi\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}\right)\geq 2-\alpha\implies

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}\geq\varphi^{-1}\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\implies\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}}\geq z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\implies

\displaystyle\varepsilon\geq z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}\implies\hat{\theta}\in\left[\theta-z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})},\theta+z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sqrt{Var(\hat{\theta})}\right].

Example 5.2.3

(Confidence interval wrt
Pois(\theta)
)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an i.i.d. sample of size from
X\sim Pois(\theta)
where
\theta
is unknown. Then, both MLE and MoM estimates agreed at the sample mean as:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\hat{\theta}_{MLE}=\hat{\theta}_{MoM}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\bar{x}.

Question: Construct an
95\%
confidence interval of
\hat{\theta}
(i.e. it contains
\theta
with probability
95\%
).

Answer: We have the following information:

i.

X\sim Pois(\theta)
, so

\displaystyle E(X)=\theta\ \mbox{and}\ Var(X)=\theta.

ii.

a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
with given n, computes a value of
\hat{\theta}=\bar{x}
. Also,
Var(\hat{\Theta})=\frac{\theta}{n}
.

iii.

In
100(1-\alpha)\%
the
\alpha=5\%=0.05
. So, z-score is given by

\displaystyle z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}=\varphi^{-1}\left(a-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)=\varphi^{-1}(0.975)=1.96.

iv.

A technique-central limit theorem.

Now, from theorem (
5.2.1
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}\in\left[\theta-1.96\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{n}},\theta+1.96\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{n}}\right]\equiv\theta\in\left[\hat{\theta}-1.96\sqrt{\frac{\hat{\theta}}{n}},\hat{\theta}+1.96\sqrt{\frac{\hat{\theta}}{n}}\right]\equiv

\displaystyle\theta\in\left[\bar{x}-1.96\sqrt{\frac{\bar{x}}{n}},\bar{x}+1.96\sqrt{\frac{\bar{x}}{n}}\right].

Example 5.2.4
.

Backdrop: consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. sample of size n from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
with the unknown
\theta
as probability of success.

Question: given
n=400
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=136
(observed 136 successes out of 400), construct a
99\%
confidence interval for
\theta
(the unknown probability of success).

Answer: We have the following information:

i.

X\sim Ber(\theta)
, so

\displaystyle E(X)=\theta\ \mbox{and}\ Var(X)=\theta(1-\theta).

ii.

a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=136
with given
n=400
, computes a value of
\hat{\theta}=\bar{x}=\frac{136}{400}=0.34
. Also,
Var(\hat{\Theta})=\frac{\theta(1-\theta)}{n}\leq\frac{1}{4n}
.

iii.

In
100(1-\alpha)\%
the
\alpha=1\%=0.01
. So, z-score is given by

\displaystyle z_{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}=\varphi^{-1}(a-\frac{\alpha}{2})=\varphi^{-1}(0.995)=2.576.

iv.

A technique-central limit theorem.

Now, from theorem (
5.2.1
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}\in\left[\theta-2.576\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{n}},\theta+2.576\sqrt{\frac{\theta}{n}}\right]\equiv\theta\in\left[\hat{\theta}-2.576\sqrt{\frac{\hat{\theta}(1-\hat{\theta})}{n}},\hat{\theta}+\right.

\displaystyle\left.2.576\sqrt{\frac{\hat{\theta}(1-\hat{\theta})}{n}}\right]=\left[\bar{x}-2.576\sqrt{\frac{\bar{x}(1-\bar{x})}{n}},\bar{x}+2.576\sqrt{\frac{\bar{x}(1-\bar{x})}{n}}\right]=

\displaystyle\left[0.34-2.576\cdot\sqrt{\frac{0.34\cdot 0.66}{400}},0.34+2.576\cdot\sqrt{\frac{0.34\cdot 0.66}{400}}\right]=[0.28,0.40].

Approach-02: Central limit theorem and an optimal bound on the variance

\displaystyle Var(\hat{\Theta})=\frac{\theta(1-\theta)}{n}\leq\frac{1}{4n}.

We have

\displaystyle 0.005

\displaystyle\geq P(\hat{\Theta}-\theta>\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{2}P(|\hat{\Theta}-\theta|>\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}P\left(\left|\frac{\hat{\Theta}-\theta}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\right|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\right)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2}P\left(|Z|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\right)=P\left(Z>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\right)

\displaystyle=1-P\left(Z\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\right)=1-\varphi\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\right).

So, we get

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\right)\geq 1-0.005=0.995=\varphi(2.576)=\varphi(z_{0.995})\implies

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)/400}}\geq 2.576\ \mbox{for any}\ \theta\in(0,1)\implies 20\varepsilon\cdot 4\geq 2.576\implies

\displaystyle\varepsilon\geq\frac{2.576}{20}\cdot\frac{1}{4}=\frac{2.576}{20}\cdot\frac{1}{4}=0.032\implies\mbox{for a realization}\ \hat{\theta}=0.34,

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}\in[\theta-0.032,\theta+0.032]\equiv\theta\in[0.307,0.372]\approx[0.31,0.37].

If we repeat this process several times (getting
n=400
sample points each time and constructing different confidence intervals), about
99\%
of the confidence intervals we construct will contain
\theta
.

Example 5.2.5

(Finding
n
with a given confidence interval)
.

Backdrop: Assume a realization/people
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
(of an I.I.D random sample of size
n
from
X\sim Ber(p)
) vote party
A
with probability
p
. We poll
n
people. Let
M_{n}
be the fraction/average of the polled persons for Party
A
and defined as

\displaystyle M_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}.

Find n such that estimator
M_{n}
to be within
\pm 1\%
of the true value p with probability at least
95\%
, statistically

\displaystyle P(|M_{n}-p|>0.01)\leq 0.05.

And geometrically, can be visualized as in the figure (
):

Figure 5.6
:
The distribution
M_{n}-p
is symmetric, and we are looking for tail bounds.

Answer: Since opinion of ith polled person for party A is
X_{i}\sim Ber(p)
and the distribution of
M_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
is symmetric around its mean
E(M_{n})=p
with
Var(M_{n})=\frac{p(1-p)}{n}
, then, we have

\displaystyle 0.025

\displaystyle\geq P(M_{n}-p>0.01)=\frac{1}{2}P(|M_{n}-p|>0.01)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}P\left(\left|\frac{M_{n}-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\right|>\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\right)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{2}P\left(|Z|>\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\right)=P\left(Z>\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\right)

\displaystyle=1-P\left(Z\leq\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\right)=1-\varphi\left(\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\right)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\varphi\left(\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\right)\geq 1-0.025=0.975=\varphi(1.96)=\varphi(z_{1-0.025})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}}\geq 1.96\ \mbox{for any}\ p\in(0,1)\implies 0.01\sqrt{n}\cdot 4\geq 1.96

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle n\geq\left(\frac{1.96}{0.01}\cdot\frac{1}{4}\right)^{2}=9604.

In this calculation we used the approximation given by the CLT, but how good is it? If we work out the exact computation, using the fact that
M_{n}
is a scaled binomial and the worse-case parameter
p=1/2
, then we get that
n=9604
will give us a
95.1\%
probability of being within
0.01
of the true value p: in this case, the CLT’s approximation is very good. So if the CLT is so much better, why use Chebyshev’s inequality at all? The reason is that Chebyshev is a bound that works for all
\varepsilon
and probabilities, is always a valid upper bound but is very conservative. The CLT is often much sharper, however it only works for
\varepsilon
of appropriate scale and does not give a rock-solid bound as it is an approximation and not a bound. Is it possible to strengthen Chebyshev’s inequality to get a bound nearly as sharp as the CLT’s, under appropriate assumptions? Yes, the Chernoff inequality provides the optimal approximation.

5.3
Hypothesis testing

Example 5.3.1

(A victim is not guilty)
.

Consider a victim is in trial for a crime in the court. Then the Judge will have following hypotheses to determine whether or not the victim is guilty (a given statement happens to be source of an alternative hypothesis) as:

\displaystyle H_{0}:

the victim is not guilty

\displaystyle H_{a}:

\displaystyle\ \mbox{the victim is guilty}.

Based on evidence/observation/realization/data and its statistical analysis, the Judge come up with a conclusion that either reject
H_{0}
or fail to reject
H_{0}
.

Example 5.3.2

(Magician’s fair coin)
.

An statistical method of hypothesis testing to prove statistically a claim by selecting a suitable hypothesis based on data. Suppose, we have a Magician Sarkar, who says

Magician Sarkar: I have here a fair coin.

And then an audience, an Agnostic (described in (
5.7
)) statistician named Agyeya, engages him in a conversation:

—

Agnostic statistician Agyeya: I don’t believe you. Can we examine it?

—

Magician Sarkar: Be my guest.

—

Agnostic Statistician Agyeya: I am going to flip your coin 100 times and see how many heads I get. (Agyeya flips the coin 100 times and sees 99 heads.) You cannot be telling the truth, there’s no way this coin is fair.

—

Magician Sarkar: Wait I was just unlucky, I swear I am not lying.

So let’s give Sarkar the benefit of the doubt. We compute the probability that we observed an outcome at least as extreme as this, given that Sarkar isn’t lying i.e. the coin is fair, so the number of heads observed should be
X\sim Bin(100,0.5)
, because there are 100 independent trials and a
50\%
of heads since it’s fair. So, the probability that we observe at least
99
heads (because we’re looking for something as least as extreme), is the sum of the probability of 99 heads and the probability of 100 heads as:

\displaystyle P(X\geq 99)

\displaystyle=\binom{100}{99}(0.5)^{99}(0.5)^{1}+\binom{100}{100}(0.5)^{100}(0.5)^{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{101}{2^{100}}\approx 7.96\times 10^{-29}\approx 0.

Basically, if the coin were fair, the probability of what we just observed (99 heads or more) is basically 0. This is strong statistical evidence that the coin is NOT fair. Our assumption was that the coin is fair, but if this were the case, observing such an extreme outcome would be extremely unlikely. Hence, our assumption is probably wrong. So, this is like a Probabilistic Proof by Contradiction.

Figure 5.7
:
A self-explanatory meaning of Agnostic in Hindi with corresponding translation in English.

Example 5.3.3

(to determine a fair coin)
.

Consider a coin toss. Then, to determine whether the coin is fair or not (happens to be source of an alternative hypothesis), we come up with following hypotheses as:

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\ \mbox{the victim is fair i.e.}\ p=0.5

\displaystyle H_{a}:

\displaystyle\ \mbox{the victim is not fairy i.e.}\ p\neq 0.5.

Based on evidence/observation/realization/data and its statistical analysis, the we can conclude that either reject
H_{0}
or fail to reject
H_{0}
.

A summarized hypothesis testing procedure

Step 1:

Make a claim (e.g. like Indian food is spicy, Dirty boots belong on the table etc.)

Step 2:

Set up a null hypothesis
H_{0}
and alternative hypothesis
H_{a}
(based on an underlined statistical statement).

•

Alternative hypothesis can be one-sided or two-sided.

•

The null hypothesis is usually a baseline, no effect, or benefit of the doubt.

•

The alternative is what you want to prove, and is opposite the null.

Step 3:

Choose a significance level
\alpha
(usually
\alpha=0.05
or
0.01
).

Step 4:

Collect data (e.g. a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size n from
X\sim p_{X}(x|\theta)
or
f_{X}(x|\theta)
). And come up with an estimate
\hat{\theta}
for the unknown parameter
\theta
(e.g.
\hat{\theta}=\bar{x}
).

Step 5:

Compute a p-value as:

\displaystyle p=P(\mbox{observing data at least as extreme as our}|H_{0}\ \mbox{is true}).

Step 6:

State the conclusion as:

•

if
p<\alpha
, reject the null hypothesis
H_{0}
in favour of the alternative
H_{a}
(i.e. our result is statistically significant in this case).

•

if
p\geq\alpha
, fail to reject the null hypothesis
H_{0}
.

Example 5.3.4
.

Backdrop: consider a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size n from
X\sim Ber(\theta)
, where
\theta
is the unknown probability of success.

Question: We want to determine whether or not more than
3/4
of Indian would vote for A in 2024. In a random poll sampling
n=137
Indian, we collected responses
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
(each is 1 or 0, if they would vote for him or not). We observe 131 yes responses:
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=131
. Perform a hypothesis test and state your conclusion.

Answer:

a.

We have

\displaystyle X\sim Ber(\theta)\implies E(X)=\theta,\ Var(X)=\theta(1-\theta).

b.

Let p denote the true proportion of Americans that would vote for A. Then, the null and alternative hypotheses are:

\displaystyle H_{0}:\ \mbox{Exactly $3/4$ Indian voted for party A i.e.}\ p=0.75

\displaystyle H_{a}:\ \mbox{More than $3/4$ Indian voted for party A i.e.}\ p\geq 0.75.

c.

Let
X_{i}\to
the response of ith polled person in the poll sampling. So, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}=\bar{X}\implies E(\Theta)=E(\bar{X})=\theta,\ Var(\hat{\Theta})=Var(\bar{X})=\frac{Var(X)}{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(1-\theta)}{n}.

As, we have a realization
\mathbb{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{137})
(such that
\sum_{i=1}^{137}=131
) of an I.I.D. random sample of size
n=137
from
X\sim Ber(x|\theta)
. So, we get

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\bar{x}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\frac{131}{137}=0.96.

d.

Now, test the hypothesis at the
\alpha=0.01
significance level. Then, under the null hypothesis, we have
p=0.75
, so, the p-value (observing data at least as extreme), is given by

\displaystyle P(\bar{X}\geq\bar{x})

\displaystyle=P\left(\bar{X}\geq\frac{131}{137}\right)=P\left(\frac{\bar{X}-0.75}{\sqrt{(0.37)^{2}}}\geq\frac{131/137-0.75}{\sqrt{(0.37)^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\approx P(Z\geq 5.42643)\approx 0.

e.

With a p-value so close to 0 (and certainly
<\alpha=0.01
, we reject the null hypothesis that (only)
75\%
of Indian would vote for A. There is strong evidence that this proportion is actually larger.

Again, what we did was: assume
p=0.75
(null hypothesis), then note that the probability of observing data so extreme (in fact very close to
100\%
of people), was nearly 0. Hence, we reject this null hypothesis because what we observed would’ve been so unlikely if it were true.

Chapter 6
Statistical modelling

6.1
Statistical models

It is fortunate, however, that a large number of estimation problems can be represented by a data model in linear framework as a linear model that allows us to easily determine this estimator. Not only is the minimum variance valued (MVU) estimator immediately evident once the linear model has been identified, but in addition, the statistical performance follows naturally. The key, then, to finding the optimal estimator is in structuring the problem in the linear model form to take advantage of its unique properties.

Derivation of least mean square estimator

Theorem 6.1.1

(Condition expectation as an LMS estimator)
.

The covariance between two random variables has an important bearing on the predictability of Y based on knowledge of the outcome of
X
. So, consider
Y
is statistically related to another random variable
X
i.e.
Y=g(X)
and
g
is unknown. Then, least-mean-square estimation of
Y
is given by the conditional expectation as

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E\left(Y|X\right)=\hat{g}(X).

Moreover, estimation error
Y-E(Y|X)
and the attribute
X
are uncorrelated as:

\displaystyle E(X(Y-E(Y|X)))=E(XY)-E(XY)=0=E(X)E(Y-E(Y|X))

\displaystyle\implies Cov(X,Y-E(Y|X))=0.

Since, we have

\displaystyle E(XE(Y|X))

\displaystyle=E\left(\int_{x\in\Omega_{X}}xE(Y|X=x)f_{X}(x)dx\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in\Omega_{Y}}\int_{x\in\Omega_{X}}xyf_{X}(x)f_{Y|X}(y|x)dxdy

\displaystyle=\int_{(x,y)\in\Omega_{X}\times\Omega_{Y}}xyf_{X,Y}(x,y)dxdy=E(XY).

Proof 6.1.2
.

For one observation of
X=x
, we have

\displaystyle MSE=E((Y-c)^{2})

\displaystyle=E\left(\left(Y-E(Y|X=x)+E(Y|X=x)-c\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=E((Y-E(Y|X=x))^{2})+(E(Y|X=x)-c)^{2}.

To find least mean square estimate, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dc}(MSE)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dc}\left(\left(E(Y|X=x)-c\right)^{2}\right)=-2\left(E(Y|X=x)-c\right)=0

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle c=E(Y|X=x)=\hat{g}(x).

That is, we have a least mean square estimate of
Y
as

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=\hat{y}=E(Y|X=x)=\hat{g}(x).

Then, varies for every possible observation of
X
, we have the least mean square estimator of
Y
as:

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=\hat{g}(X)\ \mbox{such that}\ E\left((Y-g(X))^{2}\right)\geq E\left(Y-E(Y|X)\right)\ \mbox{for any}\ g.

Alternatively, consider an arbitrary function g of
X
and define mean squared error with respect to Y as

\displaystyle MSE(g(X))

\displaystyle=E((Y-g(X))^{2})=E((Y-E(Y|X)+E(Y|X)-g(X))^{2})

\displaystyle=E\left(\left(Y-E\left(Y|X\right)\right)^{2}\right)+E\left(\left(E\left(Y|X\right)-g(X)\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left(E\left(\left(Y-E\left(Y|X\right)\right)^{2}|X\right)\right)+E\left(\left(E\left(Y|X\right)-g(X)\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=E\left(Var\left(Y|X\right)\right)+E\left(\left(E\left(Y|X\right)-g(X)\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq E\left(\left(E\left(Y|X\right)-g(X)\right)^{2}\right)\ \mbox{for any other function g of}\ X.

6.2
Linear models

Consider
Y
is statistically dependent on another random variable (or vector), as a variate
X
, in a linear framework as

\displaystyle E(Y|X)=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X.

Theorem 6.2.1

(Computation of parameters for a linear model)
.

Consider
Y
is statistically dependent on another random variable (or vector), as a variate
X
, in a linear framework as

\displaystyle E(Y|X)=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X.

Then, the parameters can be computed as

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=\left(Var(X)\right)^{-1}Cov(X,Y),\ \theta_{0}=E(Y)-\left(Var(X)\right)^{-1}Cov(X,Y)E(X).

So,

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=E(Y)+\left(Var(X)\right)^{-1}Cov(X,Y)(X-E(X))\equiv

\displaystyle Y=E(Y|X)+\varepsilon=E(Y)+\left(Var(X)\right)^{-1}Cov(X,Y)(X-E(X))+\varepsilon.

Proof 6.2.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=aX+b\implies Y=aX+b+\varepsilon\implies E(Y)=aE(X)+b\implies

\displaystyle E\left(E(X)E(Y|X)\right)=a\left(E(X)\right)^{2}+bE(X),\ E(XY)=aE(X^{2})+bE(X)\implies

\displaystyle a=\left(E(X^{2})-(E(X))^{2}\right)^{-1}(E(XY)-E(X)E(Y)),\ b=E(Y)-aE(X)

\displaystyle\implies\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=E(Y)+\left(Var(X)\right)^{-1}Cov(X,Y)(X-E(X)).

6.2.1
Simple univariate linear model (SULM)

A linear model for regression assumes that the LMS estimate of
Y=f(X)
as a conditional expectation/regression function
E(Y|X)
is
linear in the inputs
X_{1},\ldots,X_{d}
. Linear models were largely developed in the pre-computer age of statistics, but even in today’s computer era there are still good reasons to study and use them. They are simple and often provide an adequate and interpretable description of how the inputs affect the output. For prediction purposes they can sometimes outperform fancier non-linear models, especially in situations with small numbers of training cases, low signal-to-noise ratio or sparse data. Finally, linear methods can be
applied to transformations of the inputs to higher dimensional embedded spaces using suitable basis-function approaches.

Computation of parameters for SULM

Theorem 6.2.3

(Univariate linear model)
.

Consider
Y
is statistically dependent on another random variable
X
in linear framework as

\displaystyle E(Y|X)=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X.

Then, the parameters can be computed as

\displaystyle\theta_{1}=\frac{Cov(X,Y)}{Var(X)},\ \theta_{0}=E(Y)-\frac{Cov(X,Y)}{Var(X)}E(X).

So,

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=E(Y)+\frac{Cov(X,Y)}{Var(X)}(X-E(X))\equiv

\displaystyle Y=E(Y|X)+\varepsilon=E(Y)+\frac{Cov(X,Y)}{Var(X)}(X-E(X))+\varepsilon.

Proof 6.2.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=aX+b\implies Y=aX+b+\varepsilon\implies E(Y)=aE(X)+b\implies

\displaystyle E\left(E(X)E(Y|X)\right)=a\left(E(X)\right)^{2}+bE(X),\ E(XY)=aE(X^{2})+bE(X)\implies

\displaystyle a=\frac{E(XY)-E(X)E(Y)}{E(X^{2})-(E(X))^{2}}=\frac{Cov(X,Y)}{Var(X)},\ b=E(Y)-\frac{Cov(X,Y)}{Var(X)}E(X)

\displaystyle\implies\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=E(Y)+\frac{Cov(X,Y)}{Var(X)}(X-E(X)).

Example 6.2.5

(Signal detection)
.

Question: Consider a noisy observation
Y=S+E
of a signal
S
, where the signal and noisy components are uncorrelated i.e.
Cov(S,E)=0
. Let
Var(S)=\sigma_{S}^{2}
and
Var(E)=\sigma_{E}^{2}
. Then, find an LMS estimate of the signal.

Answer: Using the above derivation for linear model, we get the conditional expectation of the signal
S
conditional upon the observation
Y
as

\displaystyle E(S|Y)

\displaystyle=E(S)+\frac{Cov(S,Y)}{Var(Y)}(Y-E(Y))

\displaystyle=E(S)+\frac{\sigma_{S}^{2}}{\sigma_{S}^{2}+\sigma_{E}^{2}}(Y-E(Y)).

Example 6.2.6

(
(
Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis 2008
)
)
.

Question: Let
\Theta\sim\mathcal{U}(4,10)
and we observe
\Theta
with some noise
W\sim\mathcal{U}(-1,1)
independent of
\Theta
. That is we observe the experimental value of the random variable
Y=\Theta+W
. Find the least square estimate of
\Theta
.

Answer: As,
\Theta\sim\mathcal{U}(4,10)
, noise
W\sim\mathcal{U}(-1,1)
are both are independent. So, conditioned on
\Theta=\theta
, X is the same as
\theta+W
, and is uniformly distributed over the interval
[\theta-1,\theta+1]
. Thus, joint PDF is given by

\displaystyle f_{\Theta,X}(\theta,x)=f_{\Theta}(\theta)f_{X|\Theta}(x|\theta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{6}\cdot\frac{1}{2}&\ \theta\in[4,6],x\in[\theta-1,\theta+1]\\
0,&\ \mbox{otherwise}\ .\end{array}\right.

The slanted rectangle in the right-hand side of figure (
6.1
) is the set of ordered-pairs
(\theta,x)
for which
f_{\Theta,X}(\theta,x)
is non-zero. The optimal estimate
E(\Theta|X=x)
is the midpoint of that section. It depends on the observed value x and hence (a piecewise linear) function of x.

Figure 6.1
:
Uniform distribution in left plot and least mean squared estimate
E(\Theta|X=x)
of
\Theta
in the right plot.

Estimation of parameters for SULM

Theorem 6.2.7

(Least squared estimate of parameter of a SULM)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a joint random variable
Z=(X,Y)
with unknown linear model
\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X\equiv Y=E(Y|X)+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X
. Now, running the linear model over n training examples namely in the data-set
\mathcal{S}=\left\{z_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
as:

\displaystyle y_{i}=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\ \mbox{for}\ i=1,2,\ldots,n\implies\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{1}\\
\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{2}\\
\vdots+\vdots\\
\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{n}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{n}\end{array}\right)\implies\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&x_{1}\\
1&x_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
1&x_{n}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\theta_{0}\\
\theta_{1}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{n}\end{array}\right)\implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon,\ \mbox{where}\ \mathbb{x},\mathbb{y},\varepsilon,\mathbb{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathrm{X}=\left(\mathbb{1},\mathbb{x}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2},\theta=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\theta_{0}\\
\theta_{1}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

So, running the linear model over n training examples
\mathcal{S}=\left\{z_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
transform to a linear inverse problem (of the linear model) as:
\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon
(a general form of the linear model), where

a.

\mathbb{y}
is called the response such that
E(\mathbb{y})=\mathrm{X}\theta
.

b.

\mathbb{x}
is called the explanatory variable, the regressor, or the covariates.

c.

\mathrm{X}
is called the design matrix.

d.

A least square estimate of
\theta
:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\mathrm{X}^{\dagger}\mathbb{y}\equiv\arg\min_{\theta}\left(\|\mathbb{y}-\mathrm{X}\theta\|^{2}\right)=\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\hat{\theta}_{0}\\
\hat{\theta}_{1}\end{array}\right).

And, the MoM estimate of the parameters are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{1}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})(y_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}},\ \hat{\theta}_{0}=\bar{y}-\hat{\theta}_{1}\bar{x}.

Finally, we get the best linear predictor of Y as:

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=\hat{\theta}_{0}+\hat{\theta}_{1}X.

Example 6.2.8
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a joint random variable
Z=(X,Y)
with
Y=E(Y|X)+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X+\varepsilon
.

Question: Students in a probability course claimed that doing the assignments had not helped prepare them for the end-term exam. The end-term exam score y and assignment score x (averaged up to the time of the end-term) for the
n=18
students in the class are in the table below in table (
6.1
):

:

y

Answer: consider a linear model
\hat{Y}=E(Y|X)=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X\equiv Y=E(Y|X)+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X
, and running over
n=18
training examples namely in the data-set
\mathcal{S}=\left\{x_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{18}
as

\displaystyle y_{i}=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\ \mbox{for}\ i=1,2,\ldots,18\implies\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\

y_{n}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{1}\\

\displaystyle+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varepsilon_{1}\\

80\\
\vdots\\

\displaystyle\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon,\ \mbox{where}\ \mathbb{x},\mathbb{y},\varepsilon,\mathbb{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{18},\mathrm{X}=\left(\mathbb{1},\mathbb{x}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{18\times 2},\theta=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\theta_{0}\\
\theta_{1}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}18&1045\\
1045&80199\end{array}\right)\implies\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0.228&-0.003\\
-0.003&0.00005\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1105\\
8195\end{array}\right)\implies\hat{\theta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\hat{\theta}_{0}\\
\hat{\theta}_{1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}10.75\\
0.8726\end{array}\right).

So,
\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon
is the general form of a linear model, where

a.

\mathbb{y}
is called the response such that
E(\mathbb{y})=\mathrm{X}\theta
.

b.

\mathbb{x}
is called the explanatory variable, the regressor, or the covariates.

c.

\mathrm{X}
is called the design matrix.

d.

A least square estimate of
\theta
:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=(\hat{\theta}_{0},\hat{\theta}_{1})^{\top}=(10.73,0.8726)^{\top}.

Alternatively, using method of moments estimate of the parameters, we get

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{1}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})(y_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}}\approx 0.8726,\ \hat{\theta}_{0}=\bar{y}-\hat{\theta}_{1}\bar{x}\approx 10.73.

:

that can predict the end-term exam mark for a student with a given averaged assignment mark.

Theorem 6.2.9
.

Least squared estimate of parameters of a simple univariate linear models, under the Gaussian noise, is the maximum likelihood estimate of the parameters of the SULM.

Theorem 6.2.10
.

Method of moments estimate of parameters of a simple univariate linear model is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{1}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})(y_{i}-\bar{y})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}},\ \hat{\theta}_{0}=\bar{y}-\hat{\theta}_{1}\bar{x}.

Theorem 6.2.11
.

Max a posteriori estimate of parameters of a simple univariate linear model.

Goodness of fit for estimated parameters of SULM

Definition 6.2.12

(Goodness of fit)
.

Consider a realization
\mathbb{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a joint random variable
Z=(X,Y)
with
Y=E(Y|X)+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X+\varepsilon
. Then, goodness of fit,
R^{2}
is defined as

\displaystyle R^{2}=1-\frac{\mbox{Current deviance}}{\mbox{Total deviance}}=1-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{y}_{i})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{y}_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\bar{y})^{2}}.

Theorem 6.2.13

(Gauss-Markov Theorem)
.

Consider
\mathbb{y}
be a random vector with

\displaystyle\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon,E(\mathbb{y})=\mathrm{X}\theta,Var(\mathbb{y})=\sigma^{2}I,\mathrm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(d+1)}\ \mbox{rank}\ (d+1).

Then,
\mathbb{a}^{\top}\hat{\Theta}
is the unique linear unbiased estimator of
\mathbb{a}^{\top}\theta
with minimum variance i.e. BLUE estimator i.e.
\hat{\Theta}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\theta,\sigma^{2}\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\right)
and
\sigma^{2}
is having an unbiased estimate as:

\displaystyle s^{2}=\frac{1}{n-2}(\mathbb{y}-\mathrm{X}\hat{\theta})^{\top}(\mathbb{y}-\mathrm{X}\hat{\theta})=\frac{1}{n-2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{y})^{2}=\frac{SSE}{n-2}.

Also,

a.

\hat{\Theta}_{1}\sim\mathbb{N}\left(\theta_{1},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}}\right)
.

b.

\frac{(n-2)S^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\sim\Xi^{2}(n-2)
.

c.

\hat{\Theta_{1}}
and
S^{2}
are independent, and hence

\displaystyle T=\frac{\hat{\Theta}_{1}}{\frac{S}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\bar{X})^{2}}}}

is a
t
distribution
T(n-2,\delta)
with a non-centrality parameter
\delta=\frac{\theta}{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}}}}
. A
100(1-\alpha)\%
confidence interval for
\theta_{1}
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{1}\pm t_{(\alpha/2,n-2)}\cdot\frac{s}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}}}.

Here, with a realization
\hat{\theta}_{1}=0.8726
, and
s=13.8547
, we have

\displaystyle t=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{1}}{\frac{s}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}-\bar{x})^{2}}}}=8.8025>t_{(0.025,16)}=2.210.

So, we reject
H_{0}:\theta_{1}=0
at the a
\alpha=0.05
level of significance. And hence
\theta_{1}\in[0.8726-2.120\cdot 0.09914,0.8726+2.210\cdot 0.09914]=[0.6624,1.0828]
.

Sufficient statistics for the SULM

A linear model system: a strategy of data-driven linear modelling and application

Step 1:

Hypothesis class and model structure: propose a model for the data (i.e. a parametric formula linking the response variable with the input variables, recognising the stochastic nature of the response).

Step 2:

Estimation of model: fit the model (e.g. find the best set of parameters).

Step 3:

Goodness of fit: ask is the model adequate? (i.e. consistent with the data). Does it allow the main questions of the analysis to be answered?

Step 4:

Model evaluation and performance: fit other plausible models, compare them, and choose the best.

Step 5:

Deployment of the best linear model and decision making.

6.2.2
Simple multivariate linear model

Motivation for simple multivariate linear model:

Computation of parameters for SUMLM

Definition 6.2.14

(Multivariate linear model)
.

Consider Y is statistically dependence on a number of covariate random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{d}
(i.e. a random vector
\mathbb{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 1}
) in linear framework as:

\displaystyle E(Y|X)=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X_{1}+\theta_{2}X_{2}+\ldots+\theta_{d}X_{d}=\theta_{0}+\theta^{\top}\mathbb{X}.

Then, the parameters can be computed as

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d})^{\top}=Cov(\mathbb{X},Y)(Cov(\mathbb{X}))^{-1},

\displaystyle\theta_{0}

\displaystyle=E(Y)-Cov(\mathbb{X},Y)(Var(\mathbb{X}))^{-1}E(\mathbb{X}).

So,

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E(Y|\mathbb{X})=E(Y)+Cov(\mathbb{X},Y)(Var(\mathbb{X})^{-1}(\mathbb{X}-E(\mathbb{X}))\equiv

\displaystyle Y=E(Y|\mathbb{X})+\varepsilon=E(Y)+Cov(\mathbb{X},Y)(Var(\mathbb{X})^{-1}(\mathbb{X}-E(\mathbb{X}))+\varepsilon.

Example 6.2.15
.

xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx

Estimation of parameters for SMLM

Theorem 6.2.16

(Parameter estimation of a simple multivariate linear model)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a joint random variable
Z=(\mathbb{X},Y)
with unknown linear model
\hat{Y}=E(Y|\mathbb{X})=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X_{1}+\ldots+\theta_{d}X_{d}\equiv Y=E(Y|\mathbb{X})+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X_{1}+\ldots+\theta_{d}X_{d}
. Now, running the linear model over n training examples namely in the data-set
\mathcal{S}=\left\{\mathbb{x}_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
as

\displaystyle y_{i}=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{i1}+\ldots+\theta_{d}x_{id}+\varepsilon_{i}\ \mbox{for}\ i=1,2,\ldots,n\implies

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{11}+\ldots+\theta_{d}x_{1d}\\
\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{21}+\ldots+\theta_{d}x_{2d}\\
\vdots+\vdots+\ldots+\vdots\\
\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{n1}+\ldots+\theta_{d}x_{nd}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{n}\end{array}\right)\implies

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&x_{1}\\
1&x_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
1&x_{n}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\theta_{0}\\
\theta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\theta_{d}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{n}\end{array}\right)\implies\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}_{n\times(d+1)}\mathbb{\theta}+\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\ \mbox{where}\ \mathbb{x}_{j}=(x_{1j},\ldots x_{nj})^{\top},j=1,2,\ldots,d,\mathbb{y},\varepsilon,\mathbb{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1},\mathrm{X}_{n\times(d+1)}=

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{1},\mathbb{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{x}_{d}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(d+1)},\theta=(\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+1)\times 1}.

So,
\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon
is the general form of a linear model, where

a.

\mathbb{y}
is called the response such that
E(\mathbb{y})=\mathrm{X}\theta
.

b.

\mathbb{x}
is called the explanatory variable, the regressor, or the covariates.

c.

\mathrm{X}
is called the design matrix.

d.

A least square estimate of
\omega
:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=(\hat{\theta}_{0},\hat{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\theta}_{d})^{\top}.

Example 6.2.17
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
of an i.i.d. random sample of size n i.e.
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\to
n I.I.D. copies of a joint random variable
Z=(\mathbb{X},Y)
with
Y=E(Y|\mathbb{X})+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X_{1}+\theta_{2}X_{2}+\varepsilon
.

Question: Consider a response y with bivariate input
(x_{1},x_{2})
and
n=12
from
(

)
in the table below:

Answer: consider a linear model
\hat{Y}=E(Y|\mathbb{X})=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X_{1}+\theta_{2}X_{2}\equiv Y=E(Y|\mathbb{X})+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X_{1}+\theta_{2}X_{2}
, and running over
n
training examples namely in the data-set
\mathcal{S}=\left\{(x_{i1},x_{i2}),y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
as

\displaystyle y_{i}=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}x_{i1}+\theta_{2}x_{i2}+\varepsilon_{i}\ \mbox{for}\ i=1,2,\ldots,n\implies\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\ \mbox{where}\ \mathbb{x},\mathbb{y},\varepsilon,\mathbb{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1},\mathrm{X}=\left(\mathbb{1},\mathbb{x}_{1},\mathbb{x}_{2}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 3},\theta=(\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\theta_{2})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 1}.

So,
\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon
is the general form of a linear model, where

a.

\mathbb{y}
is called the response such that
E(\mathbb{y})=\mathrm{X}\theta
.

b.

\mathbb{x}
is called the explanatory variable, the regressor, or the covariates.

c.

\mathrm{X}
is called the design matrix.

d.

A least square estimate of
\theta
:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=(\hat{\theta}_{0},\hat{\theta}_{1})^{\top}=(10.73,0.8726)^{\top}.

Now, with respect to above data, we have the following response data vector, design matrix and the associated computations:

\displaystyle y=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccc}2\\

0&2&2&2&4&4&4&6&6&6&8&8\\
2&6&7&5&9&8&7&10&11&9&15&13\end{array}\right)^{\top}\\
&X^{\top}X=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}12&52&102\\
52&395&536\\
102&536&1004\end{array}\right)\\
&\left(X^{\top}X\right)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0.97476&0.2429&-0.22871\\
0.2429&0.16207&-0.11120\\
-0.22871&-0.11120&0.08360\end{array}\right)\\
&\hat{\theta}=\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}5.3754\\
3.0118\\
-1.2855\end{array}\right).\end{array}

So, the covariance matrix of
\hat{\theta}
is given by

\displaystyle Cov(\hat{\theta})=\sigma^{2}\left(X^{\top}X\right)^{-1}=\sigma^{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0.975&0.243&-0.229\\
0.243&0.162&-0.111\\
-0.229&-0.111&0.084\end{array}\right)

The negative value of
Cov(\hat{\theta}_{1},\hat{\theta}_{2})=-0.111
indicates that in repeated sampling (using the same
12
values of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
),
(\hat{\theta}_{1}
and
\hat{\theta}_{2})
would tend to move in opposite directions; that is, an increase in one would be accompanied by a decrease in the other.

Goodness of fit for estimated parameters of SMLM

Theorem 6.2.18

(Gauss-Markov Theorem)
.

Consider
\mathbb{y}
be a random vector with

\displaystyle\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon,E(\mathbb{y})=\mathrm{X}\theta,Var(\mathbb{y})=\sigma^{2}I,\mathrm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(d+1)}\ \mbox{rank}\ (d+1).

Then,
\mathbb{a}^{\top}\hat{\Theta}
is the unique linear unbiased estimator of
\mathbb{a}^{\top}\theta
with minimum variance i.e. BLUE estimator i.e.
\hat{\Theta}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\theta,\sigma^{2}\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\right)
and
\sigma^{2}
is having an unbiased estimate as:

\displaystyle s^{2}=\frac{1}{n-d-1}(\mathbb{y}-\mathrm{X}\hat{\theta})^{\top}(\mathbb{y}-\mathrm{X}\hat{\theta})=\frac{1}{n-d-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-\hat{y})^{2}=\frac{SSE}{n-2}.

Also,

a.

\hat{\Theta}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\theta,\sigma^{2}\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\right)
.

b.

\frac{(n-d-1)S^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\sim\Xi^{2}(n-d-1)
.

c.

\hat{\Theta_{1}}
and
S^{2}
are independent.
Here, with a realization
\hat{\theta}=(5.3754,3.0118,-1.2855)^{\top}
, we get
s^{2}=2.829
.

6.2.3
Embedded linear models (ELM)

An embedded simple multivariate linear model is deep-down inside a simple multivariate linear model in the transformed higher dimensional embedded space, so computation and estimation of parameters for the ESMLM are similar to that of the SMLM.

Computation of parameters for ELM

Definition 6.2.19

(Embedded simple multivariate linear model)
.

Consider
Y
is statistically dependent on a number of covariate random variables
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{d}
(i.e. a random vector
\mathbb{X}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times 1}
) (that got transformed to
\mathbb{\varphi(X))}=(\varphi_{1}(\mathbb{X}),\ldots,\varphi_{p}(\mathbb{X}))^{\top}
in the p-dimensional embedded space using the non-linear basis functions
\varphi_{i}
) in linear framework as:

\displaystyle E(Y|\mathbb{X})

\displaystyle=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}\varphi_{1}(\mathbb{X})+\theta_{2}\varphi_{2}(\mathbb{X})+\ldots+\theta_{p}\varphi_{p}(\mathbb{X})

\displaystyle=\theta_{0}+\theta^{\top}\mathbb{\varphi(X)}.

Then, the parameters can be computed as

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{p})^{\top}=Cov(\mathbb{\varphi(X)},Y)(Cov(\mathbb{\varphi(X)}))^{-1},

\displaystyle\theta_{0}

\displaystyle=E(Y)-Cov(\mathbb{\varphi(X)},Y)(Var(\mathbb{\varphi(X))})^{-1}E(\mathbb{\varphi(X)}).

So,

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=E(Y|\mathbb{X})=E(Y)+Cov(\mathbb{X},Y)(Var(\mathbb{X})^{-1}(\mathbb{X}-E(\mathbb{X}))\equiv

\displaystyle Y=E(Y|\mathbb{X})+\varepsilon=E(Y)+Cov(\mathbb{X},Y)(Var(\mathbb{X})^{-1}(\mathbb{X}-E(\mathbb{X}))+\varepsilon.

Theorem 6.2.20

(A Kernel embedded and ridge regularized linear model (KRLM))
.

Let us now consider a reproducing Kernel Hilbert space (RKHS),
\mathcal{H}
, associated to a positive definite Kernel K on input space
\mathcal{X}
. Then, an estimate of KRLM is obtained by regularizing the mean squared error (MSE) criterion by the RKHS (a Hilbert space produced by a Kernel inspired feature map
\varphi(x)=k(\cdot,x)
) norm:

\displaystyle\hat{f}=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-f(x_{i})\right)^{2}+\lambda\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right).

Proof 6.2.21
.

Now, the modified estimation problem can be posed as:

\displaystyle\hat{f}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(y_{i},f(x_{i}))+\lambda\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}-f(x_{i})\right)^{2}+\lambda\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right).

By the representation theorem, the solution of the unconstrained problem can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\hat{f}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},x)=\hat{\omega}^{\top}\varphi(x),\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}\ \mbox{such that}\ \varphi(x)=k(\cdot,x).

To estimate
\omega
, we need to consider the following:

\displaystyle y=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n})^{\top},K=\left(k(x_{i},x_{j})\right)_{n\times n},k(x_{i},x_{j})=\varphi(x_{i})^{\top}\varphi(x_{j}),

\displaystyle\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n})^{\top},\left(\hat{f}(x_{1}),\ldots,\hat{f}(x_{n})\right)^{\top}\approx K\alpha.

Then, the estimation problem can be reposed as

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}=\arg\min_{\alpha in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(\frac{1}{n}(K\alpha-y)^{\top}(K\alpha-y)+\lambda\alpha^{\top}K\alpha\right)

So, the estimate of
\alpha
is computed as:

\displaystyle\nabla J(\alpha)=\frac{2}{n}K(K\alpha-y)+2\lambda K\alpha=\frac{2}{n}K\left((K\alpha+\lambda nI)-y\right)=0\implies

\displaystyle\alpha-\left(K+\lambda nI\right)^{-1}y\in Ker(K)\implies\alpha=\left(K+\lambda nI\right)^{-1}y+\varepsilon,\varepsilon\in Ker(K)\implies

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}=\left(K+\lambda nI\right)^{-1}y\ \mbox{with}\ K\varepsilon=0.

Estimation of parameters for ELM

Theorem 6.2.22

(Parameter estimation of an embedded simple multivariate linear model)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size
n
i.e.
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\to n
I.I.D. copies of a joint random variable
Z=(\mathbb{X},Y)\to(\mathbb{\varphi(X)},Y)
with unknown linear model
\hat{Y}=E(Y|\mathbb{\varphi(X)})=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}\varphi_{1}(\mathbb{X})+\ldots+\theta_{p}\varphi_{p}(\mathbb{X})\equiv Y=E(Y|\mathbb{\varphi(X)})+\varepsilon=\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}\varphi_{1}(\mathbb{X})+\ldots+\theta_{p}\varphi_{p}(\mathbb{X})+\varepsilon
. Now, running the linear model over n training examples namely in the data-set
\mathcal{S}=\left\{\mathbb{x}_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
as

\displaystyle y_{i}=\theta_{0}+\theta^{\top}\mathbb{\varphi{x}}_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\ \mbox{for}\ i=1,2,\ldots,n\implies\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}_{n\times(p+1)}\mathbb{\theta}+\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\ \mbox{where}\ \mathbb{\varphi(x)}_{j}=(\varphi_{1j}\mathbb{x},\ldots\varphi_{nj}(\mathbb{x}))^{\top},j=1,2,\ldots,p,\mathbb{y},\varepsilon,\mathbb{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1},\mathrm{X}_{n\times(p+1)}=

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{1},\mathbb{\varphi(x)}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{\varphi(x)}_{p}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(p+1)},\theta=(\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{p})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{(p+1)\times 1}.

So,
\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon
is the general form of a linear model, where

a.

\mathbb{y}
is called the response such that
E(\mathbb{y})=\mathrm{X}\theta
.

b.

\mathbb{x}
is called the explanatory variable, the regressor, or the covariates.

c.

\mathrm{X}
is called the design matrix.

d.

A least square estimate of
\omega
:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}=\left(\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathrm{X}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{X}^{\top}\mathbb{y}=(\hat{\theta}_{0},\hat{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\theta}_{p})^{\top}.

Example 6.2.23
.

Consider a synthetic data generated from the function:

\displaystyle f(x)=1+cos(\pi x)+sin(\pi x)+cos(2\pi x)+sin(2*\pi x)+0.5\varepsilon,\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Then, the estimate of the embedded linear model is given by

\displaystyle\hat{y}=1.07+1.05\cos(\pi x)+1.16\sin(\pi x)+1.15\cos(2\pi x)+0.98\sin(2\pi x).

:

\theta_{0}+\theta_{1}X+\theta_{2}X^{2}+\theta_{3}X^{3}+\theta_{4}X^{4}+\varepsilon
.

, and running over

\mathbb{y}=\mathrm{X}\theta+\varepsilon

6.2.4
Multiple multivariate linear model (MMLM)

Motivation and intuition of multiple multivariate linear model:

Definition 6.2.25

(Kronecker product)
.

Consider two
2\times 2
matrices as:

\displaystyle A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&0\\
1&3\end{array}\right),\ B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}5&-1\\
-1&4\end{array}\right)

Then, Kronecker product is defined as:

\displaystyle A\otimes B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2B&0B\\
B&3B\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}10&-2&0&0\\
-2&8&0&0\\
5&-1&15&-3\\
-1&4&-3&12\end{array}\right).

Computation of parameters for MMLM

Theorem 6.2.26

(Simplification of MMLM in column and row pictures)
.

Backdrop: Consider a realization
\mathbb{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
of an I.I.D. random sample of size
n
i.e.
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})\to n
I.I.D. copies of a joint random variable
Z=(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y})
with unknown linear model
\hat{Y}_{i}=E(Y_{i}|\mathbb{X})=\theta_{i0}+\theta_{i1}X_{1}+\ldots+\theta_{id}X_{d},i=1,\ldots,p\equiv Y_{i}=E(Y_{i}|\mathbb{X})+\varepsilon=\theta_{i0}+\theta_{i1}X_{1}+\ldots+\theta_{id}X_{d},i=1,\ldots,p
. Now, running the linear model over n training examples namely in the data-set
\mathcal{S}=\left\{\mathbb{x}_{i},\mathbb{y}_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
as a column picture:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Y}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{X}\mathrm{\Theta}+\mathrm{\varepsilon}\implies vec(\mathrm{Y})=vec(\mathrm{X}\mathrm{\Theta})+vec(\varepsilon)\implies

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
\vdots\\
y_{p}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{X}\theta_{1}\\
\mathrm{X}\theta_{2}\vdots\\
\mathrm{X}\theta_{p}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{p}\end{array}\right)=(I_{p}\otimes\mathrm{X})vec(\Theta)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varepsilon_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{p}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\mathrm{X}&0&\ldots&0&0\\
0&\mathrm{X}&\ldots&0&0\\
\vdots&0&\ldots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\ldots&0&\mathrm{X}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\theta_{1}\\
\theta_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\theta_{p}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{p}\end{array}\right)\implies

\displaystyle y_{j}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{X}\theta_{j}+\varepsilon_{j},j=1,\ldots,p.

As a row picture:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Y}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{X}\mathrm{\Theta}+\mathrm{\varepsilon}\implies vec(\mathrm{Y}^{\top})=vec((\mathrm{X}\mathrm{\Theta})^{\top})+vec(\varepsilon^{\top})\implies

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}Y_{1}^{\top}\\
Y_{2}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
Y_{n}^{\top}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(\mathrm{X}_{1}\Theta)^{\top}\\
(\mathrm{X}_{2}\Theta)^{\top}\vdots\\
(\mathrm{X}_{n}\Theta)^{\top}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varepsilon_{1}^{\top}\\
\varepsilon_{2}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{n}^{\top}\end{array}\right)=(\mathrm{X}\otimes I_{p})vec(\Theta^{\top})+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varepsilon_{1}^{\top}\\
\varepsilon_{2}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{n}^{\top}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}x_{11}I_{p}&x_{12}I_{p}&\ldots&x_{1d}I_{p}\\
x_{21}I_{p}&x_{22}I_{p}&\ldots&x_{2d}I_{p}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ldots&\vdots\\
x_{n1}I_{p}&x_{n2}I_{p}&\ldots&x_{nd}I_{p}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Theta_{1}\\
\Theta_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\Theta_{p}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\varepsilon_{1}^{\top}\\
\varepsilon_{2}^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\varepsilon_{n}^{\top}\end{array}\right)\implies

\displaystyle Y_{i}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{X}_{i}\otimes\Theta)+\varepsilon_{i},1=1,\ldots,n.

Remark 6.2.27
.

Computation and estimation of parameters for the multiple multivariate linear models can be carried with the help of simple multivariate linear models approach using a suitable decomposition technique either in row or column picture form.

6.3
Generalized linear models

First, we consider a model conditional probability of success for
Y\in\{0,1\}
given
X,P(Y=1|X),
as:

\displaystyle logit(P(Y=1|X))=\log\left(\frac{P(Y=1|X)}{1-P(Y=1|X)}\right)=\theta^{\top}X\implies

\displaystyle P(Y=1|X)=\frac{1}{1+\exp(-\theta^{\top}X)}=\frac{\exp(\theta^{\top}X)}{1+\exp(\theta^{\top}X)}=\sigma\left(\theta^{\top}X\right).

So, in general a statistical modelling (i.e. a categorical modelling), we wish to estimate/predict a discrete class label (or a posteriori probability), with a linear argument and a known activation as:

\displaystyle logit\left(\hat{Y}\right)=\ln\left(\frac{\hat{Y}}{1-\hat{Y}}\right)=\theta^{\top}X\implies\hat{Y}=\frac{1}{1+\exp(-\theta^{\top}X)}=\sigma\left(\theta^{\top}X\right).

6.3.1
Logistic regression: Bernoulli logit model

Algorithmic approach of logistic regression:

Step 1:

First transform the space of class probability to the space of logit of the probability using the transformation

\displaystyle logit(p)=\log\left(\frac{p}{1-p}\right)\in(-\infty,\infty),\ p\in[0,1].

Step 2:

Now, we perform linear regression as the linear model

\displaystyle logit(\hat{Y})=logit(p)=\theta^{\top}X

in order to maximize the likelihood of observing the data.

Step 3:

Finally, we remap the space of
logit(p)
to class probability using the inverse of the logit as the logistic map as:

\displaystyle\hat{Y}=\hat{p}=logit^{-1}(\theta^{\top}X)=\frac{\exp(\theta^{\top}X)}{1+\exp(\theta^{\top}X)}=\sigma(\theta^{\top}X).

Step 4:

Further, as
p\in[0,1]
, we have a decision boundary at:

\displaystyle p=0.5\implies\theta^{\top}X=logit(0.5)=\log\left(\frac{0.5}{1-0.5}\right)=\log(1)=0.

Example 6.3.1
.

Figure 6.5
:
A visualization of logistic regression via linear model of logit(p) followed by inversion map of the logit function.

Now, running over the given dataset
\mathcal{S}=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we have

Theorem 6.3.2
.

Consider
\mathcal{X}
be a set of inputs.
Y\in\{-1,1\}
binary outputs,
\mathcal{S}=(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\left(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\right)^{n}
a training set of
n
pairs. Estimate a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
to predict y by
sign(f(x))
.

Proof 6.3.3
.

Consider a
0/1
loss measures if a prediction is correct or not as:

\displaystyle\ell_{0/1}(y,f(x))=I\left(yf(x)<0\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\ \mbox{if}\ y=sign(f(x))\\
1&\ \mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

And, the estimate of
f
, we get by solving

\displaystyle\hat{f}=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{0/1}(y_{i},f(x_{i}))+\lambda\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right).

The minimization problem of minimizing misclassification error with regularization is having various limitation as follows:

a.

the problem is non-smooth, and typically NP-hard to solve,

b.

the regularization has no effect since the
0/1
loss is invariant by scaling of
f
. In fact, no function achieves the minimum when
\lambda>0
.

So, an alternative is to define a probabilistic model of
y\in\{-1,1\}
parametrized by
f(x)
, as follows:

\displaystyle y\omega^{\top}x=yf(x)=logit\left(E(Y=y|X=x)\right)=logit\left(P(Y=y|X=x)\right)\implies

\displaystyle p(y|x)=logit^{-1}(yf(x))=\sigma(yf(x))=\frac{1}{1+\exp(-yf(x))}\implies

\displaystyle\ell_{logistic}(y,f(x))=-ln\left(p(y|x)\right)=ln(1+\exp(-yf(x))).

A few properties of sigmoid-logistic function and logistic loss can be characterized as:

\displaystyle\sigma(u)=\frac{1}{1+exp(-u)},\sigma(-u)=1-\sigma(u),\sigma^{\prime}(u)=\sigma(u)\sigma(-u),

\displaystyle\ell_{logistic}(u)=\ln(1+\exp(-u)),\ell^{\prime}_{logistic}(u)=-\sigma(-u),\ell^{\prime\prime}_{logistic}(u)=\sigma^{\prime}(u).

Figure 6.6
:
Plots of sigmoid-logistic function and logistic loss.

Now, the modified estimation problem can be posed as:

\displaystyle\hat{f}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{logistic}(y_{i},f(x_{i}))+\lambda\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln\left(1+\exp(-y_{i}f(x_{i}))\right)+\lambda\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right).

By the representation theorem, the solution of the unconstrained problem can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\hat{f}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},x)\implies\left(\hat{f}(x_{1}),\ldots,\hat{f}(x_{n})\right)^{\top}=K\alpha,\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\alpha^{\top}K\alpha.

To estimate
\alpha
, we need to solve the following:

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln\left(1+\exp(-y_{i}(K\alpha)_{i})\right)+\lambda\alpha^{\top}K\alpha\right)

\displaystyle\approx\arg\min_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{1}{n}(K\alpha-z)^{\top}W(K\alpha-z)+\lambda\alpha^{\top}K\alpha\right).

The above convex quadratic approximation (with respect to an affine transformation
z=K\alpha_{0}-W^{-1}Py
) is as follows:

\displaystyle\nabla J(\alpha)=\frac{1}{n}KP(\alpha)y+\lambda K\alpha,P(\alpha)=diag(P_{1}(\alpha),\ldots,P_{n}(\alpha)),

\displaystyle P_{i}(\alpha)=\ell^{\prime}_{logistic}(y_{f}(x_{)})=-\sigma(-y_{i}f(x_{i}))

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}J(\alpha)=\frac{1}{n}KW(\alpha)K+\lambda K,W(\alpha)=diag(W_{1}(\alpha),\ldots,W_{n}(\alpha)),

\displaystyle W_{i}(\alpha)=\ell"_{logistic}(y_{i}f(x_{i}))=\sigma(y_{i}f(x_{i}))\sigma(-y_{i}f(x_{i})).

So, the estimate of
\alpha
is computed as:

\displaystyle\nabla J_{quadratic}(\alpha)=\frac{2}{n}KW(K\alpha-x)+2\lambda K\alpha=\frac{2}{n}K\left((WK+\lambda nI)-Wz\right)=0

\displaystyle\implies\alpha-\left(WK+\lambda nI\right)^{-1}Wz\in Ker(K)\implies\alpha=\left(WK+\lambda nI\right)^{-1}Wz+\varepsilon,

\displaystyle\varepsilon\in Ker(K)\implies\hat{\alpha}=\left(WK+\lambda nI\right)^{-1}Wz\ \mbox{with}\ K\varepsilon=0.

6.3.2
SoftMax regression: a multinomial logit model

6.4
Future note: control, discovery models, and generation

In order to capture discrete pattern in the response variable, we need to introduce some kind of non-linear function on it (e.g. logit, multinomial logit etc.), that leads to generalized linear models (i.e. a model having known
\&
non-linear activation function with linear argument e.g. logistic regression, multinomial logistic regression etc.). Furthermore, discovery of patterns within input space with a few or no labels (i.e. prediction of labels or response variable via representation learning) lead to discovery models. At the end, the linear models, the generalized linear models and the discovery models can be unified by universal approximation of neural networks.

A machine learning system

Step 1:

Methods:-mathematical modelling with scientific meaning, estimation/optimization, optimization algorithm/ numerical methods, generalization/concentration inequalities, inference/prediction, and numerical methods etc.

Step 2:

Techniques:-machine learning algorithms for decision making, control, discovery and generation (e.g. regression, classification, neural networks etc.).

Step 3:

Tools: Implementation in computing devices using some platform e.g. Scikit Learn (for machine learning algorithms other than neural networks), TenFlow, PyTorch (neural networks), Keras (deep learning).

Step 4:

Deployment in the real world system e.g. a self deriving car etc.
